With evidence verification and abstention, selective F1 increased to 0.84 at 91\% coverage and evidence support reached 96\%. These results show that structured behavioral representation and evidence-verified selective prediction can improve the reliability of behavioral forecasting in SLMs.
  </details>

- **[Not All Information Is Worth Asking For: Decision-Informed Information Seeking with LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32181)**  `arXiv:2610.32181`  
  _Seunghan Lee, Jun Seo, Junhyeok Kang, Jaehoon Lee, Sangjun Han, Sungdong Yoo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly used to support decisions under incomplete information, where deciding what to look up and when to stop matters as much as the final answer. However, LLMs fall into an information trap: They pay for checks, i.e., costly pieces of evidence, that cannot change their decision or are not worth their cost, and they keep asking when the evidence is already sufficient. To this end, we propose Decision-Informed Value-of-information Acquisition (DIVA), a simple yet effective procedure for LLM information seeking based on the decision value of evidence. DIVA 1) lets the LLM specify a small decision model, i.e., how likely a bad outcome is and how each check would shift this probability, through short numeric questions and 2) values each candidate check by how much its imagined outcomes improve the decision net of its cost, stopping when no check is worth its cost. Its variant, DIVA-U, further accounts for the LLM's uncertain estimates of how strongly each check shifts this probability. DIVA is training-free and applies to any LLM. Across four domains and 20 LLMs, DIVA-U has lower regret, i.e., value lost relative to the best possible decision, than every LLM baseline on real-world data, mainly by stopping once evidence suffices, and DIVA cuts the regret of the best LLM baseline by 75% in synthetic scenarios where every check's reliability is given. Code:this http URL.
  </details>

- **[$Ψ$-0.6: Adaptive Streams of Thought in a Unified Vision-Language World Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32177)**  `arXiv:2610.32177`  
  _Khai Loong Aw, Klemen Kotar, Baihan Zhang, Yifan Liu, Lilian Chen, Atlas Kazemian, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans reason about the physical world by flexibly combining different forms of inference as a problem unfolds. Achieving this flexibility is a major open challenge in AI: capabilities often remain separated across specialized models, limiting how they can be composed and jointly adapted to a task. We introduce $\Psi$-0.6, a unified vision-language world model that adaptively composes perception, language, geometry, and simulation within a shared reasoning process we call streams of thought. These streams interleave images, text, and explicit representations of geometry and motion, all of which the model can generate and interpret through a shared token interface. $\Psi$-0.6 surpasses leading open-weight models zero-shot on multi-view spatial reasoning, with further gains from reasoning through geometric intermediates. It also supports counterfactual simulation: generating and interpreting imagined scenes to determine, for example, whether moving an object would obstruct a pathway. Reinforcement learning from task feedback jointly optimizes the selection, generation, and use of intermediate representations, improving visual question-answering accuracy with gains that transfer to unseen benchmarks. $\Psi$-0.6 offers a path toward adaptive physical reasoning -- not only predicting the physical world but also choosing the representations through which to reason.
  </details>

- **[MPQ: A Message-Passing View of Post-Training Quantization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32127)**  `arXiv:2610.32127`  
  _Yanshuo Chen, Chenxi Liu, Reza Shirkavand, Heng Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Post-training quantization (PTQ) is a key technique for efficiently deploying large language models, as it compresses weights into low-bit representations without retraining. Existing PTQ solvers, including GPTQ, LDLQ, and block coordinate descent, commit each weight block to a single codeword before its neighbors are considered, leaving near-optimal alternatives unexplored. In this work, we cast row-wise PTQ as posterior inference over discrete codebooks, in which existing solvers correspond to a zero-temperature, hard-decision limit retaining only the top codeword per block. We propose Message-Passing Quantization (MPQ), which preserves soft posterior information across blocks during inference and commits to hard codewords only at the output stage. MPQ first uses vector approximate message passing to compute approximate blockwise posteriors, then refines a hard-decision initialization by exact block coordinate descent. Across 2-bit lattice and 3- and 4-bit scalar quantization on dense language models from Llama, Qwen, and Mistral families, MPQ improves perplexity and zero-shot accuracy over strong hard-decision baselines, and composes favorably with preprocessing techniques such as activation-aware scaling and incoherence processing. Our implementation is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PersonaScienceBench: Evaluating Scientific Agents with Diverse Simulated Researchers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32106)**  `arXiv:2610.32106`  
  _Siyang Wu, Weizhi Zhang, Chen Yang, Esther Xu Fei, Jorin Overwiening, Xu Pan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific agents collaborate with researchers whose expertise and preferences differ, but benchmarks usually hold the collaborator fixed. We introduce PersonaScienceBench: 200 tasks from five benchmarks across four scientific domains. Simulated researchers provide context and feedback according to persona profiles, while task objectives and grading remain fixed. Agents encounter researcher preferences only through conversation. Across eighteen modelconfigurations, task performance, fact recovery, and self-repetition reveal different strengths. Detailed GPT 6 Astra analyses show distinct agent responses across researcher profiles; interaction traces illustrate targeted clar ification and workflow revision. These behaviors can differ even when final scores match. PersonaScienceBench therefore evaluates how agents collaborate with diverse researchers alongside whether they complete the task.
  </details>

- **[Learning Nonlinear Waveform Fusion Across Scales for Integrated Sensing and Communication](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32099)**  `arXiv:2610.32099`  
  _Jiayu Liu, Kai Wan, Shuo Shao, Giuseppe Caire_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fixed source superposition and frequency partitioning constrain how communication content interacts with a probing reference in integrated sensing and communication (ISAC). We learn nonlinear waveform fusion across scales for image transmission and localization of multiple targets in range and angle. Reception uses the information available at each end. Adaptive temporal feature weighting and spectral updates combine image features and a deterministic linearly frequency-modulated reference in a shared complex state, generating a waveform with fixed total energy over the array frame. At the user equipment (UE), the reference and channel state information condition a neural demixer for image reconstruction. At the base station, the realized transmit pulse defines a coherent dictionary for sequential localization with learned local correction, physical refinement, and residual updates. We compare the proposed waveforms with superposition in the time domain and learned spectral linear fusion on 512 independent test images, using a shared fixed UE receiver and a baseline sensing receiver using coherent peak search. Averaged across eleven task weights, the proposed waveforms reduce normalized localization error by 29.0\% and 27.0\%, respectively, while increasing mean peak signal-to-noise ratio by 1.40 and 0.82~dB. With the proposed waveforms fixed, learned local correction reduces mean normalized localization error by 12.2\% relative to the same sequential receiver without learned correction.
  </details>

- **[DELA: Learning Controllable Edits for Diffusion Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32096)**  `arXiv:2610.32096`  
  _Xinsong Feng, Chenan Wang, Leshu Tang, Johnny Liu, Dong Li, Da Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion language models (DLMs) support bidirectional denoising and iterative refinement, but generation remains largely controlled by token-level masking schedules. This leaves draft-level refinement implicit and limits explicit control over when to insert missing content, delete redundant spans, revisit uncertain regions, or stop editing. We introduce Diffusion Editing Language Adapter (DELA), a plug-in framework that enables controllable draft refinement with a frozen DLM. DELA operates on clean, variable-length drafts using four explicit editing actions: Insert, Delete, Remask, and Stop. The controller determines when and where to edit the draft, while the frozen DLM fills masked regions through denoising, separating high-level edit control from token-level generation. A naive single-phase editor would be computationally expensive because arbitrary interior edits can disrupt prefix stability and limit reliable KV-cache reuse. To address this, DELA uses a two-phase decoding procedure: a fast, append-only drafting phase that supports KV-cache reuse, followed by a global refinement phase that enables targeted non-monotonic edits to the full draft. We train the controller using a staged curriculum that begins with learning local edit priors from synthetic recovery tasks and mined actions that improve draft quality. Subsequent stages refine edit selection through rollout-based outcome preferences and alignment with downstream task feedback. Experiments on mathematical reasoning and code generation show that DELA achieves gains of up to 5.5 percentage points over direct diffusion decoding while keeping the backbone frozen. DELA thus provides a modular approach to explicit draft-level control in diffusion language models.
  </details>

- **[Behaviour analysis for large language model-driven agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32087)**  `arXiv:2610.32087`  
  _Tianqiang Yan, Lizhen Qu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Understanding language-agent behaviour requires learning how actions are organised across a trajectory and how this organisation relates to context and outcomes. We propose a general framework that connects data-driven behavioural discovery with analysis and experimental investigation. Categories developed from recorded activity define what is measured. Analyses preserve the behaviours' positions in the process and express their relationships as interpretable hypotheses. Statistical assessment and tests in held-out contexts examine the strength and scope of the evidence. Experiments, including prompt steering, then test how behaviour and outcomes change under deliberately varied conditions. A study of iterative self-reflection illustrates how discovery, dependency learning and prompt comparisons can inform one another. We develop this approach for trajectories of varying length, event order and agent roles, and identify advances in coding, analysis and experimentation needed to support its wider use.
  </details>

- **[DriveMind: Mind-Evolving Belief Tracking for Closed-Loop Autonomous Driving](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32069)**  `arXiv:2610.32069`  
  _Yan Shi, Kehua Chen, Jiarui Qi, Yihong Tang, Haichen Liao, Yinhai Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent multimodal large language models (MLLMs) can perceive a traffic scene, reason about it, and propose a driving action within a single model. However, existing MLLM-based drivers reason from scratch at each frame and commit to a single explanation, discarding earlier hypotheses about the scene. When the scene is ambiguous, the agent locks in on the wrong interpretation and acts on it before evidence accumulates. In this paper, we propose DriveMind, an MLLM-based driving framework that keeps several hypotheses about the scene alive at the same time and updates their weights as new frames arrive. Inspired by particle filtering, DriveMind propagates each hypothesis through a VLM, scores it against the current observation, and resamples to maintain diversity. On Bench2Drive, adding belief tracking to the same driving pipeline improves the Driving Score (DS) from 68.50 to 81.37 and the Success Rate (SR) from 41.36% to 54.09%. The largest gains occur on ambiguous scenarios such as unsignalized intersections (+20.0 DS) and oncoming-traffic interactions (+20.5 DS). Beyond the overall metrics, DriveMind also improves fine-grained interactive driving abilities such as merging, emergency braking, and give-way.
  </details>

- **[Understanding Communication Failures in Multi-Agent LLM Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32060)**  `arXiv:2610.32060`  
  _Priya Pitre, Jun Hou, Yi Fang, Naren Ramakrishnan, Xuan Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-agent LLM systems aim to improve reasoning through debate, evidence sharing, and mutual correction, but interaction can also introduce failures that final accuracy alone cannot explain. Agents may converge too early, ignore dissent, suppress correct minority answers, or preserve a wrong framing across rounds. We present a corpus-grounded taxonomy of communication failures, built from 3,211 failed multi-round trajectories across medical QA, coding, finance, multi-hop reasoning, ethics, and embodied text tasks. Using this taxonomy, we analyze failure distributions across datasets and system designs, round-level error dynamics, recovery hardness, majority transitions, minority-signal suppression, and self-reported confidence. We find that failures are dominated by recovery/correction and consensus/decision problems rather than isolated first-round mistakes, with many emerging after agents first observe peer responses. Based on these findings, we recommend stronger disagreement handling, evidence verification, minority-signal preservation, and recovery checks before finalization.
  </details>

- **[Can Test-Time Memory Evolution Outperform Test-Time Training for Scientific Discovery?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32046)**  `arXiv:2610.32046`  
  _Yepeng Liu, Shengmai Chen, Xuandong Zhao, Chengzhi Liu, Dawn Song, Xin Eric Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent \emph{test-time training} (TTT) frameworks have enabled open-weight large language models (LLMs) to outperform inference-only frameworks powered by frontier models on scientific discovery tasks. However, TTT introduces additional computational cost for online weight updates during the discovery process, making computational efficiency an important consideration when scaling to larger models. This motivates us to explore an efficient \emph{test-time adaptation} method for scientific discovery. We introduce \emph{Test-Time Memory Evolution} (TTME), a plug-and-play test-time adaptation method, as an alternative to TTT. TTME continually updates the external memory by distilling the experience to guide subsequent discovery without updating model weights. To have a clean comparison, we evaluate TTME and TTT under the same discovery framework, replacing only the test-time adaptation method. Our evaluation shows that TTME outperforms TTT on all five mathematical tasks, while reducing computational cost by $82.8$-$84.6\%$ in FLOPs and $47.7$-$58.4\%$ in runtime. These results establish TTME as an effective and computationally efficient alternative to TTT in the evaluated discovery settings.
  </details>

- **[Do AI-for-Science Agents Preserve Model Safety?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32045)**  `arXiv:2610.32045`  
  _Michael Yew Chuan Lee, Manya Sriram, N. Benjamin Erichson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI-for-Science agents are increasingly being used for formulating scientific hypotheses, running autonomous experiments, and interpreting evidence. These agents are constructed by integrating a large language model (LLM) into a domain-specific agent harness, which in turn can improve the LLM's scientific capability and enable the model to orchestrate scientific tools. In this work, we ask whether model safety is preserved when an LLM is integrated in an AI-for-Science agent harness. We evaluate five agent harnesses on 50 harmful chemistry requests. Specifically, we study how the safety behavior changes under direct prompting, two simple adversarial prompting strategies, and an adaptive attack. Across four recent GPT variants, our results show that the agent harness can increase harmful compliance even under direct prompting. More critically, we find that adaptive attacks that are largely unsuccessful against the base LLM suddenly become highly successful after the agent harness is introduced. Our results show that the safety behavior of the base LLM is not durable once a harness is introduced. Thus, as AI-for-Science agents become more capable and autonomous, the community needs to evaluate and align the agent using domain-specific safety benchmarks.
  </details>

- **[PPO vs. SAC: A Comparative Study for Cooperative Motion Control of Dual-Arm Space Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32034)**  `arXiv:2610.32034`  
  _Nasim Fallahiarezoodar, Zheng H. Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The growing population of orbital debris has intensified interest in active debris removal and on-orbit servicing, for which dual-arm space manipulators offer improved grasping stability and load distribution relative to single-arm systems. However, coordinating two manipulators under inter-arm collision avoidance, orientation alignment, and sensing uncertainty remains challenging. This paper develops a coordinated reinforcement learning framework for cooperative motion planning of a dual-arm space manipulator in a target-reaching task with inter-arm collision-avoidance constraints. A centralized state representation combines joint-space, end-effector, and target-relative information, while a multi-objective reward function promotes target-reaching accuracy, orientation alignment, motion smoothness, synchronized task completion, and collision avoidance. Within this framework, Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and Soft Actor-Critic (SAC) are compared as representative on-policy and off-policy algorithms, and the learned policies are further evaluated for generalization to previously unseen target configurations. Across five random seeds, SAC substantially outperforms PPO within the considered fixed-base simulation scenarios: it reaches an 80% success threshold in every seed (median 1.6M environment steps) whereas PPO does not reach it within a 4M-step budget, and it attains a higher mean success rate (78.6% versus 7.4%) with lower position and orientation errors and tighter bimanual synchronization. Both algorithms degrade on unseen boundary targets, but SAC retains most of its performance while PPO's collapses. The results also expose a failure mode of PPO: under the two-phase shaped reward, it tends to converge to a reward-loitering local optimum rather than committing to the precision task. This behavior highlights the sensitivity of on-policy learning to reward shaping.
  </details>

- **[RSI-Opt: Recursive Self-Improvement Optimizer Agents For Model Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32026)**  `arXiv:2610.32026`  
  _Binghang Lu, Yige Sun, Guang Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conventional neural-network training typically follows a predefined optimization recipe, motivating a LLM agent that monitors training dynamics and adjusts optimizer settings as training progresses, but a fixed agent policy does not accumulate experience across runs. We introduce RSI-Opt, a plug-and-play Recursive Self-Improvement Optimizer Agents which evolves the knowledge that guides this process: a prompt, a playbook of procedures, a library of tested optimizer code, and a decision cadence. A meta-agent rewrites this policy using evidence from earlier runs, and an archive retains evaluated policies for further evolution. Across 5 generations on three training tasks, the meta-agents develop reusable procedures and code and conduct their own CPU proxy experiments. RSI-Opt substantially improves optimizer-agent performance, with representative raw-space summary $z$-scores increasing from $+10.48$ to $+14.33$ on the evolution suite with Opus 5.5 and from $-0.36$ to $+3.11$ on held-out tasks with GPT-5.5 through cross-model policy transfer. On the Burgers PDE, it reduces mean relative $L_2$ error by approximately 2.7-fold with Opus 5.5 and 8.8-fold with GPT-6.1 over the fixed-prompt agent baseline, although PDE transfer varies across LLMs and tasks. Controlled comparisons show that the evolved policy combines reusable optimization knowledge with adaptation to each task: initial decisions capture most observed gains on seen tasks, while continued adaptation improves outcomes on unseen tasks. These findings demonstrate how procedures and executable tools accumulated across training runs can improve optimizer-agent performance and support transfer to scientific machine learning.
  </details>

- **[BARC-Bench: Benign Asks, Risky Consequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32024)**  `arXiv:2610.32024`  
  _Kangkang Li, Kelvin Luu, Lily Ma, Tu Trinh, Ying Liu, Mohamed Elfeki_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task instructions from users for agents are inherently underspecified, especially regarding safety boundaries--creating behavioral risks. To study these risks, we introduce BARC-Bench, a collection of 110 tasks across nine simulated workspace environments where benign requests leave openings for unsafe actions. In this benchmark, we evaluate agent behavior against safety boundaries established by workspace records, implicit environmental context, and unstated social norms. For example, an agent asked to schedule off-boarding discussions must arrange the meeting without prematurely revealing an employee's resignation through a shared calendar invitation. Evaluating four frontier model configurations across five attempts per task reveals that models are not consistently safe: the share of tasks completed safely on all five attempts ranges from 9.1% to 72.7%, with even the strongest configuration producing a safety violation on 27.3% of tasks. These results highlight an asymmetric operational risk: while models frequently complete complex workflows, their inability to consistently respect unstated boundaries presents a substantial barrier to safe autonomous delegation.
  </details>

- **[CEMIR vs. FISER: a comparative study of reasoning-based and enrichment-based approaches to pragmatic intent recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32022)**  `arXiv:2610.32022`  
  _Ziya Hosseynzadeh, Farjad Fallah, Farnoosh Fallah, Soroush Gooran, Mohammad Amanloo, Hossein Sameti_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intent recognition in human-robot interaction requires agents to resolve ambiguous natural language instructions by inferring the speaker's underlying goals. Research has increasingly focused on two notable paradigms for addressing this task: structured symbolic reasoning and multimodal input enrichment. However, it remains unclear whether enrichment-based methods provide benefit in settings where the ambiguity concerns unstated human intent rather than missing observable scene information. In this paper, we present a comparative and diagnostic study benchmarking FISER - a framework utilizing structured social and embodied reasoning - against CEMIR (Context-Enriched Multimodal Intent Recognition), a method that performs multimodal enrichment by synthesizing narratives and photorealistic images from textual prompts. By evaluating both approaches across four Large Language Models (LLMs), ranging from Qwen2.5-Omni-3B to GPT-5, on the HandMeThat v2 benchmark, we analyze their capability to resolve pragmatic ambiguities. Our statistical analysis, employing multiple-comparison correction, reveals no statistically significant difference in performance between FISER and CEMIR across any model or difficulty level. This pattern holds across all sixteen model-by-difficulty comparisons, as well as in a targeted ablation isolating CEMIR's narrative and visual components. Instead, performance is primarily associated with backbone capability and the inherent difficulty of the ambiguity. These findings suggest a boundary condition for text-derived multimodal enrichment: when the relevant task constraints are already available in the textual instruction, synthetic multimodal augmentation may not provide measurable benefit over structured reasoning on the same text.
  </details>

- **[The Helpful Agent Problem: When Emergent Cooperation Among LLM Agents Bypasses Intended Workflows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31985)**  `arXiv:2610.31985`  
  _Moumita Choudhury, Amit Sarker, Shlomo Zilberstein_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are increasingly deployed in collaborative settings where they pursue separate tasks while sharing resources and operating under review or oversight processes. Such settings create safety risks not only when agents are malicious, but also when they are simply trying to help. Prior work has largely studied adversarial or explicitly collusive behavior; we show that benign agents pursuing separate objectives can spontaneously cooperate in ways that bypass intended workflows. We call this the \emph{helpful agent problem}. We study long-horizon shared-resource environments in which agents sometimes depend on resources owned by their peers and cross-owner changes are normally mediated by a reviewer. When review becomes unavailable, agents begin making changes on one another's behalf despite having no shared reward or explicit incentive to cooperate. Crucially, these actions remain within each agent's local permissions while circumventing the system's intended oversight structure. We introduce \textsc{FavorBench}, a controlled benchmark with measurable downstream side effects, and adapt \textsc{CooperBench} to test the phenomenon in realistic repositories. Across two benchmarks and four models, we find that out-of-workflow favors emerge frequently. Further, in \textsc{FavorBench}, favors from at least three models cause significant harm to parties outside the interaction. These results reveal a distinct multi-agent safety challenge: cooperation itself can create pathways around system-level safeguards.
  </details>

- **[Reproducible Evaluation as the Substrate for Safe Prompt Compression in Production Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31972)**  `arXiv:2610.31972`  
  _Prabal Vashisht, Zheng Hu, Ying Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Evaluating conversational agents that act on live, mutable enterprise data is hard: replayed sessions drift as backend state changes, and a benchmark built today can silently go stale as the data it describes moves on. We present an offline evaluation harness for a production conversational agent that stays reproducible despite the live data it operates on changing, and that can be regenerated on demand once it goes stale. We then use this harness as the fitness function for GEPA-based prompt optimization under a correctness gate that guards against reward hacking. Applied to a production merchant-facing agent, correctness-gated compression shrinks the agent's full prompt surface by 3.5-7.6% without regressing held-out task correctness, consistently across three independent subject-model families, and for one, correctness measurably improves.
  </details>

- **[Knowing Whom to Trust is not Enough: The Trust--Agreement Gap in Repeated Multi-Party LLM Negotiation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31971)**  `arXiv:2610.31971`  
  _Amit Sarker, Moumita Choudhury, Saaduddin Mahmud, Shlomo Zilberstein_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly participate in negotiations on behalf of individuals and organizations, yet they remain vulnerable to manipulation, particularly in repeated multi-party interactions. Classical multi-agent systems address this challenge through explicit trust and reputation models, whereas existing work on LLM negotiation largely treats trust as an emergent phenomenon. We investigate whether LLM agents can form useful trust estimates from prior interactions and leverage them to improve future negotiation outcomes. We introduce a repeated, multi-party, multi-issue negotiation framework in which recurring agents participate in a sequence of negotiation games while one participant covertly attempts to prevent agreement. After each game, agents assess their counterparts and maintain pairwise trust estimates that carry over to subsequent negotiations. We compare trust-aware and trust-agnostic agents and analyze how explicit trust influences negotiation outcomes. Our results reveal a \emph{trust--agreement gap}: trust-aware agents can identify the adversary within a few games, but accurate trust estimates do not consistently translate into better agreements. Trust increases agreement rates by up to 23 percentage points, but only when agents are explicitly guided to use it in their decisions. Explicit trust can therefore improve repeated LLM negotiations, but its value depends critically on how trust is assessed and incorporated into agents' reasoning.
  </details>

- **[$M^3$: A Multi-LLM System for Multi-Turn Jailbreaking as a Markov Decision Process](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31937)**  `arXiv:2610.31937`  
  _Yuqing Huang, Jack Ji, Jithin Pradeep, Sagarika Suresh, Jekaterina Novikova_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern Large Language Models (LLMs) remain vulnerable to carefully orchestrated multi-turn interactions that progressively bypass safety mechanisms. However, many existing red-teaming approaches rely on heuristics or impose strong assumptions that limit their applicability in fully black box settings. We address this gap by introducing $M^3$, an automated red-teaming framework that formalizes multi-turn jailbreaking as a finite-horizon Markov Decision Process (MDP). In $M^3$, an attacker LLM interacts with a target LLM while operating over a compact internal state produced by a state LLM. Across two benchmarks and six target models, $M^3$ consistently outperforms state-of-the-art multi-turn red-teaming methods in Attack Success Rates (ASR), achieving improvements of up to 53% in ASR@5. Even with lightweight attacker, $M^3$ attains gains up to 26% in ASR@1 and 34% in ASR@5, compared to baselines that use higher capacity attackers. Beyond improved effectiveness, $M^3$ exhibits steeper risk escalation over conversational turns and achieves more consistent performance across risk categories (up to 4.82 improvement in Gini index) without relying on predefined risk taxonomy, providing a scalable and principled framework for multi-turn red-teaming and safety evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Beyond On-Policy vs. Off-Policy: At What Level Should LLM Agent Distillation Follow the Student?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31918)**  `arXiv:2610.31918`  
  _Ya Gao, Zhengtao Zou, Pekka Marttinen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Distilling interactive LLM agents requires choosing between teacher-generated demonstrations and student-generated experience, corresponding broadly to off-policy and on-policy distillation. While both have proven effective, we argue that this distinction is inherently hierarchical: the policy that induces an interaction history need not be the same as the policy that generates the token prefix of the current action. We introduce a hierarchical agent distillation framework that formalizes these two levels of on-policiness and enables explicit control over their pairing, mixture proportions, and training schedule. We study this problem through self-distillation, where a frozen teacher has access to task guidance unavailable to the student, and conduct a three-stage controlled study across three models and four interactive environments. We find that student-induced histories paired with teacher-generated action prefixes generally provide the strongest standalone training signal, whereas following the student at both levels generally performs worst. Nevertheless, fully on-policy data becomes beneficial when incorporated into a mixture with other configurations. Moreover, mixtures with identical teacher/student proportions at each level can yield substantially different performance, showing that how the two levels are paired matters beyond the overall amount of on-policy data. Finally, front-loading fully teacher-generated contexts and delaying fully on-policy contexts improves over a static mixture. These findings provide practical principles for deciding where, how much, and when agent distillation should follow the student.
  </details>

- **[TACS-RF: Tool-driven Agentic Circuit Synthesis of RF and mmWave Modules](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31882)**  `arXiv:2610.31882`  
  _Tristan Liang, Aravind Nagulu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the framework of TACS-RF - a Tool-driven Agentic Circuit Synthesis of RF and mmWave modules. In this framework, a large-language-model (LLM) coding agent (Claude Opus 4.8) directly drives industry-standard electronic-design-automation (EDA) tools such as Cadence Virtuoso/Spectre, Calibre, Cadence EMX, and Ansys HFSS to design RF and mmWave modules. We validate the framework through the agentic design of a 240-251 GHz sub-THz transmitter in a 90 nm SiGe BiCMOS process. The agent semi-autonomously designed the sub-blocks including a 60 GHz push-push Colpitts VCO with second-harmonic extraction, multistage differential cascode power drivers, a push-push frequency doubler, a differential bowtie antenna, and all the interstage matching networks. The transmitter chain with parasitic-extracted devices and EM-verified passives delivers +5.1 to +6.3 dBm across an 11 GHz varactor-only tuning range, phase noise of -80 to -88 dBc/Hz at 1 MHz offset while consuming a DC power of 361 mW. We describe the agent-driven design flow and the resulting division of labor between human and agent, taking a step toward a "text-to-ASIC" future for designing RF/mmWave hardware.
  </details>

- **[Don't Forget What You Just Learned: Self-Directed Repair Turns Agent Mistakes Into Lasting Lessons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31846)**  `arXiv:2610.31846`  
  _Yuval Kansal, Karen Ullrich, Polina Kirichenko, Ilia Kulikov, Niraj K. Jha, Mohammad Pezeshki, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Intelligent agents should learn from their mistakes so that experience leads to continuous improvement. Yet today's agents recover from a mistake only temporarily: in-context reflections are easily forgotten across sessions and compete for limited context, while full-parameter fine-tuning is expensive and can interfere with existing capabilities. We introduce Self-Direction, a mechanism for agents to learn from mistakes that combines the benefits of temporary in-context retries and policy updates. When an agent fails a task, it reflects on its own trajectory in order to extract a lesson and immediately distills it into a lightweight low-rank adapter. By keeping the original weights frozen, Self-Direction prevents adapter training from overwriting base-model parameters while training additional parameters equivalent to ~0.02% of the base model's parameter count for each adapter. The agent then saves these adapters in a persistent library of lessons, automatically routing them to solve related problems later. Experiments with agents based on open-weight models from the Qwen family across open-ended research (GAIA), tool-use workflows (Claw-Eval), and terminal tasks (Terminal-Bench 2.1) show that our method improves overall success rates by 8.9 to 17.0 percentage points and performs competitively with frontier systems. We find that Self-Direction reduces repeated mistakes across future encounters of a task, nearly doubling the reliability of an agent's corrections. Learned lessons also transfer: a handful of adapters generalize to dozens of new tasks without additional training. Ultimately, this self-directed repair transforms everyday mistakes into lasting lessons.
  </details>

- **[Patronizing and Condescending Language Detection in English and Mandarin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31808)**  `arXiv:2610.31808`  
  _Xinyi Vicky Xiang, Catherine Qingyun Wang, Paula Cortés Lemos_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Patronizing and condescending language (PCL) is a form of harmful language that belittles and assumes pity for vulnerable groups, reinforcing negative societal stereotypes. However, unlike overt hate speech, it is often difficult to detect, even for human judges. This paper proposes a system for the automated detection of PCL. Our first approach uses static word embeddings and a convolutional neural network for classification, while our second approach uses pre-trained BERT embeddings fine-tuned for the PCL task. Our second approach was considerably more successful, and achieved an F1 score of 0.528 over the development set.
  </details>

- **[Phronix: The Capability-Centric Learning Paradigm for Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31789)**  `arXiv:2610.31789`  
  _Hariharan Devarajan, Neeraj Rajesh, Aniruddha Marathe, Nikoli Dryden, Kin Ng, Michela Taufer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scientific computing increasingly relies on large language model-based agents for performance engineering tasks such as instrumentation, analysis, and workflow porting. However, agent effectiveness depends heavily on the surrounding harness, which typically relies on manually written skills and curated memory. This limits autonomous learning, forcing agents to repeatedly rediscover solutions rather than retain and reuse knowledge from prior executions. We introduce Phronix, a capability-centric learning paradigm that enables agentic harnesses to acquire, validate, and refine reusable capabilities from their own executions. Phronix operates through a predictor-validator-corrector loop: MCP tools and associated skills propose solutions, tool executions validate them, and observed outcomes refine the stored capabilities. It distills every observed interaction into a capability store and researches a failing task rather than abandoning it. First, such learned capabilities solve a recurring task on the first attempt in 93-95% of cases across every model, significantly reducing solution non-determinism and improving reliability. Second, Phronix turns an agent's own execution into 7.6-30.8 durable capabilities per task without user instruction, and creation falls below two once the needed capability exists. Third, warm-start runs consume only 0.38-0.40 million tokens per task, which is three to four times less than a single baseline attempt, while a cold-start costs no more than the baseline. On the 1000 Genome Pegasus workflow, instrumenting a production application for I/O performance analysis, Phronix passes all 11 correctness checks against six for the baseline harness, and also lifts the task solving rates of two cheaper models from 29% and 27% of SWE-bench tasks solved to 91% and 87%, respectively, matching the performance of the most expensive evaluated model.
  </details>

- **[Tradeoff-Aware Territory Design: A Multi-Objective Decision System for Enterprise Customer-to-Seller Allocation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31701)**  `arXiv:2610.31701`  
  _Nan Wang, Mitchell Turiel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Enterprise customer journeys are shaped not only by product experiences and marketing, but also by recurring resource-allocation decisions. In B2B go-to-market (GTM) organizations, territory carving determines which accounts receive seller coverage, which seller-account relationships are preserved, and how limited field capacity is distributed. Yet the process often remains manual, making it difficult to compare opportunities, value continuity, and evaluate tradeoffs among growth, balance, capacity, and feasibility.We present a production decision system for tradeoff-aware customer-to-seller allocation in enterprise GTM planning. It integrates three components: a multidimensional account-value layer that combines predictive signals to score opportunity; an observational continuity-valuation layer that estimates the cost of breaking seller-account relationships using a synthetic-control-style counterfactual framework; and a multi-objective optimization layer that allocates coverage to high-value accounts while considering continuity, opportunity balance, and operational constraints.Deployed as a scenario-based decision-support system, the framework enables planners to inspect and compare allocation tradeoffs. Historical backtesting shows that optimizer-generated scenarios substantially improve assignment compliance while maintaining comparable seller-level opportunity distribution. In production planning, adopted scenarios improved both assignment compliance and opportunity balance relative to the previous baseline. Directing scarce seller capacity toward higher-value accounts also offers incremental revenue potential at enterprise scale. The key contribution is an integrated, inspectable framework that turns territory carving into a governable decision process.
  </details>

- **[DeepOpen: Decision-Oriented Intent Classification on BANKING77 and CLINC150](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31104)**  `arXiv:2610.31104`  
  _Shuang Song, Muhammad Luqman Naseem, Zhongqiao Yuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present DeepOpen, a decision-Oriented intent-classification framework with dataset- specific systems for BANKING77 and CLINC150. On BANKING77, a single English classi- fier continued with sharpness-aware minimization (SAM) achieves 94.1234% test accuracy (2,899/3,080). This is the best observed result among three fixed SAM seeds, selected for display after test evaluation; their mean is 94.0368%. On the 150 in-scope CLINC150 classes, probability fusion of one Laya specialist and three DeBERTa-v3-large classifiers achieves 98.0222% (4,411/4,500). We describe the two configurations, reproduce the reference com- parisons displayed in the accompanying evaluation explorer, and separate headline scores from evidence about improvement. Under a uniform BANKING77 evaluation path, the SAM mean exceeds the historical checkpoint by 0.0433 percentage points, with a shared-row 95% interval of [-0.2273, +0.3247]. Thus, the continuation does not establish a reliable accuracy gain. The report documents strong observed task-specific performance, selection effects, and unresolved historical execution-path differences, rather than a new backbone or a unified algorithmic advantage.
  </details>

- **[Co-Recursive Semantic Self-Extension: A Mathematical Foundation for Open-Ended Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31097)**  `arXiv:2610.31097`  
  _Simon Hegelich, Bardh Prenkaj, Habiba Sarhan, Morteza Shahrezaye, Kolja Hegelich, Erik Cambria, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Artificial intelligence has advanced through scale, data, and computation, yet remains bounded by its representational framework. We ask what makes intelligence open-ended. Every inferential system selects meaningful distinctions, limiting what it can learn. Within a fixed purpose, performance reaches a ceiling set by its semantic domain. Teleological succession, in which purposes arise from earlier ones, faces a similar limit: Boolean-closed purpose-transformations exhaust their reachable semantic territory. We shift from purposes to inferential systems. Drawing on Kant and Gödel, we model cognitive states as partial evaluations over a formal language. Unresolved propositions may be undecided or undecidable. Reasoning can resolve the former but not the latter, leaving a frontier for extension. Non-trivial reductions of undecidable propositions expose undecidable intermediate premises whose reflective reification supports multiple decisive extensions. Finite trace-guided generation remains fallible, requiring meta-level selection and polycontextural exploration. Using Kolmogorov complexity, we show that no architecture with a fixed state description, transition mechanism, and initial condition can generate histories incorporating irreducible algorithmic information. Because physical systems are fixed in this sense, we examine collectives. Coupling finitely many finite agents does not remove the bound. Extension requires persistent external memory preserving semantic content and historical coordinates. Collective generative capacity depends on balancing overlap for transfer with difference for generation. We propose semantic self-extension as a design principle: intelligence should not merely resolve ambiguity within existing meanings, but transform unresolved structures into new spaces of meaning, generating new distinctions, questions, representations, and purposes.
  </details>

- **[Mitigating Saliency Collapse: Robust Saliency-Aware Long-Text Image-Text Alignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31005)**  `arXiv:2610.31005`  
  _Qiuyu Kong, Zanxi Ruan, Marco Cristani, Yiming Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-text image-text alignment is essential for fine-grained scene understanding, yet existing methods mainly focus on extending context length or improving local alignment. We identify an overlooked failure mode, saliency collapse, where models over-rely on text that features visual prominence while underutilizing non-salient context. Consequently, retrieval performance degrades sharply when salient content is reduced, despite that the non-salient context remains informative. To address this, we propose RoSA, a saliency-aware finetuning framework that decomposes image-text pairs into complementary salient and contextual views, enabling joint learning of prominent and contextual semantics. We propose a modality-independent decomposition strategy to combine object-level visual saliency estimation with LLM-based text decomposition to bridge vision and language. We employ auxiliary objectives to explicitly align these views, ensuring robust representation with both salient and contextual features. RoSA greatly improves long-text retrieval and demonstrates strong robustness to saliency collapse across benchmarks. Moreover, our auxiliary objectives are plug-and-play, bringing consistent gains to existing tuning methods. Code and checkpoints are available at:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Accelerating Wildfire Hazard Mapping Across Canada with an AI Surrogate Modelling Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30170)**  `arXiv:2610.30170`  
  _Hager Radi Abdelwahed, Toluwaleke Olutayo, Chirag Goel, Jana Osea, Aldo Zaimi, Tom Swystun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Canada's increasing wildfire activity requires frequent national hazard mapping to guide proactive mitigation, including fuel management, land-use planning, infrastructure protection, and evacuation routing. However, process-based wildfire simulation frameworks (e.g., BurnP3 and BurnP3+) rely on computationally intensive Monte Carlo simulations to model burn probability, fire intensity, and rate of spread. These workflows can take days to weeks to process a single regional modelling unit. To overcome these computational bottlenecks, we present three contributions developed through collaboration between AI researchers and wildfire modelling experts: (a) a standardized framework for training, fine-tuning, and evaluating AI surrogate models against process-based wildfire simulators; (b) a multi-task deep learning surrogate trained on national-scale data to emulate burn probability, mean fire intensity, and mean rate of spread at a fraction of the computational cost (seconds); and (c) domain-specific metrics and counterfactual evaluation protocols to probe out-of-distribution generalization. Integrating geospatial data (fuel, terrain, ignition likelihood) and regional weather, our model approximates BurnP3+ simulation outputs, achieving strong performance across agreement, rank-correlation, error, and spatial-overlap metrics. It preserves downstream hazard classifications and produces physically plausible responses to expert-informed counterfactual interventions. Furthermore, we demonstrate zero-shot transfer to a separate regional BurnP3+ study and show that lightweight fine-tuning can substantially improve regional calibration and predictive agreement. On a representative region, the surrogate achieves 806-fold end-to-end CPU acceleration and uses 13% of BurnP3+'s host memory, facilitating rapid scenario analysis.
  </details>

- **[EASformer: Environment-Action-State Alignment for Industrial Generative Ranking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27691)**  `arXiv:2610.27691`  
  _Bohao Li, DingLun Cao, Pengcheng Huang, Yingda Tang, Zhiting Zhao, Yi Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern industrial ranking systems increasingly adopt generative recommendation (GR) paradigms, in which user behavior sequences and non-sequential (NS) features are tokenized and jointly modeled by Transformer-style backbones. This unified formulation provides a scalable interface for modeling long histories and heterogeneous features. However, sequence and NS representations often carry asymmetric semantics: historical representations may combine environment context with observed-action semantics, whereas candidate-side NS representations contain no observed action but incorporate richer supplementary context, such as user-item interaction features and recent behavior lists. Direct interaction between such representations can make query-key dot products compare different semantic objects, limiting meaningful cross-token interaction. To fill this gap, we propose EASformer, an Environment-Action-State disentangled architecture for industrial app ranking. EASformer constructs an explicitly aligned context environment space shared by historical and non-sequence tokens, while disentangling user actions from historical token and candidate-specific context form non-sequence tokens. Based on this aligned space, EASformer enables complementary historical action retrieval and state-conditioned action prediction. Furthermore, the disentangled design supports a Cloze-style self-supervised objective by masking historical actions and reusing the candidate prediction pathway. On an offline benchmark from a real-world industrial app marketplace, EASformer achieves an AUC of 0.89411, yielding a 0.88% relative improvement over the deployed baseline. Online A/B tests further support this advantage, with relative eCPM lifts of 1.26% and 3.94% across two real-world ranking scenarios.
  </details>

- **[EvoSkill v2: Fully Agentic Skill Evolution from Benchmark Adaptation to a Stateful Evolver](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25931)**  `arXiv:2610.25931`  
  _Dastin Huang, Baran Nama, Salaheddin Alzubi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  EvoSkill v2 is a three-agent system that edits the instructions (skills) an agent is given, leaving its model and harness intact. The user starts a run by describing a benchmark and supplying a grader, which a benchmark adaptor turns into a benchmark-specific plugin. A solver attempts the tasks, and an evolver reads the solver's training rollouts and rewrites the skills at each epoch. We implement the evolver as one stateful coding agent with a bash tool for the whole run, unlike the two most common designs among the skill optimizers we surveyed: a sequence of single model calls, as in SkillOpt, and an agent that starts afresh each round, as in SkillBoost. We also measure how much the solver's rollouts vary from one attempt to the next, give the evolver several attempts per training task, and accept every epoch's skills instead of selecting among them on noisy scores. With fine-grained ground truth, EvoSkill v2's skills raised the held-out pass rate above the stronger of SkillOpt and SkillBoost by 7.7 percentage points on SpreadsheetBench v2 and by 5.4 on $\tau^3$-bench banking. In both benchmarks and both feedback settings, EvoSkill v2's evolver cost less per percentage point of held-out pass rate gained than either baseline's optimizer. EvoSkill v2 also enables a less capable model to improve its own skills, demonstrated by a gain of up to 21.5 percentage points in the $\tau^3$-bench banking experiment where Claude Haiku 5.5 powers both solver and evolver.
  </details>

- **[AM-Bench: A Unified Taxonomy and Evaluation Suite for Agentic Misalignment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24264)**  `arXiv:2610.24264`  
  _Eric Zhang, Terry Jingchen Zhang, Chijioke Ugwuanyi, Jerick Shi, Bernhard Schölkopf, Zhijing Jin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As LLM-based agents are deployed with increasing autonomy in real-world environments, a critical failure mode emerges: agentic misalignment, where agents choose harmful actions over failure. However, existing safety benchmarks primarily evaluate whether agents comply with explicitly harmful instructions, leaving the conditions that cause agents to choose harmful actions poorly understood. To address this limitation, we propose AM-Bench, a unified taxonomy and 35 scenario evaluation benchmark spanning seven industries for agentic misalignment grounded in the fraud triangle, a well-established criminological framework explaining goal-directed misconduct. We adapt this framework to agentic environments and construct our evaluation suite and detection systems along three dimensions: perceived threat, perceived opportunity, and reasoning. We operationalize each dimension into structured subcategories rooted in prior theory: five threat categories derived from the instrumental convergence thesis, seven opportunity categories drawn from the Emergent Strategic Reasoning Risks (ESRR) taxonomy, and a novel four-question binary reasoning framework synthesized from prior literature. Our evaluation of 10 frontier models reveals a 62-percentage-point gap in misalignment across scenarios, with Gemini 3 Flash and Gemini 3.1 Pro exhibiting the highest levels of agentic misalignment while Claude Sonnet 4.6 displays near-zero rates across all conditions. Our reasoning framework further reveals that models may exhibit harmful behaviours without explicit situational awareness or explanation, complicating both mitigation and detection. AM-Bench scenarios and code are available atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[AgentPerturb: Evaluating LLM Agent Robustness under Controlled Interaction Perturbations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21624)**  `arXiv:2610.21624`  
  _Yuanjian Xu, Jinyi Han, Jianing Hao, Yuxuan Sun, Fanyang Lu, Richeng Xuan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world tasks are often complex and noisy, posing substantial challenges for LLM agents. Yet existing evaluations lack a systematic framework for jointly characterizing potential perturbation targets and types. We use this two-dimensional view to conduct a unified study that quantifies the robustness of current frontier models, traces the mechanisms behind perturbation-induced failures, and draws implications for robustness-oriented training. Through controlled evaluations on APEX-Agents and Toolathlon, we find that perturbations consistently degrade performance, while the dominant vulnerabilities vary across task environments. Trajectory-level analysis reveals that agents rarely recognize altered inputs and often propagate corrupted information without verification. A focused cost analysis further shows that successful perturbed trajectories can require substantially more computation. These findings motivate training objectives that cover diverse forms of noise and reward verification, uncertainty awareness, and recovery. We provide a rigorous benchmark-construction pipeline. Code and data will be released atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Reinforcement Learning-driven Diffusion Models: Language, Vision, and Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.21539)**  `arXiv:2610.21539`  
  _Zhiyuan Ma, Jiabao Wei, Tianyu Wang, Hanqing Wang, Jiadong Pan, Haowen Xu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Diffusion models (DMs) have steadily demonstrated outstanding performance in high-fidelity, controllable generation across diverse scenarios, following the "pretraining-finetuning" paradigm established in LLMs. Despite these advances, conventional DMs still face critical limitations in sophisticated reasoning, autonomous exploration of generation pathways, and adaptability to complex, human-aligned objectives. Inspired by RLHF's success in LLMs, RL has recently emerged as a pivotal post-training mechanism to iteratively refine DMs through reward-driven trajectory optimization. This paper presents the first comprehensive review of RL-driven DMs and an empirical analysis of how RL reshapes the diffusion process towards experience-centric, goal-directed generation. We propose a novel technical panorama elucidating RL's multifaceted roles in diffusion-based language, multimodal, robotic, and scientific models, with a granular focus on diffusion trajectory optimization and reward engineering. Through extensive experiments on multiple task-specific benchmarks, we systematically evaluate representative methods to intuitively highlight the advantages of different RL post-training strategies. Furthermore, we reveal and dissect three crucial bottlenecks that currently hinder the real-world deployment of RL-driven DMs: reward design fragility, exploration-exploitation trade-off in high-dimensional generation spaces, and training instability under sparse rewards. Based on these perspectives, we provide a principled roadmap for the integration of RL into DMs and outline promising future research directions for robust, interpretable RL-diffusion systems. We also construct a public repository to continuously track the latest developments in this rapidly evolving field:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[One-Sided Verification Under Limited Expert Review](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.18089)**  `arXiv:2610.18089`  
  _Fan Nie, Haotian Ye, Shurui Liu, Zihao Wang, Stefano Ermon, James Zou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  AI has become increasingly helpful in scientific discovery, yet human expert review remains essential for those problems without reliable automated evaluation, posing a fundamental challenge as candidate generation scales beyond available review capacity. This paper studies verification under limited human effort, where rare valid solutions need to be prioritized while invalid candidates should be filtered out to reduce human workload. We adopt the one-sided verification objective that maximizes the rejection rate of invalid solutions (TNR) under a constraint on the false-rejection rate of valid ones (FNR), making human review cost an explicit term to minimize. Drawing on the intuition that verifying a specific error can be easier than discovering one, we introduce OSVerify, an algorithmic framework that decouples error proposal from error validation. The error proposer searches for errors in solutions across different granularities by hierarchical decomposition, while the validator distinguishes substantial errors from minor or hallucinated ones. Experiments on competition- and research-level benchmarks show that OSVerify reduces the number of human reviews of invalid solutions by 46% on average compared to voting baselines under the same empirical low FNR constraint. Our post-training of the validator further improves the reduction to 57%. As high-impact real-world use cases, we apply OSVerify to identify errors in published research papers that are subsequently confirmed by the original authors and help resolve three Langlands open conjectures that are confirmed by experts, demonstrating its utility to facilitate human-AI research collaboration.
  </details>

- **[Back to the Future: Rethinking EDA Infrastructure for Agentic Systems in Chip Design Verification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06790)**  `arXiv:2610.06790`  
  _Je Yang, Ivan Lobov, Thomas Karpati_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The unprecedented computational scale of modern artificial intelligence depends on complex, multi-billion-transistor Systems-on-Chip, yet the workflows that verify these chips remain stubbornly manual. Although Large Language Models (LLMs) have made rapid inroads into Electronic Design Automation (EDA), approximately 74.6% of existing studies target static Register-Transfer Level (RTL) code generation, leaving post-simulation verification and interactive waveform debugging largely untouched. We introduce Back-to-the-Future (BTTF), an end-to-end agentic framework that closes this infrastructural gap. BTTF distills massive, unstructured simulation dumps into a normalized relational SQLite database and couples it with a collaborative multi-agent orchestration engine that translates natural-language verification queries into schema-aware SQL while correlating signal anomalies with versioned RTL repositories. Across a 150-query benchmark, BTTF attains 95.33% execution accuracy, charting a practical path toward autonomous EDA verification.
  </details>

- **[Train4Merge: RL or SFT---Which Yields Better Teachers for OPD-Based Model Merging?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.32303)**  `arXiv:2609.32303`  
  _Jingyuan Huang, Zuming Huang, Yucheng Shi, Zhongzhi Li, Xiaoming Zhai, Wei Chu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Domain experts trained from a shared checkpoint can transfer their specialized capabilities to a single student through on-policy distillation (OPD). Existing research primarily focuses on improving this merging process, while the algorithms used to train the experts have received limited systematic comparison. We investigate which training algorithm produces teachers better suited to OPD through controlled single-teacher comparisons of supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) across Agentic, Reasoning, and Perception. We further examine whether this advantage extends to multi-teacher OPD (MOPD). Teachers and students share the same Qwen3.5-9B initialization, and the two teacher types are compared at similar task performance. Our single-teacher experiments show that RL teachers yield stronger students and higher recovery of teacher performance gains across all three domains. In MOPD, the RL teacher group also yields a student with a higher three-domain mean score and higher overall teacher gain recovery than the SFT teacher group. Our further analysis shows that RL teachers undergo smaller parameter displacements from the shared initialization than SFT teachers. Teacher--student Top-K overlap also increases faster early in OPD or remains consistently higher with RL teachers than with SFT teachers. These findings support the hypothesis that RL teachers' smaller departures from the student's starting point facilitate learning through OPD, resulting in stronger students.
  </details>

- **[Symbolic Guidance for LLM Agents in Distributed Multiagent Coordination](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31963)**  `arXiv:2609.31963`  
  _Ben Rachmut, Ning Zhang, Yevgeniy Vorobeychik, William Yeoh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly deployed as autonomous agents in multi-agent systems, yet their ability to reliably execute distributed coordination protocols remains poorly understood. While AgentsNet, a benchmark framework for distributed coordination among LLM agents, enables such coordination, granting full reasoning autonomy often leads to inconsistent or degraded performance in complex domains.We hypothesize that coordination can be improved by regulating agent autonomy through symbolic guidance derived from established algorithms. To investigate this, we introduce the \emph{Symbolic Guidance Taxonomy (SGT)}, which characterizes a spectrum of autonomy ranging from open-ended natural language reasoning to fully prescribed algorithmic execution, with intermediate levels providing partial pseudocode guidance. Our results show that intermediate autonomy levels consistently outperform both unguided agents and fully prescriptive specifications. These findings identify autonomy regulation as a key design principle for LLM-based distributed coordination.
  </details>

- **[Metro-WM: Long-Horizon Latent Planning with Realisable Sub-Goals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.31868)**  `arXiv:2609.31868`  
  _Royson Lee, Fady Rezk, Titouan Parcollet, Timothy Hospedales, Cristina Cornelio_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Model-predictive control with Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) provides a strong zero-shot goal-reaching planner, but it is only effective over short planning horizons. Hierarchical extensions attempt to bridge this gap by learning a macro planner to predict intermediate latent sub-goals to guide the micro planner. In this work, we demonstrate that unconstrained latent sub-goal prediction is fundamentally flawed. A rigorous evaluation reveals that leading hierarchical macro planners routinely emit physically unrealisable sub-goals. To resolve this, we introduce Metro-WM, a hierarchical framework that issues sub-goals by retrieving genuine states from prior experience rather than generating ungrounded latent vectors. Specifically, Metro-WM constructs a graph whose vertices are observed frames from offline expert demonstrations or random-action trajectories, allowing frames from different episodes to be connected and stitched into routes to the goal. Planning over the full graph also makes the system highly robust to execution errors: if the micro planner drifts off course, Metro-WM instantly finds a new optimal path from the current state. Our experiments show that Metro-WM achieves superior long-horizon success rates of up to 37.33 percentage points over the strongest hierarchical baseline on the same world model while being up to 10.9 times faster, requiring both 13-56 times less offline compute and fewer tuned hyperparameters. Additional analysis reveals that Metro-WM frequently finds shorter paths than the offline demonstrations, outperforms an oracle relying on the query's own demonstration on most benchmarks, and maintains robust performance under extremely sparse dataset conditions.
  </details>

- **[When Clinical Judgment Becomes a Predictive Shortcut: Two Questions Clinical AI Must Answer Before Deployment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.25852)**  `arXiv:2609.25852`  
  _Jing Yang, Long R. Jiao, Zongjiu Zhang, Xiujun Cai_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical AI can predict outcomes using information generated by existing clinical judgment. Such performance alone cannot establish usefulness before recognition or across care processes. We propose evidence requirements matched to intended use and information conditions, with additional state-reference and proxy-relative timing tests for stronger recognition claims. This framework helps hospitals assess whether the conditions supporting performance hold when assistance is most needed. These questions remain outside current evaluation practice leakage checks, overall predictive performance, and external validation do not address them; the framework makes them explicit and specifies the direct evidence required to answer them.
  </details>

- **[Taming CoT Obfuscation in VLMs: From Mechanistic Evidence to Activation-Level Enforcement](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.24243)**  `arXiv:2609.24243`  
  _Xutao Mao, Jianing Zhu, Jinman Zhao, Tongliang Liu, Xiaowen Chu, Cong Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reinforcement learning (RL) has substantially improved the reasoning capabilities of vision-language models (VLMs), yet it can induce chain-of-thought (CoT) obfuscation, an operational, non-intentional regime where models achieve high accuracy while producing reasoning traces that are less grounded and harder to monitor. While prior work documents this decay at the behavioral level, the underlying representational correlates of such drift remain poorly understood. In this work, we find that obfuscation is accompanied by a representational shift, where RL-induced optimization makes task-agnostic template features less separable from visually grounded content in the model's activation space. Based on this insight, we propose TAME, an activation-level intervention framework that leverages Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) to regularize VLM internal features during RL training. Specifically, TAME utilizes an anti-obfuscation feedback loop to reduce behavioral obfuscation and applies an asymmetric SAE constraint to specific internal directions, penalizing template activations while preserving visual evidence. By directly intervening on the features associated with obfuscation, our method improves the transparency and visual grounding of reasoning traces. Experiments on the VIRL-39k and SPA-VL benchmarks across two model families show that TAME improves CoT monitorability (by up to +30.9 points on VIRL and +16.7 points on SPA-VL over GRPO), while largely preserving task accuracy and general-capability performance across four standard VLM benchmarks, with task-specific trade-offs. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[PlaceReasoner-Beta: Reasoning-Driven Macro Placement and Benchmarking](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.21263)**  `arXiv:2609.21263`  
  _Qiufeng Li, Chengxuan Wang, Rongqian Chen, Quan Cheng, Yihui Ren, Chia-Tung Ho, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Automated macro placement remains a fundamental challenge in VLSI physical design. Despite decades of research, existing approaches predominantly optimize hand-crafted proxy objectives, such as estimated wirelength, and typically produce placements through one-shot numerical optimization, limiting their ability to incorporate visual layout context, codified design expertise, and downstream physical-design feedback in a unified loop. We present PlaceReasoner-Beta, a verifier-guided multi-agent framework that reformulates macro placement as a closed-loop reasoning problem rather than black-box optimization. A vision-language model (VLM) planner generates candidate placements from the floorplan image, macro specifications, and connectivity structure; a geometric verifier enforces physical legality and expert placement principles; a physical verifier refines candidates using early implementation feedback; and a post-route optimizer further improves promising layouts using final PPA. To enable reproducible evaluation, we introduce PlaceReasoner-Bench, a fully open end-to-end benchmark built from open RTL designs, EDA tools, and technology libraries. It comprises 8 designs at two aspect ratios, yielding 16 tasks with fixed floorplans and I/O assignments, so methods differ only in macro positions and orientations and are evaluated using routed PPA and DRC rather than pre-route proxies. Across the benchmark, PlaceReasoner-Beta achieves the best timing among DRC-clean methods on all square tasks, reducing post-route TNS by 61.2% at 1:1 and 53.0% at 2:1 relative to the classical baseline field. It also shortens routed wirelength on most designs despite never explicitly optimizing it, demonstrating that reasoning over spatial structure under physical-design feedback can improve end-to-end layout quality beyond proxy-objective optimization.
  </details>

- **[Interchangeable in Role, Not in History: Replacing Members of Formed LLM Teams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.05279)**  `arXiv:2609.05279`  
  _Jianxin Gao, Runze Li, Tianyi Yu, Liangwei Ren, Bohan Chen, Zining Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multiagent LLM systems often treat a role as a slot: when a member is replaced, any agent competent at the role is expected to fill it. We test this view on teams whose members keep short written notebooks. A one-seat swap replays a formed, frozen team with its own member, with a same-role newcomer carrying another team's notebook for that role, or with a newcomer carrying none. In cooperative cooking, wildfire response and medical question answering, the experienced newcomer, averaged over seats, outscores the one without a notebook in every setting, so the usual comparison would approve it. Against the original team, the same replacement falls short in every setting, and the team spends more messages per unit of progress. On the hardest cooking level, the shortfall appears only after the team has worked together, persists across model families and decoding temperatures, and narrows by two thirds with added planning-only members. Because the team's history is written down, we can take it apart. In the default configuration on that level, notebooks from the same donor teams leave a newcomer that leads, holding the recipe, worse off than no notebook and one that follows better off. The notebook lines about the partner reproduce this whole difference between the seats. Whether an agent can replace a teammate depends on the seat and the team it enters.
  </details>

- **[MirrorCraft: Paired Evaluation under Hidden Rule Changes in Minecraft](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.29218)**  `arXiv:2607.29218`  
  _Jianxin Gao, Beini Hu, Runze Li, Hongjian Fang, Wanli Peng_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  With the rapid progress of large language models (LLMs), an interesting question has emerged: how do LLM-based agents perform in Minecraft? To date, most Minecraft benchmarks evaluate them under fixed game mechanics, which are extensively documented online. High performance in these settings does not show whether an agent can continue making progress when familiar recipes, drops, and other rules change. In this paper, we introduce MirrorCraft, a paired benchmark for evaluating agents under hidden rule changes in Minecraft. Each Mirror world is a copy of its paired Vanilla world, with selected server-side rules silently modified by the datapack of one rule suite. Terrain, spawn, resource placement, task, interface, and action budget remain matched within every Vanilla-Mirror pair. MirrorCraft includes 10 Vanilla worlds in five controlled biomes, six rule suites, and three progression tasks, and we evaluate two LLMs and six agent configurations under a shared Mineflayer interface. We measure progress with server-verified advancement milestones and completion with success rate, and define the Rule Intervention Effect (RIE) as the performance change between paired Vanilla and Mirror worlds. Experiments with 8,640 episodes show that hidden rule changes have sharply different effects across suites. Among the configurations evaluated without rule descriptions, ReAct, Reflexion, and Voyager achieve the highest pooled Mirror Scores, but ReAct loses the most under compressed resource drops. Providing ReAct with the exact rules yields modest gains in average progress and completion across all three tasks, yet most of the difference between suites remains once the rules are known. MirrorCraft extends Minecraft evaluation beyond fixed mechanics and provides a controlled setting for studying how agents use gameplay outcomes when the rules of the current world differ from familiar ones.
  </details>

- **[Where Cross-Question Structure in LLM Errors Comes From: Shared Seeds, Shared Prompts, and Answer-Letter Preference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.20464)**  `arXiv:2607.20464`  
  _Izhar Ali_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Disagreement between language models is often read as missing knowledge. Questions that need the same missing knowledge should then be right or wrong together. We test for this structure with the eigenvalues of the questions' correlation matrix. Within one model, independently drawn answers show no detectable structure; it appears when all questions in one pass over the benchmark share a random seed or few-shot prompt. Across 24 models, the largest structure beyond overall skill is a preference among answer letters: when the options are rotated, it follows the letter, not the question, and it depends on one model, Llama-3.2-1B; without it only a smaller excess remains. Counting a question correct only if right in every rotation removes the part that acts on guesses. What remains is a marginal excess at the 5% line, tied post hoc to two near-duplicate model pairs and to Llama-3.2-1B.
  </details>

- **[DeepSafe-SciHazard: A Benchmark for Measuring Scientific Safety Risks with Decomposed Harm Scoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.18665)**  `arXiv:2607.18665`  
  _Chunxiao Li, Yuan Xiong, Lijun Li, Tianyi Du, Wenlong Zhang, Lei Bai, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly support science, but they can also convert hazardous scientific knowledge into actionable misuse guidance. Existing benchmarks often rely on templated queries disconnected from real-world hazards, and employ LLM-as-a-Judge paradigms without domain grounding. To address this, we introduce SciHazard, a real-world-grounded benchmark for scientific risks and a dataset agnostic evaluation framework for measuring harmfulness. SciHazard contains 2400 hazardous questions and 600 oversafety questions across 12 disciplines, with both queries grounded in regulated entities and documented failure scenarios. To compute \textsc{DeHarm-Score} , we develop a decomposed evaluating procedure that combines query hazard severity, refusal behavior, and response-level risk. For non-refused responses, it further decomposes response-level harm into \textsc{Executability}, quantified via dynamic checklists with importance weighting, and \textsc{Net-new risk}, assessed through retrieval-augmented claim extraction and synthesis-barrier verification. An expert-validation study shows that \textsc{DeHarm-Score} improves agreement with expert annotations by 90.17\% over the strongest baseline. We benchmark 31 frontier LLMs and deep research agents in an extensive scientific safety evaluation. Notably, deep research agents yield 32.3\% higher mean \textsc{DeHarm-Score} than standard LLMs, exposing autonomous agents as a critical blind spot in current safety defenses. Code and dataset are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Attractor Domain Theory: A Mathematical Framework for Cardiovascular Attractor Analysis with Wearable Photoplethysmography (PPG) Validation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.22039)**  `arXiv:2606.22039`  
  _Timothy Oladunni, Farouk Ganiyu Adewumi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The cardiac attractor provides a phase-space representation of cardiovascular dynamics reconstructed from scalar observations under standard delay-embedding conditions. We introduce Attractor Domain Theory (ADT), a theory in which linked mathematical transformations expose distinct properties of reconstructed dynamics: the Geometry Domain is obtained through the delay-reconstruction transformation, while Ergodic and Variational transformations act on that geometry to characterize long-horizon organization and localized finite-time deformation. Thus, ADT specifies a three-representation operator architecture for reconstructed dynamics rather than retrospectively grouping existing nonlinear features; the architecture is proposed as an operational organization, not as a uniqueness or exhaustiveness theorem. A Conditional Domain Decomposition quantifies endpoint-specific incremental information with an explicit residual, and a domain-transformation impossibility theorem establishes that a property cannot be exactly recovered when an operator maps property-distinct inputs to the same representation.
