Empirical evaluation includes PPG analysis across 176{,}742 segments with 18 patient-level held-out BIDMC records (AUC 0.757 [0.686--0.828]) and an independent ECG analysis of 5{,}000 PTB-XL records (AUC 0.719 [0.686--0.750]). Cross-observation transfer is property-specific: the Lyapunov exponent shows subject-level ECG--PPG agreement ($\rho=0.703$, 95\% CI [0.503, 0.836]), whereas the predicted class-wide separation of invariant and observation-coupled descriptors is not supported ($p=0.54$). These results provide empirical support for attractor-domain analysis across cardiovascular measurements while leaving cross-observation recoverability as a property-specific empirical question.
  </details>

- **[AlphaMemo: Structured Search-Process Memory for Self-Evolving Alpha Mining Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.20625)**  `arXiv:2606.20625`  
  _Hang Yu, Zifan Zheng, Chun Gan, Jeff Z. Pan, Tongliang Liu, Zhiyong Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM agents are promising for alpha mining via combining financial priors, symbolic reasoning, executable factor generation, and feedback-driven refinement. Yet, they face a combinatorial search space, noisy non-stationary feedback, redundant discoveries, and overfitting risks from naively reusing past successes. To address these challenges, we propose AlphaMemo, a self-evolving alpha mining agent with Structured Search-Process Memory. Rather than memorizing only final factors or full trajectories, AlphaMemo records reusable evidence about which edit motifs work or fail under specific parent-factor contexts. It extracts motifs from Abstract Syntax Tree (AST) differences, applies confidence-gated residual memory on top of a search-ledger prior, and uses asymmetric veto control to suppress high-confidence failure patterns. Experiments on CSI 500 and S&P 500 show improved out-of-sample performance and fixed-budget discovery efficiency, with ablations validating the roles of residual learning, confidence gating, AST-diff motifs, and veto memory. Code is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Trust the Right Teacher: Quality-Aware Self-Distillation for GUI Grounding](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.18101)**  `arXiv:2606.18101`  
  _Jingyuan Huang, Zuming Huang, Yucheng Shi, Tianze Yang, Xiaoming Zhai, Wei Chu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  GUI grounding requires vision-language models (VLMs) to identify small target elements in high-resolution screenshots and predict precise screen coordinates. On-policy self-distillation (OPSD) is a promising post-training approach for this coordinate-sensitive task because it provides dense token-level teacher signals beyond hard coordinate labels. However, naive OPSD is not well-suited to GUI grounding: since the teacher is evaluated on student-generated prefixes, its coordinate-token signals can become unreliable once a prefix deviates from the target coordinate. We propose quality-aware self-distillation for GUI grounding, centered on direct spatial correctness verification of teacher predictions. A soft gate checks whether the teacher's top coordinate-token prediction can still be completed into the ground-truth box under the student-generated prefix and down-weights the signal when this check fails. Teacher-probability scaling then uses the teacher's confidence to further calibrate the gated supervision. A key empirical finding is that neither component alone improves overall performance, whereas combining them consistently does. This suggests that the two mechanisms play complementary roles: gating suppresses unreliable coordinate-token supervision, while probability scaling calibrates the remaining signals. Experiments across six GUI grounding benchmarks show that our method consistently improves the base model and outperforms strong baselines.
  </details>

- **[Architecture-Aware Reinforcement Learning Makes Sliding-Window Attention Competitive in Math Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.11634)**  `arXiv:2606.11634`  
  _Kai Liu, Peijie Dong, Xinchen Xie, Jianfei Gao, Qipeng Guo, Xiaowen Chu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid progress of reasoning and agentic large language models (LLMs) has increased the demand for long-context inference, but self-attention (SA) scales quadratically with context length.To address this, we study SWARR (Sliding-Window Attention with Reinforced Adaptation for Math Reasoning), a practical recipe for adapting SWA models to mathematical reasoning. SWARR has two stages: (1) efficient conversion from a pretrained SA model to SWA with supervised fine-tuning (SFT), which avoids pretraining a new base model, and (2) policy adaptation with reinforcement learning (RL).We find that SWA still underperforms SA after SFT, and we hypothesize that this gap is caused in part by a data-architecture mismatch: most SFT data are prepared for SA models and may contain long-range dependencies that are difficult for SWA to model. Because on-policy RL optimizes self-generated trajectories under the SWA constraint, it can adapt trajectories to better match SWA.Experiments on mathematical reasoning benchmarks show that this recipe substantially narrows the gap between SWA and SA, recovering much of the accuracy lost during SWA conversion while preserving the efficiency benefits of linear-complexity attention. Our central contribution is the empirical finding that RL changes the conclusion one would draw from conversion and SFT alone about SWA's viability for math reasoning.
  </details>

- **[SkillPyramid: A Hierarchical Skill Consolidation Framework for Self-Evolving Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.03692)**  `arXiv:2606.03692`  
  _Yuan Xiong, Ziqi Miao, Qian Chen, Lijun Li, Ken Deng, Yequan Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent AI agents can flexibly invoke skills to solve complex tasks, but their long-term improvement is fundamentally constrained by a lack of systematic skill construction, accumulation, and transfer. In particular, without a unified framework for skill consolidation, agents tend to redundantly construct similar capabilities across different tasks, are unable to effectively transform experience into reusable assets, and struggle to generalize task-specific skills to novel scenarios. To address this limitation, we propose SkillPyramid, a skill consolidation framework that reuses existing skill experience for broader task generalization. Operating on a hierarchical skill topology, SkillPyramid further introduces a self-evolution mechanism that enables agents to compose, validate, and incorporate new skills during task execution. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld across four backbone models show that SkillPyramid substantially increases the average reward by 38.0% and reduces execution steps by 27.7%. Overall, our method transforms a skill collection from a static resource pool into a dynamic evolution system.
  </details>

- **[Same Answer, Different Confidence: Protocol Sensitivity in LLM Confidence Calibration](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27752)**  `arXiv:2605.27752`  
  _Hankyeol Kim, Pilsung Kang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLM) can state their confidence in an answer (verbalized confidence), or the confidence can be read from the probability of the answer's tokens (token confidence). Comparisons of the two ask which is better calibrated, but the token probability of an answer depends on the text that precedes it. Across five open LLMs and four question-answering datasets, we fix each answer, its verbalized confidence, and its correctness label, and vary how each score is measured. The scores themselves change with the protocol, and so does the comparison between them. Changing the context in which the token probability is read shifts the gap between the two scores by about the size of the gap itself (.287 vs. .304 in expected calibration error, ECE; .189 vs. .145 in AUROC). The 95% interval of the shift excludes zero in 19 and 17 of the 20 settings, and under AUROC the shift has the same sign in all of them, so the context changes how answers are ranked. The better score flips in 8 settings under each metric, counting only flips that the intervals support, and the shift also appears in four larger models of up to 72B parameters. Most of the shift appears with changes to the instruction, not to the position of the answer. Other measurement choices also matter, and stated confidence depends on how a supplied answer is written. We turn these results into a reporting checklist for LLM confidence and its comparisons.
  </details>

- **[Can Broad Biomedical Knowledge be Contextualized into Scenario-Grounded Propositions?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.27082)**  `arXiv:2605.27082`  
  _Qingyuan Zeng, Ziyang Chen, Pengxiang Cai, Zixin Guan, Anglin Liu, Lang Qin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Biomedical discovery often requires connecting broad biomedical knowledge with specific experimental or clinical data. Background knowledge suggests relevant mechanisms but is usually too general to map directly onto dataset variables, while data-driven patterns can be dataset-specific and hard to interpret mechanistically. We study this missing link as knowledge contextualization: transforming broad biomedical knowledge into evidence-supported, scenario-grounded propositions that domain experts can inspect, replay, and validate. We propose SCENE, a bi-level multi-agent framework that treats knowledge contextualization as iterative search. The upper level converts broad knowledge into search directions and grounds them in the dataset schema. The lower level executes these directions through multi-objective optimization to identify concrete propositions that balance evidential strength and data support. Feedback between the two levels progressively refines the search. We evaluate SCENE in two settings: discovering patient subgroups with heterogeneous treatment benefits in clinical trial scenarios, and identifying context-specific biological responses in LINCS L1000 studies. In clinical trials, SCENE discovers specific, well-supported subgroups and outperforms existing baselines. In L1000 studies, SCENE identifies perturbational contexts with strong target-response matching and high positive rates. These results show that SCENE bridges broad knowledge and scenario-specific evidence, producing traceable, inspectable hypotheses for follow-up validation.
  </details>

- **[Model-Adaptive Tool Necessity Reveals the Knowing-Doing Gap in LLM Tool Use](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.14038)**  `arXiv:2605.14038`  
  _Yize Cheng, Chenrui Fan, Mahdi JafariRaviz, Keivan Rezaei, Soheil Feizi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) increasingly act as autonomous agents that must decide when to answer directly vs. when to invoke external tools. Prior work studying adaptive tool use has largely treated tool necessity as a model-agnostic property, annotated by human or LLM judge, and mostly cover cases where the answer is obvious (e.g., fetching the weather vs. paraphrasing text). However, tool necessity in the wild is more nuanced due to the divergence of capability boundaries across models: a problem solvable by a strong model on its own may still require tools for a weaker one. In this work, we introduce a model-adaptive definition of tool-necessity, grounded in each model's empirical performance. Following this definition, we compare the necessity against observed tool-call behavior across four models on arithmetic and factual QA dataset, and find substantial mismatches of 26.5-54.0% and 30.8-41.8%, respectively. To diagnose the failure, we decompose tool use into two stages: an internal cognition stage that reflects whether a model believes a tool is necessary, and an execution stage that determines whether the model actually makes a tool-call action. By probing the LLM hidden states, we find that both signals are often linearly decodable, yet their probe directions become nearly orthogonal in the late-layer, last-token regime that drives the next-token action. By tracing the trajectory of samples in the two-stage process, we further discover that the majority of mismatch is concentrated in the cognition-to-action transition, not in cognition itself. These results reveal a knowing-doing gap in LLM tool-use: improving tool-use reliability requires not only better recognition of when tools are needed, but also better translation of that recognition into action.
  </details>

- **[MCPHunt: An Evaluation Framework for Cross-Boundary Data Propagation in Multi-Server MCP Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.27819)**  `arXiv:2604.27819`  
  _Haonan Li, Tianjun Sun, Yongqing Wang, Qisheng Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multi-server MCP agents create an information-flow control (IFC) problem: faithful tool composition can turn individually benign read/write permissions into cross-boundary credential propagation -- a structural side effect of workflow topology, not necessarily malicious model behavior. We present MCPHunt, to our knowledge the first controlled benchmark that isolates non-adversarial, exact-match credential propagation across multi-server MCP trust boundaries, with three methodological contributions: (1) canary-based taint tracking that reduces propagation detection to objective string matching; (2) an environment-controlled coverage design with risky, benign, and hard-negative conditions that validates pipeline soundness and adds a matched alternative-value control (same tasks, distinct synthetic credentials); (3) completion-requires-secret (CRS) stratification that disentangles task-mandated propagation (faithful execution of verbatim-transfer instructions) from policy-violating propagation (credentials included despite the option to redact). Across 3,615 main-benchmark traces from 5 models spanning 147 tasks and 9 mechanism families, policy-violating propagation rates of 11.5-41.3% persist across all models. This propagation is pathway-specific (rates from 0.0% to 74.4% across mechanisms for GPT-5.4) and concentrated in browser-mediated data flows; under the matched alternative-value control, propagation persists (25.9% vs. 20.4%; the matched-pair CI spans zero, so the comparison neither distinguishes nor equates the two rates). A prompt-mitigation study across 3 models shows policy-violating propagation rates as low as 0.3% (98% relative reduction from baseline) while preserving 80.5% utility, but effectiveness varies across models, motivating data-flow-aware defenses beyond prompting. Code, traces, and the labeling pipeline are publicly available under MIT and CC BY 4.0.
  </details>

- **[Uncertainty-Guided Latent Diagnostic Trajectory Learning for Sequential Clinical Diagnosis](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.05116)**  `arXiv:2604.05116`  
  _Xuyang Shen, Haoran Liu, Dongjin Song, Martin Renqiang Min_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Clinical diagnosis requires sequential evidence acquisition under uncertainty. However, most Large Language Model (LLM) based diagnostic systems assume fully observed patient information and therefore do not explicitly model how clinical evidence should be sequentially acquired over time. Even when diagnosis is formulated as a sequential decision process, it is still challenging to learn effective diagnostic trajectories. This is because the space of possible evidence-acquisition paths is relatively large, while clinical datasets rarely provide explicit supervision information for desirable diagnostic paths. To this end, we formulate sequential diagnosis as a Latent Diagnostic Trajectory Learning (LDTL) framework based on a planning LLM agent and a diagnostic LLM agent. For the diagnostic LLM agent, diagnostic action sequences are treated as latent paths and we introduce a posterior distribution that prioritizes trajectories providing more diagnostic information. The planning LLM agent is then trained to follow this distribution, encouraging coherent diagnostic trajectories that progressively reduce uncertainty. Experiments on the MIMIC-CDM benchmark demonstrate that our proposed LDTL framework outperforms existing baselines in diagnostic accuracy under a sequential clinical diagnosis setting, while requiring fewer diagnostic tests. Furthermore, ablation studies highlight the critical role of trajectory-level posterior alignment in achieving these improvements.
  </details>

- **[Spatial disparities in simulated patrol detection: a reproducible generative-model audit across Baltimore and Chicago](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.18987)**  `arXiv:2603.18987`  
  _Pronob Kumar Barman, Pronoy Kumar Barman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Auditing predictive policing requires distinguishing recorded crime incidents, simulated patrol observations, and the demographic characteristics of places. We present a reproducible spatial simulation audit combining a generative adversarial network (GAN) for patrol allocation with a probabilistic detection model. Using frozen archives of 145,178 Baltimore records (2017-2019) and 234,882 Chicago records (2022), we rebuild geographic covariates from Census tract boundaries and American Community Survey data. We compare GAN allocation with empirical-location sampling and uniform-area deployment under alternative observation streams. Twenty random seeds per condition yield 560 trajectories, each covering February through December. Under detection-only training, mean detection-rate differences between Black-majority and White-majority areas range from -2.03 to 0.75 percentage points across city-years, while total coverage ranges from 1.78% to 7.50%. Paired comparisons with full-record reference trajectories assess observation feedback conditional on the recorded incident archives. In an experiment using a conditional tabular generative model for balanced synthetic replacement, geographic consistency requirements fail in 107 of 220 seed-month fits, leaving no complete annual synthetic-replacement trajectories. Simulated disparities vary with the observation process, allocation policy, training budget, and random seed. These results support reproducible comparisons of modeled scenarios but do not estimate individual racial disparities in police contact or the distribution of unrecorded crime. We recommend reporting absolute detection differences, coverage, synthesis feasibility, and simulation uncertainty alongside ratio-based fairness metrics.
  </details>

- **[Learning to Predict, Discover, and Reason in High-Cardinality Event Sequences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.16313)**  `arXiv:2603.16313`  
  _Hugo Math_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Electronic control units (ECUs) embedded within modern vehicles generate a large number of asynchronous events known as diagnostic trouble codes (DTCs). These discrete events form complex temporal sequences that reflect the evolving health of the vehicle's subsystems. In the automotive industry, domain experts manually group these codes into higher-level error patterns (EPs) using Boolean rules to characterize system faults and ensure safety. However, as vehicle complexity grows, this manual process becomes increasingly costly, error-prone, and difficult to scale. Notably, the number of unique DTCs in a modern vehicle is on the same order of magnitude as the vocabulary of a natural language, often numbering in the tens of thousands. This observation motivates a paradigm shift: treating diagnostic sequences as a language that can be modeled, predicted, and ultimately explained. Traditional statistical approaches fail to capture the rich dependencies and do not scale to high-cardinality datasets characterized by thousands of nodes, large sample sizes, and long sequence lengths. Specifically, the high cardinality of categorical event spaces in industrial logs poses a significant challenge, necessitating new machine learning architectures tailored to such event-driven systems. This thesis addresses automated fault diagnostics by unifying event sequence modeling, causal discovery, and large language models (LLMs) into a coherent framework for event streams. It is structured in three parts, reflecting a progressive transition from prediction to causal understanding and finally to reasoning for vehicle diagnostics. Consequently, we introduce several Transformer-based architectures for predictive maintenance, scalable sample- and population-level causal discovery frameworks and a multi-agent system that automates the synthesis of Boolean EP rules.
  </details>

- **[MedInsightBench: Evaluating Medical Analytics Agents Through Multi-Step Insight Discovery in Multimodal Medical Data](https://arxiv.org/abs/2512.13297)**  `arXiv:2512.13297`  
  _Zhenghao Zhu, Chuxue Cao, Sirui Han, Yuanfeng Song, Xing Chen, Caleb Chen Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In medical data analysis, extracting deep insights from complex, multi-modal datasets is essential for improving patient care, increasing diagnostic accuracy, and optimizing healthcare operations. However, there is currently a lack of high-quality datasets specifically designed to evaluate the ability of large multi-modal models (LMMs) to discover medical insights. In this paper, we introduce MedInsightBench, the first benchmark that comprises 332 carefully curated medical cases, each annotated with thoughtfully designed insights. This benchmark is intended to evaluate the ability of LMMs and agent frameworks to analyze multi-modal medical image data, including posing relevant questions, interpreting complex findings, and synthesizing actionable insights and recommendations. Our analysis indicates that existing LMMs exhibit limited performance on MedInsightBench, which is primarily attributed to their challenges in extracting multi-step, deep insights and the absence of medical expertise. Therefore, we propose MedInsightAgent, an automated agent framework for medical data analysis, composed of three modules: Visual Root Finder, Analytical Insight Agent, and Follow-up Question Composer. Experiments on MedInsightBench highlight pervasive challenges and demonstrate that MedInsightAgent can improve the performance of general LMMs in medical data insight discovery.
  </details>

- **[MEDMKG: Benchmarking Medical Knowledge Exploitation with Multimodal Knowledge Graph](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.17214)**  `arXiv:2505.17214`  
  _Xiaochen Wang, Yuan Zhong, Lingwei Zhang, Lisong Dai, Ting Wang, Fenglong Ma_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Medical deep learning models depend heavily on domain-specific knowledge to perform well on knowledge-intensive clinical tasks. Prior work has primarily leveraged unimodal knowledge graphs, such as the Unified Medical Language System (UMLS), to enhance model performance. However, integrating multimodal medical knowledge graphs remains largely underexplored, mainly due to the lack of resources linking imaging data with clinical concepts. To address this gap, we propose MEDMKG, a Medical Multimodal Knowledge Graph that unifies visual and textual medical information through a multi-stage construction pipeline. MEDMKG fuses the rich multimodal data from MIMIC-CXR with the structured clinical knowledge from UMLS, utilizing both rule-based tools and large language models for accurate concept extraction and relationship modeling. To ensure graph quality and compactness, we introduce Neighbor-aware Filtering (NaF), a novel filtering algorithm tailored for multimodal knowledge graphs. We evaluate MEDMKG across three tasks under two experimental settings, benchmarking twenty-four baseline methods and four state-of-the-art vision-language backbones on six datasets. Results show that MEDMKG not only improves performance in downstream medical tasks but also offers a strong foundation for developing adaptive and robust strategies for multimodal knowledge integration in medical artificial intelligence.
  </details>

- **[Yalda-Embedding: Open Development of a Competitive Persian Retrieval Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32933)**  `arXiv:2610.32933`  
  _Yalda Taheri, Mohammad Hassan Heydari, Armon Rasooli_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Despite advances in multilingual text representations, effective dense retrieval for Persian remains constrained by the limited availability of language-specific training resources and compact embedding models. We present Yalda Embedding, a 595.8-million-parameter Persian retrieval model adapted from Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B using more than one million query-document pairs. On eight Persian retrieval tasks from FaMTEB, the model achieves the highest macro-average among the evaluated 0.6B models, scoring 0.5488 and outperforming Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B and F2LLM-v2-0.6B on every task. It also approaches Qwen3-Embedding-4B, winning on four tasks, tying on one, and trailing by 0.0075 in macro-average. An incremental ablation study examines contrastive batch size, optimization stabilization, and relational knowledge distillation from F2LLM-v2-8B. Under the one-epoch training setup, contrastive fine-tuning alone yields a modest mean improvement over initialization and regressions on two tasks. The final distilled configuration improves all eight tasks over the non-distilled model, raising the mean from 0.4813 to 0.5488 without increasing inference-time model size. These gains include hard-negative evaluation tasks despite training without explicitly mined or supplied hard negatives. We release model weights, training and evaluation code, the pairwise dataset, and its teacher-embedded counterpart, enabling inspection of data composition and training procedures, independent evaluation, and reuse without repeating expensive teacher inference.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive Frequency Threshold Byte Pair Encoding for Mixed Natural Language and Structured Symbolic Corpora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32921)**  `arXiv:2610.32921`  
  _Pietro Di Nicco, Lucio La Cava, Andrea Tagarelli_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Modern language models rely on subwording tokenization to define the discrete units of input processing, typically using methods like Byte Pair Encoding (BPE) that treat whitespace as a fixed semantic boundary. This design is inherently limited, as many meaningful concepts---including multiword expressions and the structured environments of formal languages like LaTeX---frequently span across whitespaces. While recent superword tokenization methods attempt to address this by relaxing whitespace constraints, they often rely on greedy frequency-driven merging that fails to account for token length or the specific needs of underrepresented domain-specific languages. In this paper, we introduce Adaptive Frequency Threshold Byte Pair Encoding (AdaBPE), a new tokenization method that leverages an adaptive frequent-pattern mining approach. AdaBPE features a twofold dynamicity: it adapts minimum frequency thresholds based on both the length of a candidate token, to prevent favoring frequent short patterns, and its type, to distinguish between general and formal language patterns, using LaTeX as case in point. This allows the model to efficiently allocate vocabulary capacity to domain-specific notations, such as LaTeX, even when they represent a small fraction of the training data. We evaluate AdaBPE across five datasets spanning LaTeX and natural language, showing consistent gains in encoding efficiency and downstream language-model performance on domain-specific content without compromising general-language performance.
  </details>

- **[J-Comm: Jacobian-Guided Latent Communication between LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32871)**  `arXiv:2610.32871`  
  _Xiangwei Wang, Wei Wang, Sachith Seneviratne, Ken Chen, Yu Xia, Peng Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Latent communication requires both an effective continuous reasoning process and an interface through which an external model can leverage its intermediate computation. We present J-Comm, a framework comprising two core components. First, a training-free Geometric Agreement for Latent Reasoning (GEAR) reweights token candidates over existing model embeddings, which monotonically optimizes geometric consensus among candidate tokens in embedding space at each step. Second, we introduce an H+J (Hidden-and-Jacobian) communication interface that gives the Receiver two complementary views of the Sender's reasoning. The H channel supplies continuous inputs derived from feedback distributions, while the J channel conveys intermediate-state cues in vocabulary-indexed Jacobian-lens coordinates. Intuitively, H provides inputs for the Receiver to reason from, while J offers additional guidance within its computation. The Receiver reconstructs these cues using its own dictionary and injects them into intermediate layers during prefill, enabling communication without sharing key-value caches or requiring identical model widths. In Qwen3-8B to Qwen3-4B communication, J-Comm achieves the highest mean score across seven benchmarks, including 94.80% on MATH500, 54.55% on GPQA-Diamond, 73.33% on AIME2024+2025, and 62.93% success on ALFWorld. Compared with TextMAS, it improves performance by 1.29-8.59 percentage points and reduces generated text tokens by 19.2-74.8%.
  </details>

- **[Communicating with the Entire Population: Machine Translation of Emergency Alerts and Warnings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32842)**  `arXiv:2610.32842`  
  _Jen Wilson, Vipasha Bansal, William D. Lewis_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  It is essential that we communicate with the entirety of an affected population during a crisis, including those who do not speak a region's dominant language(s). Commercial Translation tools offer a tempting solution to broaden access and can provide a lifeline when no other resources are available. However, Emergency response managers often operate in the dark regarding the quality and adequacy of these automated outputs. In this paper, we conduct a nationwide survey of emergency managers to understand their current workflows, perspectives, and operational challenges. We then evaluate commercial translation tools against these needs, shedding light on their utility and offering practical guidance on how and when these tools can be safely used in emergency response.
  </details>

- **[KrishokTech: A Bengali Agricultural Advisory System with Explicit Authorization Controls](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32817)**  `arXiv:2610.32817`  
  _Khan Raiyan Ibne Reza, Sanjana Aktar Maria, Shakil Ahmed, Faiyad Hossain, Sumaiya Tabassum Nimi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For agricultural advisory, generating an answer is only part of the problem: the system must also control unsafe requests, work with limited connectivity, and accommodate changing guidance. KRISHOKTECH is a Bengali agricultural advisory system built around an explicit authorization boundary around generation. Before retrieval, normalization and rule-based admission checks refer policy-covered high-risk requests and ask for a crop when treatment intent is underspecified. The system then binds retrieval to a single crop and asks for confirmation when the text and image indicate different crops. After generation, a narrow verifier removes dosage claims not supported by retrieved BARI/BRRI evidence. Agricultural guidance is kept in an external evidence index that can be updated without retraining the generator, while browser-side vision and local retrieval reduce connectivity requirements. The interface makes these decisions visible as ASK, CONFIRM, REFER, and DROP, together with their evidence. On 200 farmer queries and 400 stress queries, the controls halted underspecified requests and reduced cross-crop retrieval violations from 36.5% to 0% under the tested alias filter. Mismatch detection caught 453 of 454 text-image conflicts, while the verifier caught 114 of 118 inserted dosage errors. The demonstration shows how these controls govern the path from request to delivered advice.
  </details>

- **[Frontier-OPD: Discovering the Edge of Agent Capability and Teaching Agents to Go Beyond](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32802)**  `arXiv:2610.32802`  
  _Zheng Jiang, Yiming Chen, Houde Qian, Ling Li, Chaoyang Li, Lifeng Sun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation aligns teacher feedback with a student's current behavior, yet acquiring useful supervision for multi-turn agents requires identifying both where guidance is needed and whether the student can sustain the interaction afterward. We introduce Frontier-OPD, which addresses this problem through the student's learning frontier: decision situations that remain challenging but admit useful autonomous continuation after brief teacher guidance. To operationalize this frontier, Frontier-OPD organizes on-policy experience into a shared-state trajectory graph, where trajectory-balanced aggregation of teacher-student disagreement identifies distinct intervention candidates while preserving the histories needed for replay. Short teacher bridges are then assessed by comparing agreement over the bridge and the ensuing student continuation with an equally long segment of the original trajectory, providing a behavioral proxy for guidance that the current student can build on. Accepted teacher responses augment ordinary distillation data, and fresh rollouts renew the frontier estimate as the policy changes. Experiments on ALFWorld, WebShop, and ScienceWorld report higher mean autonomous performance than the compared distillation baselines across the evaluated student scales and teacher configurations, with ablations indicating complementary effects of candidate selection and guidance filtering. In one ALFWorld timing setting, the additional collection cost is offset by a shorter time to a target success rate.
  </details>

- **[ParaTaxe: a Suite of Tools for Processing and Publishing Multi-parallel Corpora](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32796)**  `arXiv:2610.32796`  
  _Olivier Kraif, Cyrille François, Elnaz Jalilian, Natacha Rimasson-Fertin, Pascale Roux_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Over the past fifteen years, digital tools for parallelizing and aligning textual corpora have developed significantly within the field of digital humanities. Several projects and software tools have been created to compare different translations or versions of texts. Despite these technical advances, many parallel corpora produced in academic contexts quickly become inaccessible online due to the lack of maintenance or funding. To address this gap, we present a suite of open-source tools developed within the ACR Grimm Tradalign and ParaTaxe projects. This suite includes the AIlign aligner, based on multilingual embeddings, a Python processing pipeline for converting and aligning corpora, a web interface called ParaTaxe for managing collections of parallel corpora, and a web interface for exploring aligned corpora built with TEI-Publisher.
  </details>

- **[On-Policy Self-Training: Data-Free Self-Improvement in Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32770)**  `arXiv:2610.32770`  
  _Leon Sick, Lukas Hoyer, Yannick Strümpler, Christian Reisswig, Timo Ropinski, Federico Tombari_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy self-distillation enables post-training foundation models without relying on stronger external teachers. However, existing approaches rely on ground-truth question-answer pairs, human-curated problem sets, or other supervised signals. In this work, we reformulate on-policy self-distillation as a self-training framework, driven by self-generated question-answer pairs and privileged reasoning demonstrations. We push supervision to the limit: guided solely by coarse, high-level topic descriptions, the model synthesizes its own curriculum of queries and privileged reasoning traces without access to any human-curated prompts or answers. We find that self-distillation on fully synthetic privileged information enables a model to improve across multiple domains while retaining its capabilities on domains it is not self-trained on. We demonstrate the generality of this framework across text and multimodal domains, achieving consistent self-improvement on standard language benchmarks and Visual Question Answering. Our findings demonstrate that foundation models possess rich latent capabilities that can be amplified without external task supervision or curated problem distributions.
  </details>

- **[Ontology-Guided Knowledge Graph Construction from Structured Metadata and Climate Science Publications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32725)**  `arXiv:2610.32725`  
  _Aayush Acharya, Qi Zhang, Eduard Dragut, Longin Jan Latecki_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid expansion of scientific literature far outpaces the speed of manual curation, creating a fundamental bottleneck in extracting structured knowledge from scientific texts. To address this, we introduce an agentic, ontology-guided framework for knowledge graph (KG) construction. Our framework utilizes specialized agents for document understanding, information extraction, ontology conformance, and entity linking. First, an LLM extracts factual knowledge in the form of triples with explicit textual evidence from research papers. Then the triplets undergo a multistage verification process to ground the triplets in the ontology. By enforcing schema constraints and requiring evidence in the form of text fragments, we ensure the data is both semantically consistent and traceable. In an agreement study over 1,000 extracted triplets, Gemini-2.5-flash achieves Cohen's $\kappa$ scores of 0.640--0.700 against three human annotators, matching the human--human agreement range of 0.616--0.689. We applied this framework to 2,000 scientific publications and construct a novel, ontology-grounded KG for climate science with 133,990 nodes and 755,308 relations; of the 23,327 triplets that the publications contribute after validation, 98.96\% are judged correct. The pipeline code and KG is publicly availablethis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Natural Language Autoencoders Identify Without Explaining](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32691)**  `arXiv:2610.32691`  
  _Kristina Šekrst, Barbara Geld_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Natural Language Autoencoders (NLAs) express a model's internal activations in the form of readable text. The verbalizer turns an activation into an English explanation; the reconstructor then converts that explanation back into an activation, and the explanation is considered successful if the round trip brings back the original activation. We claim that this criterion evaluates whether the information is sufficient and does not address descriptive faithfulness. A sentence can contain sufficient information to make it possible to identify an activation even if it wrongly explains what the activation does: in the case of reconstruction, the primary aim is to accurately identify the activations and not to describe them. This issue has the same structure as the chain-of-thought faithfulness problem, in which verbal output that has been trained to go with a process is taken as a report on that process, and it parallels the dissociation between an individual's introspective report and the actual governing process as documented in human psychology. Although proposed as interpretability tools for the purpose of alignment, NLAs reintroduce, through unverified explanations, the epistemic problem that they were intended to resolve.
  </details>

- **[When is more search useful? An Empirical Study of Search Utility in Quality-Aware Decoding for Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32679)**  `arXiv:2610.32679`  
  _Javier Garcia Gilabert, Rico Sennrich, André F. T. Martins_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quality-Aware Decoding (QAD) improves machine translation through test-time scaling, generating multiple candidate translations and selecting the final output with a quality-aware decoder. Usually the same candidate budget N is spent on every source sentence, assuming that all inputs require the same amount of search. We show that this assumption does not hold and that the quality gain obtained from additional candidates varies across sentences. We study search utility through three quantities: total scaling gain, how much quality a sentence can gain from a much larger candidate pool; marginal scaling gain, the expected gain from generating one additional batch of candidates; and the sampling saturation point, the candidate budget required to realize most of the total scaling gain. We investigate two sources of information for predicting these quantities: signals available before candidate-set expansion, and statistics computed from the candidate pool accumulated during search. We find that sentence-level signals correlate with total scaling gain but are less informative about the sampling saturation point. In contrast, properties of the current candidate pool provide useful signals of marginal scaling gain. Finally, we show that these signals can identify sentences that obtain larger gains when additional search is selectively applied.
  </details>

- **[Residual Expert Routing for Text-Only Domain Adaptation of Factorized Transducer ASR](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32637)**  `arXiv:2610.32637`  
  _Shashi Kumar, Esaú Villatoro-Tello, Thibault Bañeras-Roux, Sergio Burdisso, Petr Motlicek, Kadri Hacioglu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Fine-tuning the non-blank predictor of a factorized transducer on target-domain text improves recognition on that domain but degrades performance on the source domain. A single interpolation weight between base and adapted predictors cannot resolve this: the adapted predictor helps at some prefixes and hurts at others, often within one utterance. We propose conservative residual routing: the adapted predictor becomes a residual correction to the base, and a 16k-parameter gate decides at each prefix how much of it to admit. Closing the gate reproduces the base exactly. On a 40-hour conversational corpus, routing retains essentially all of the text-only gain on two held-out domains (19.89 to 16.74, 20.91 to 16.21 WER) while leaving source-domain WER unchanged (12.90 to 12.93); the adapted predictor alone costs 1.08 WER. Routing needs no per-domain tuning on target-domain audio. Analysis reveals the gate does not behave as a simple domain-level switch: it modulates intervention through the disagreement between the two predictors, suppressing it in-domain and amplifying it out-of-domain.
  </details>

- **[PHYRD: A Living Benchmark for Reproducing and Extending Theoretical Physics Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32575)**  `arXiv:2610.32575`  
  _Qituan Zhang, Guo Chen, Linfeng Zhang, Wenlong Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models and agents have shown significant power in advanced physical research. In this paper, We introduce PHYRD, a living benchmark for theoretical-physics reasoning with 63 Reproduce and 43 Discover problems. Reproduce tests derivation of published results; Discover tests consequences of modified theoretical conditions. FormulaForest constructs the tasks with reference solutions and subquestion-level rubrics. Across five models, whole-problem accuracy ranges from 49.21% to 68.25% on Reproduce and from 18.60% to 74.42% on closed-book Discover. With tools, four models complete more Discover problems, but conceptual errors and missing arguments persist. Besides, our experimental results demonstrate the value of FormulaForest as synthetic data. On FrontierScience, a benchmark developed by OpenAI, it raises the accuracy of a Qwen-35B-A3B model from 61% to 72%.
  </details>

- **[The Punjabi Geet Toxicity Dataset (PGT-FG): Fine-Grained Multimodal Annotations for Toxic Content in Punjabi Songs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32513)**  `arXiv:2610.32513`  
  _Lakhjeet Kaur, Jaspreet Kaur, Komal Rani, Yougal Attri, Ramakant KumarJaydeep Kishore, Pravin Kumar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Resources for toxicity detection in Punjabi remain limited, particularly for musical content that combines linguistic and multimodal information. We present the Punjabi Geet Toxicity Dataset (PGT-FG), a segment-level resource developed to support research on toxicity detection in Punjabi musical content. The dataset was constructed from publicly available Punjabi songs collected from YouTube. An initial collection of 620 songs resulted in 465 successfully downloaded songs and 3,402 preliminary segments from 450 songs. After quality control and annotation, the final dataset contains 2,396 unique segments from 392 songs. Four native Punjabi annotators produced 2,870 annotation records, including a shared subset of 158 segments annotated by all four. Each annotation includes binary toxicity, eight fine-grained toxicity categories, severity, sentiment, mode, and an uncertainty flag. Inter-annotator agreement was measured with Fleiss' kappa on the shared subset, and majority-based resolution produced the final segment-level labels. Of the 2,396 segments, 705 (29.42%) were labelled toxic and 1,691 (70.58%) non-toxic. Violence was the most frequent category, occurring in 539 toxic segments (76.45%), followed by gang references (227, 32.20%) and drug references (133, 18.87%). The overall toxicity label achieved substantial agreement (kappa = 0.6521). PGT-FG provides a fine-grained resource for Punjabi toxicity detection and multimodal content analysis. The resolved dataset, with one final record per segment, is publicly available on Zenodo (this https URL). Original lyrics, lyric excerpts, and copyrighted audio or video content are not redistributed.
  </details>

- **[Reading Between the Layers: Cross-Modal Alignment and Representational Hierarchy in Vision Transformers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32463)**  `arXiv:2610.32463`  
  _Sugam Karki, Raúl Vázquez, Yova Kementchedjhieva_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  While large vision-language models excel at optical character recognition, it remains unclear how their underlying vision encoders process text and its meaning, and whether they support anything resembling reading comprehension. We systematically study transformer-based vision encoders, using cross-modal alignment between rendered text and visual concepts together with probing and retrieval-based analyses as a controlled window into these broader capabilities. Our layer-wise analyses span orthographic, grammatical, and semantic organization, and reveal that strong cross-modal mapping emerges only in the deepest layers, after unimodal object and text recognition have already developed, coinciding with a progression from surface-level to semantic representations. Although this hierarchy is strongest in contrastively trained models, larger self-supervised models and text-rich training data partially support similar behaviors without explicit language supervision. Together, our results suggest that increasing model and training-data scale, as well as text-rich training data, can partially compensate for the absence of language supervision.
  </details>

- **[Decoupling Patient Personas and Clinical Workflows: A Dual-Agent Approach to Realistic Medical Dialogue Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32443)**  `arXiv:2610.32443`  
  _Qiyuan Li, Xiaomei Guo, Chao Gao, Haijiang Liu, Deyu Chen, Caicai Guo, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Pre-consultation medical dialogues are essential for collecting comprehensive patient information, but are costly to obtain at scale due to privacy and ethical constraints. Existing LLM-based synthesis methods often rely on prompt engineering or rigid rules, limiting their ability to balance clinical relevance with realistic patient diversity. We propose CoRPS-Dial, a controllable dual-agent framework for pre-consultation medical dialogue synthesis that integrates patient personas with guideline-aligned clinical workflow rules. CoRPS-Dial generates dialogues through interactions between a persona-driven patient agent and a process-guided doctor agent, followed by post-hoc quality control for role consistency, workflow completeness, and textual consistency. Experiments on coronary heart disease demonstrate that CoRPS-Dial improves alignment between synthetic and real dialogues in information coverage, clinical compliance, and dialogue naturalness. Downstream fine-tuning experiments further confirm the utility of the generated synthetic data for medical dialogue tasks. These results highlight the effectiveness of CoRPS-Dial for clinically grounded and controllable medical dialogue synthesis.
  </details>

- **[SCI-AGENT-EXAM: Benchmarking Agents on Scientific Research Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32441)**  `arXiv:2610.32441`  
  _Tianyun Zhong, Wei Wang, Jialin Li, Jianhong Tu, Liyao Li, Chuqiao Kuang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents are increasingly capable of supporting scientific workflows, but their capabilities across diverse research activities remain difficult to assess systematically. Existing benchmarks often focus on selected scientific capabilities or task types, limiting a comprehensive characterization of scientific agents' strengths and weaknesses in realistic research workflows. We introduce SCI-AGENT-EXAM, a workflow-oriented benchmark comprising 120 expert-curated tasks across 11 scientific fields and 7 research task types defined by the Scientific Workflow Task Taxonomy (SWTT). Each task requires both executable artifacts (e.g., code and scripts) and textual artifacts (e.g., reports and explanations). To evaluate the heterogeneous task outputs, we develop a hybrid evaluation protocol combining deterministic programmatic verification for executable artifacts with a calibrated, expert-validated LLM-based judge for textual artifacts. Beyond outcome assessment, we analyze progress and solution stability along execution trajectories to characterize how agents reach their final results. Experiments with 10 frontier LLM agents reveal substantial performance gaps: GPT-6 Astra achieves the highest overall score of 80.9, yet only 26.0% of its runs exceed the 0.95 success threshold. Further analyses uncover distinct capability profiles across scientific task types and substantial variation in execution efficiency and stability. Our code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Profile-Driven User Simulation via Reinforcement Learning with Hierarchical Rewards for Task-Oriented Dialogues](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32435)**  `arXiv:2610.32435`  
  _Juan Li, Fangshu Chang, Xinyi Jiang, Ling Huang, Yunzhang Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Building Task-Oriented Dialogue (TOD) systems for high-value scenarios such as financial marketing requires large volumes of domain-specific dialogue data, which privacy regulations, annotation costs, and the need for expert labor make hard to obtain. User simulation offers a remedy, but existing methods target general-domain dialogue and exhibit two key limitations: insufficient modeling of domain-specific business processes and compliance constraints, and inadequate capture of the colloquial, spoken-language character of voice interactions. We propose ProfileSim, a profile-driven user simulation framework for financial marketing outbound calls. It jointly models user profiles---demographics and Big Five personality traits---and business profiles---intents reverse-engineered from real call logs---enabling fine-grained control over simulated behavior. The base model is fine-tuned on real voice dialogue data and refined by Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) with hierarchical rewards at the dialogue and utterance levels. Paired with a customer service agent integrating Standard Operating Procedure (SOP) constraints and Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG), ProfileSim achieves the highest colloquial naturalness, the strongest user profile adherence and the lowest agent score on long-tail intents among all evaluated user generators, while producing substantially shorter user utterances at comparable business compliance. The generated dialogue data is designed to support intent recognition training, agent evaluation, and rapid cold-start deployment.
  </details>

- **[Can LLM Annotators Match Human Consistency? Benchmarking Human Agreement and Optimizing Multi-Agent with Contrastive Knowledge](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32415)**  `arXiv:2610.32415`  
  _Xiaopeng Ke, Zhixin Wang, Xuebo Liu, Xinyu Ma, Xing Zheng, Bowen Song, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LLM-based annotation has advanced rapidly, yet existing benchmarks remain misaligned with real-world practice. Actual annotation often involves lengthy, task-specific guidelines, and different annotators may produce divergent but reasonable judgments, especially on boundary cases, where single-labeled dataset fail to reflect this disagreement. This gap also limits existing single-agent and workflow-based annotation systems, which often lack adaptability to complex, evolving guidelines. To bridge this gap, we introduce ANT-A Benchmark, a real-world multi-annotator benchmark spanning 7 datasets across domains and modalities, with task-specific guidelines and 5 independent human labels per instance. ANT-A enables a distribution-aware evaluation, using Fleiss kappa to assess how closely model annotations align with human labeling distributions. We further propose ANT-OR, a multi-agent annotation framework that refines prompts by distilling contrastive decision rules from positive and negative feedback. Experiments show that ANT-OR improves agreement@>=2 accuracy by 4.5 points over the strongest baseline and achieves the highest alignment with human annotator distributions as measured by Fleiss kappa within 2 points. Code and benchmark are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[A Broader Look into MLLM's Retrieval Heads](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32411)**  `arXiv:2610.32411`  
  _Jia Wang, Yuqing Li, Jiangnan Li, Zheng Lin, Mo Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Locating relevant evidence in long inputs is a core capability of Multimodal Large Language Models. Prior studies attribute this capability to retrieval heads, a sparse set of attention heads that transfer information from the context to the output. However, these analyses are confined to a limited set of modalities and the corresponding models. Moreover, how retrieval heads can be leveraged to enhance the model calls for further exploration. In this paper, we ask whether this mechanism expands together with the input modalities of the model. Taking audio-visual input as a newly added modality, we construct paired inputs in which the same answer is either spoken or shown on screen. With the evidence content fixed, retrieval heads remain sparse in the new modality and are shared to a large extent across modalities. Across 18 language, vision-language, and omni-modal checkpoints, omni-modal models largely preserve the retrieval heads of their backbones. Ablating these heads eliminates nearly all audio-visual retrieval. The heads shared across modalities make up fewer than 2\% of all heads, yet ablating them also degrades document understanding, spatial perception, and visual mathematics. Building on these findings, we turn retrieval heads into a target of optimization by training their attention with a ranking objective. This raises the page Recall@1 of Qwen3-VL-8B on MMDocIR by 6.03 points and improves document retrieval across three model families. Functional attention heads can thus serve both as a lens on multimodal models and as a target for improving them.
  </details>

- **[Acoustic and Lexical Signals for Native Language Identification of Language Learners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32382)**  `arXiv:2610.32382`  
  _Ahmet Yavuz Uluslu, Mengjie Qian, Kate Knill, Mark Gales_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Spoken native language (L1) identification for second language learners has traditionally relied on acoustic attributes of speech. Comparatively little attention has been paid to analysing speech transcripts for L1 traces. In this paper, we present a study of L1 identification using both speech and corresponding transcripts from a spoken learner English corpus. We compare a WavLM-based audio L1 classifier with a zero-shot GPT-4o classifier operating on transcripts and analyse their predictions across proficiency levels using a CEFR-balanced evaluation set. Our experiments show that the audio classifier outperforms the transcript-based approach overall when full transcripts are available, although their relative strengths vary with learner proficiency. Audio performs better at A1, whereas text performs better from A2 to C1 with full transcripts. Transcript-based accuracy generally increases with proficiency, while acoustic performance remains comparatively stable. Restricting transcript length substantially reduces text-based accuracy, whereas audio is less sensitive to input length and performs relatively well with limited input. These findings provide insights into L1 identification from acoustic and textual representations of learner speech and how performance varies with proficiency and the amount of available language material.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Final Outcomes: Quantifying Claim-Level Information Dynamics in Multi-Agent LLM Interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32333)**  `arXiv:2610.32333`  
  _Haocheng Jiang, Herun Wan, Yongyuan Peng, Minnan Luo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When LLM-powered agents interact, information is repeatedly shared, expanded, and forgotten, yet multi-agent evaluation typically focuses on final task outcomes. We study such interaction as claim-level information dynamics and propose RIVER, which decomposes agent outputs into atomic claims and evaluates three complementary dimensions: convergence, exploration, and retention. Convergence captures cross-agent claim correspondence and stance alignment; exploration captures semantic breadth and extension beyond the source; and retention tracks source preservation and the persistence of new claims across stages and lineages. We evaluate five interaction strategies on 40 controlled news-generation cases. Repeatedly pooled interaction promotes convergence but narrows the information space, whereas distributed relay yields broader, less shared information. Further comparisons show that method-specific constraints increase exploration while weakening convergence and retention. Our findings suggest that collective information dynamics are better explained by underlying information-flow mechanisms than by simple downstream performance.
  </details>

- **[Many Perturbations, Shared Failure Dynamics: Tracing Direction-Sensitive Perturbation Influence in Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32309)**  `arXiv:2610.32309`  
  _Zhibo Hu, Chen Wang, Yanfeng Shu, Liming Dong, Hye-young Paik, Liming Zhu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Why can a task-preserving rewrite turn a correct answer into a failure while a larger internal change leaves the answer intact? We develop a direction-sensitive, readout-relative account of semantic fragility: heterogeneous language changes can share geometric and readout relationships without sharing an input-independent failure direction. Clean decision reserve, representation transformation, and response alignment provide complementary descriptions of this process. An exact longitudinal-transverse decomposition identifies directional information discarded by scalar transmission. A compact reserve-and-transmission model predicts failures across held-out families and items in Qwen3-32B and Gemma 4 31B, with useful but parameterization-dependent transfer to ReClor. Matched interventions show that orientation carries causal information beyond scalar geometry. Among naturally failed rewrites, attention-component tests on disjoint original items and subsequent answer-order controls favor the natural paired direction over tested random and same-answer-letter donor directions. Yet failures rarely persist across option orders, and current-order corrections use recomputed clean references. Shared predictive regularity thus coexists with context-dependent directional effects. Numerical path integration reduces, but does not eliminate, finite-patch approximation error within a fixed context; a larger discrepancy from the natural rewrite effect remains. A complementary fixed-CoT cue study extends this account to influence unacknowledged in recorded reasoning: readout associations transfer across domains, although the positive-mean causal criterion fails and signed-effect prediction remains post hoc. Together, these results support shared relations among reserve, transformation, and response while distinguishing local causal sensitivity from a complete explanation of natural failure.
  </details>

- **["Not" is Not Lost in Translation: A Causal Analysis of Negation Representations in Multilingual LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32281)**  `arXiv:2610.32281`  
  _Giang Son Nguyen, Wray Buntine, Dung D. Le, Wenya Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Negation occurs in every language, yet its surface realization differs drastically. Despite this, multilingual large language models (LLMs) translate negation robustly, raising the question of whether they share an underlying representation of negation across languages. We study this question in six open models across English, French, Vietnamese, and Chinese, using causal interventions on a synthetic contrastive dataset and a negation translation dataset derived from FLORES-200. Across models, we find strong evidence that negation representations are causally controllable and largely shared across languages. In particular, we show that a contrastive activation direction extracted from one language can induce or remove negation in the others, and directions extracted from different languages are substantially aligned. We further find that negation is represented in sparse features: a single sparse autoencoder (SAE) feature per model suffices to control polarity across languages, while responding selectively to negated inputs. Finally, both dense and sparse interventions generalize to four additional languages and FLORES-derived sentences. We release data and code to support future work:this https URL.
  </details>

- **[Do Halting Heads Really Save Compute in Looped Language Models?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32246)**  `arXiv:2610.32246`  
  _Tsung-Han Wu, Minsuk Heo, Kenny Tan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Looped language models run each token through the same layers several times, and each run is one iteration. To save compute, an extra layer, the halting head, gives after each iteration the probability that the token stops there, so that easy tokens can stop early. Pretraining models from scratch, up to 1.3B parameters, we find two problems. First, heads trained on a mix of all iterations predict that tokens need under half the iterations, yet under the standard stopping rule tokens run almost every iteration, because this training never scores the single iteration where a token stops. Scoring every iteration instead fixes this, but in our runs it works only with a strong penalty favoring early stops. Second, savings are commonly reported as an average number of iterations per token, but when sequences are generated in batches that run until their slowest token stops, a model without a head is almost as good at that cost. The reported average can therefore overstate the saving. We recommend reporting the iterations tokens actually run, with the stopping threshold and batch size, and give a test, run before training a head, that bounds its gain.
  </details>

- **[SOPO: Socratic Guided Policy Optimization for Span-Level Hallucination Detection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32212)**  `arXiv:2610.32212`  
  _Jiawei Dong, Zilong Bai, Pengtian zhu, Peng Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large Language Models (LLMs) have gained widespread adoption in various natural language processing tasks, but suffer from hallucination issues where they generate unfaithful or inconsistent content. While Reinforcement Learning with Verifiable Rewards (RLVR) has shown promising applications in reasoning tasks, its application to span-level hallucination detection is challenged by localization ambiguity, reward sparsity on hard examples, and uncontrolled length inflation. To address these issues, we introduce Socratic Guided Policy Optimization (SOPO), a multi-turn RL framework for robust hallucination detection. The key innovation of SOPO is a Socratic feedback mechanism, where a teacher model guides the reasoning trajectories during rollout by providing indirect hints inferred from the ground truth. This teacher-guided approach enables the model to generate more effective reasoning trajectories for hard examples, thus alleviating reward sparsity. Furthermore, we incorporate Turn-based Advantage Scaling to penalize verbosity bias, encouraging the model to develop efficient and autonomous reasoning capabilities. Empirical results on RAGTruth demonstrate that SOPO (4B) achieves state-of-the-art performance, surpassing leading proprietary models (e.g., GPT-5) and standard RL baselines, while obtaining higher reported mean scores than GRPO on four out-of-domain benchmarks, with mixed evidence against CAPO. We release our training and evaluation code alongside the 1.7B and 4B SOPO models atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Collection-Date Assignment Redirects Answers in Temporal QA](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32191)**  `arXiv:2610.32191`  
  _Minsu Oh, Seunghui Jwa, Soo Kyun Kim, Yeo-Chan Yoon_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A collection date records when a source revision was acquired, not when its contents changed. Can assigning this metadata redirect temporal question answering despite unchanged evidence? Swapping the question day and following day between two revisions lowers later-reference exact match (EM) by 37.6-51.4 percentage points across five open-weight LLMs on 595 Wikipedia pages, replicated on 128 disjoint pages. Previous-day swaps retain 29.0-39.6-point losses on 497 pages. Seven prompt variants, including explicit ignore-collection instructions and archived-revision provenance, retain the effect. Fictional answers and English month-name question dates show that remembered answers and identical date strings are unnecessary. Matched thinking reduces Qwen3.5's penalty from 30.1 to 11.1 points, while native reasoners vary. Two API LLMs show small Wikipedia swap effects and rarely shift to earlier answers on fictional records. Large swap-induced losses concentrate around the question date; swaps between distant dates have much smaller effects. Adding fields containing one observed acquisition date lowers EM relative to omission in three of five LLMs after correction. The diagnostic isolates metadata effects from changing evidence or missing the relevant revision, and provides a practical audit for temporal QA inputs.
  </details>

- **[The LLENS Platform for On-Premises Large Language Model Agents in a Japanese University Hospital: Development and Evaluation Study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32151)**  `arXiv:2610.32151`  
  _Ken Enda, Jien Saito, Masami Mukai, Feng Han, Emir Turkes, Tatsumi Kusakabe, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model agents that use tools could take on part of the electronic health record and desk work that occupies physicians' time, but in many hospitals the medical information systems run inside closed networks, so the model and the agent harness must also sit inside. We designed, implemented, and deployed LLENS, a clinical support AI agent platform that runs inside the closed network of Hokkaido University Hospital on one host with 8 NVIDIA H200 GPUs, the open-weight model Kimi K2.6 (~1 trillion parameters), and a web application rebuilt from Open WebUI. The harness has 10 components, including page image reading, full-text attachment input, a path-addressed document tree without a vector index, a network-isolated sandbox, artifact return, self-checking of generated output, subagents, and saved procedures. Kimi K2.6 was chosen from 3 open-weight candidates and scored 480/500 on the 2026 Japanese Medical Licensing Examination; it generated 119 tokens/s for one user and served 16 concurrent requests at 908 tokens/s in total. On 180 questions over hospital documents (3 seeds each), mean accuracy was 10.7% with no documents, 81.3% with fixed retrieval (top 10 chunks of one lexical search), and 97.2% with interactive access, in which the model reads with document tools and the sandbox (15.9 percentage points above fixed retrieval, 95% CI 10.9-21.3); the difference lay in filtering/counting questions (16.7% vs 87.8%). Five scenarios with synthetic data ran on combinations of the components: needlestick injury response, batch adverse event grading, scanned referral letter transcription, falls data aggregation and plotting, and investigation of 3 topics by subagents. The platform has run on the hospital network since September 14, 2026.
  </details>

- **[From Promises to Accountability: Comparative AI Modeling and Visualization of Electoral Commitments in Nepali Political Texts](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32150)**  `arXiv:2610.32150`  
  _Prakash Poudyal, Nischal Karki, Amrit Dahal, Rajani Chulyadyo, Sameer Tamrakar, Bal Krishna Bal_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Election Commitment Mining refers to the analysis of election commitments made by electoral candidates or political parties during election campaigns. However, many elected candidates and political parties, especially in developing South Asian countries, do not fully deliver on the promises they make before elections. Even after the elections, commitments presented through election manifestos, posters, pamphlets, videos, and other campaign materials are often not systematically or transparently archived. As a result, these promises tend to fade away over time, making it difficult to hold the elected candidates accountable for their commitments. To address this persistent issue, this research aims to develop a system capable of structuring the commitments made by candidates during election periods. In this work, we created the first gold-standard dataset of Nepali political texts (Cohen's kappa = 0.777) and evaluated machine learning, deep learning and large language models to automatically extract and analyze election commitments. LaBSE-LR achieved the best results among machine learning models (0.897 accuracy, 0.896 macro F1-score, 0.947 AUROC), while fastText-LSTM achieved the highest performance among deep learning models (0.914 accuracy, 0.913 macro F1-score, 0.961 AUROC). Among the three evaluated large language models, Qwen3-8B performed best in the few-shot setting (0.848 macro F1-score). The outcome of this work is integrated into a mobile application, where commitments can be visualized with the support of GAI, enhancing transparency, civic engagement, and electoral accountability.
  </details>

- **[Disentangling Question, Response, and Verifier Languages in Multilingual P(True) Self-Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32144)**  `arXiv:2610.32144`  
  _Jewon Yeom, Jaewon Sok, Seonghyeon Park, Jeongjae Park, Hwiyeong Lee, Taesup Kim_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a language model is asked whether its own answer is correct, three languages are involved: that of the question, that requested for the answer, and that of the verifier instruction asking for the judgment. Multilingual confidence evaluations seldom separate the three. We vary them independently in a six-language factorial design with ten open-weight models (3B-14B) on LSQA, MMMLU and TriviaQA, holding each answer fixed across verifier languages and measuring confidence with a first-token P(True) probe. The three languages move different parts of the evaluation: which of them governs accuracy depends on the benchmark, matching the response language to the question or verifier language sharpens discrimination but not calibration, and the verifier language alone shifts calibration error by up to 0.04 without changing accuracy. The scorer matters as well: an unvalidated judge would have shrunk the MMMLU English-Hindi accuracy gap from 0.198 to 0.038. Multilingual confidence results, model rankings included, are therefore comparable only when all three languages are reported and the scorer is validated.
  </details>

- **[From Noisy Retrieval to Decision Evidence: Silver Evidence Demonstrations for In-Context Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32128)**  `arXiv:2610.32128`  
  _Masoud Monajatipoor, Fazlolah Mohaghegh, Hamid Reza Hassanzadeh, Akash Uttamrao Bhoite, Robert E. Tillman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  RAG and retrieval-augmented few-shot ICL can struggle when decision-relevant evidence is buried in long, noisy context. Retrieved passages and demonstrations may contain redundant or distracting information. Although expert-labeled development examples may identify the text supporting each decision, these annotations are typically used only as answer-side rationales rather than as supervision for filtering noisy retrieval. We propose silver-evidence demonstrations, which replace raw retrieved passages in ICL examples with generated, target-conditioned evidence summaries. For each labeled development example, an LLM generates silver evidence without access to the expert label or gold evidence; this evidence is then paired with the expert label and gold evidence where available. At test time, a single structured call generates silver evidence from retrieved passages and then predicts the final label and decision evidence. Across EI 2.0, SciFact, synthetic policy retrieval, and HICRIC/IMR data, our full method improves over equally sized gold-only ICL (label-only for policy) on three of four datasets and improves evidence F1 on all annotated datasets. It also achieves higher mean label accuracy than InstructRAG-style ICL on all four datasets. These results suggest that replacing demonstration-side retrieved passages with compact, target-conditioned silver evidence is particularly useful for decisions based on long and noisy text, where it reduces distracting context while preserving decision-relevant signals and expert supervision.
  </details>

- **[Semantically Rich, Metrically Affect-Blind: A Geometric Blind Spot in Modern Text Embedders](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32122)**  `arXiv:2610.32122`  
  _Mahdi Naser Moghadasi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We report a systematic dissociation in modern text embedders: sentiment is almost perfectly decodable from their embeddings, yet almost entirely absent from their metric. Across eleven frozen encoders spanning 2021 to 2026, including 2026-generation models and the 4B-parameter LLM embedder Qwen3-Embedding-4B, a linear probe recovers sentence polarity at up to 91.3% accuracy while cosine similarity separates polarity by a margin of only 0.003-0.064. Qwen3-Embedding-4B, the strongest probe in the panel, reaches 91.3% probe accuracy but only 64.3 retrieval MAP. Because retrieval, clustering, deduplication, and retrieval-augmented generation operate on the metric rather than on a trained probe, these systems are effectively blind to information their encoder demonstrably contains. A task-conditioning control suggests this reflects the pretraining objective rather than a prompting artifact: conditioning Jina-v5 on its classification task rather than its retrieval task recovers only about half of the MAP gain that injection achieves on the same weights (63.2 to 73.8, versus 85.1), and leaves the alignment gap essentially unchanged. Sentiment-Injected Sentence Embeddings (SISE) train a small (0.13M-0.69M-parameter) supervised-contrastive head on a frozen encoder in seconds on a CPU, using the labelled training set. The head improves every encoder released since 2022 on full-data probe accuracy (up to +5.8 points), retrieval MAP (up to +26.0) and polarity margin (7-61x). This is a specialization, not a free gain: the compact 128-d head loses 38-41 Spearman points on STS-B, while a fused variant that concatenates it with the base embedding loses 2.4-12.0. With the head, a 0.6B encoder at 128 dimensions matches a 4B encoder given the same head (91.8 vs. 90.3 MAP). We release a CPU-reproducible pipeline and a reusable diagnostic, the Attribute Alignment Gap.
  </details>

- **[EDWARD-NMT: Elastic Dimensions With Adaptive Routing and Dual-consistency for Neural Machine Translation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32115)**  `arXiv:2610.32115`  
  _Edward Nguyen, Thai Tran, Kiet Anh Hoang, Duy Hoang, Duy Vo, Khanh Le, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Sparse Mixture-of-Experts (MoE) is a powerful paradigm for scaling Neural Machine Translation (NMT) and managing language heterogeneity. However, practical limitations hinder its deployment on resource-constrained devices: the entire expert pool must remain in memory, and the per-token compute budget is statically fixed during training by rigid top-k routing. We introduce EDWARD-NMT (Elastic Dimensions With Adaptive Routing and Dual-consistency for NMT), a novel MoE encoder-decoder architecture designed for a train once, deploy anywhere paradigm that exposes a family of deployable sub-networks at inference time. EDWARD-NMT integrates three key innovations: (i) Matryoshka-style elastic dimensions to flexibly reduce the memory footprint; (ii) an adaptive sigmoid router with a learned, task-conditioned threshold, allowing each token to dynamically determine its active expert count; and (iii) a dual-consistency objective to maximize the performance of the extracted sub-networks. Extensive experiments on PhoMT (En-Vi), WMT-14 (En-De), multilingual OPUS-16, and a multi-domain De-En benchmark validate our approach. The full-capacity architecture matches or exceeds strong MoE baselines with improved parameter efficiency. Furthermore, the sub-networks seamlessly extracted from a single checkpoint consistently outperform comparably sized dense Transformers without any retraining.
  </details>

- **[Context or Knowledge: How LLMs Attribute Their Own Answers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32109)**  `arXiv:2610.32109`  
  _Kirill Afendulev, Alexey Dontsov, Alla Chepurova, Elena Tutubalina, Anton Korznikov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Language models can answer a question from a supplied document or from their parametric knowledge. When later asked where the answer came from, what determines their source report? We study this by intervening on the model's key-value (KV) cache after the answer is generated and before the source question is asked. On a controlled factual task with four models (7B-32B), changing or removing the document's cache never changes the report. In contrast, transplanting the question-and-answer (Q+A) cache between runs with identical question and answer tokens transfers it: every eligible report follows the donor, while the recipient's document is unchanged. On the two smaller models, steering Q+A values along directions fitted before any source question reverses 98.9-100% of reports across four document conditions at a fixed strength, while norm-matched random directions flip at most 1.1%. The components of keys and values along fitted directions carry the transplant effect: copying them alone nearly reproduces the report change; holding them fixed nearly prevents it.
