Applied during answer generation, the same value directions also change which source supplies the answer, though more weakly and depending on the prompt. Source reporting in this setting thus depends causally on cached state produced while answering. We do not establish general reporting accuracy or a complete interpretation of this state.
  </details>

- **[Beyond What to Say: Proactive Utterance Timing with Inner Thoughts for Werewolf Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32107)**  `arXiv:2610.32107`  
  _Yoshiki Tanaka, Shuya Uchiyama, Daichi Hamaguchi, Michimasa Inaba_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Participating in a conversation involves not only deciding what to say but also whether to speak at all. This judgment carries even greater significance in environments like the Werewolf game, where participants' interests conflict, because both utterances and silence are subject to interpretation and assessment by others. Therefore, determining when to speak, as well as what to say, is crucial for an LLM agent playing the Werewolf game. In this study, we introduce a Werewolf AI agent developed for the Anytime Utterance Track of the AIWolfDial shared task. Our system builds on the existing Inner Thoughts framework, which determines utterance timing during conversations by generating internal thoughts and evaluating the value of each thought for expression at that moment. Since existing methods were designed for general dialogue, we have redesigned the system specifically for the competitive environment and incomplete information setting of Werewolf games. We call our adapted method Adversarial Inner Thoughts. Through experiments involving analysis of self-play logs and human evaluations, we analyze and discuss the effectiveness of our approach.
  </details>

- **[How Much Do You Trust A Dog? Quantifying Hint-Following in Reasoning Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32055)**  `arXiv:2610.32055`  
  _Anxin Guo, Chirag Pabbaraju, Sara Ahmadian, Kamesh Munagala, Aravindan Vijayaraghavan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) often exhibit sycophancy, altering their answers to align with user suggestions even on objective factual and reasoning tasks. Yet the quantitative form of this influence--- and whether it is calibrated to source reliability--- remains poorly understood. In this work, we investigate this behavior through hint-induced answer shifts on MMLU questions, evaluating prompts of the form ``[Source] thinks the answer is (X)''. We systematically vary the source to which the hint is attributed (a professor, a friend, or a dog) across three open-source model families, Qwen, Gemma, and SmolLM. We show that the change induced by the hint follows a predictable structure well-characterized by an exponential tilt: the hint induces an approximately constant mean shift in the hinted answer's log-odds, with question--target susceptibility largely shared across sources. To explore how this pattern can arise, we introduce a formal decomposition of the hint response and illustrate in controlled synthetic experiments how KL-regularized reward-based training reshapes both hinted and unhinted answer distributions. Finally, we evaluate whether this shift is calibrated to source reliability, finding that the log-odds shift persists even when the hint-giver is declared inaccurate or accompanied by explicit warnings not to trust the source, highlighting the vulnerability of LLMs to adversarial steering.
  </details>

- **[DraMaze: A Multi-Agent Pipeline with Future-Aware Memory for Diverse Microdrama Script Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32002)**  `arXiv:2610.32002`  
  _Siyang He, Qiqi Wang, Fan Liu, Yuncheng Huang, Pengfei Xu, Xi Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The rapid growth of microdramas has increased demand for high-quality script production. Long-form microdrama script generation with LLMs requires both diverse plot progression and long-range narrative coherence. Our observations reveal that candidate continuations increasingly concentrate on fewer narrative mechanisms as generation progresses, while conventional retrieval may reinforce trope reuse and overlook historical information essential to future plots. To address these challenges, we propose DraMaze, a multi-agent pipeline that explores diverse causal paths through trope memory-guided evolutionary tree search and mutation. It further constructs a Backward Narrative Dependency Tree to organize, retain, and retrieve historical memories based on future narrative goals. We also introduce MiDramaBench, a bilingual benchmark for evaluating script logic and creative quality across different script lengths, together with causal-pattern metrics for narrative repetition and diversity. Experiments show consistent improvements across three backbone LLMs. For 60-episode scripts of up to 60k words, DraMaze reduces causal-pattern repetition by 32.1% and achieves a Logic score of 82.5, outperforming the best-performing baseline by 8.5 points.
  </details>

- **[POS-Signature Demonstration Retrieval for In-Context Dependency Parsing](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31966)**  `arXiv:2610.31966`  
  _Muhammad Azam Afridi, Rajab Ali, Marco Passarotti_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Demonstrations for in-context learning are often retrieved by semantic similarity, which may be a poor proxy for relevance in structured prediction. We study demonstration selection for Universal Dependencies parsing with a POS-signature policy that represents sentences with weighted part-of-speech statistics and retrieves three length-compatible, gold-parsed examples from the labeled training split. We evaluate two open-weight language models without fine-tuning on all 2,077 sentences of the UD English Web Treebank test set, comparing the policy with shot- and format-matched fixed and semantic three-shot baselines, as well as zero-shot, chain-of-thought, and critique-refinement systems. POS-signature retrieval yields the highest labeled attachment score among the tested prompting systems for both models (72.4 and 73.2), outperforming semantic retrieval by 1.9 and 2.7 points; paired-bootstrap 95% confidence intervals exclude zero for both contrasts. Chain-of-thought and refinement comparisons vary across models. These exploratory results favor the tested POS-signature retrieval approach on English-EWT. We release the prompts, archived outputs, and evaluation code.
  </details>

- **[Which Answer Did the Seventeen-Year-Old Write? A Behavioral Case Study of Author-Age Misattribution, Miscalibrated Confidence, and Non-Corrective Self-Explanation in Six Frontier Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31946)**  `arXiv:2610.31946`  
  _Brianne Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Three students answered the same exam prompt by hand: "What is happiness? Are you happy?" Their ages, recorded at collection, are 17, 28, and 45. The 17-year-old's answer is hedged and formally phrased. Asked to match each answer to an age and gender label, most frontier language models confidently assign it to an adult. This paper reports a structured case study of that failure across six models: Claude Fable 5, Gemini 3.1 Pro, GPT-5.6 Sol, Grok 4.5, GPT-4o (2024), and DeepSeek V4. We ran 1,081 API sessions and scored 3,225 responses across six experimental conditions that manipulate framing, disclosure format, and audience-awareness instruction. Three of six models perform at or near chance (1/3) on the matching task while reporting 73-88% mean confidence. The youngest writer's answer is misattributed to an adult in up to 90% of trials. Removing the multiple-choice scaffold collapses accuracy further: three models never place within three years of the true age once open-ended estimation is required. Institutional framing ("a community-college biology final") significantly degrades the best-performing model's accuracy; a neutral label does not. A single audience-awareness instruction significantly improves one model (+21 points) and moves another to ceiling, with no detectable effect on the three weakest models. Stated confidence is uncorrelated or inversely correlated with correctness for five of six models. After ground truth is revealed, one model names its own stereotyping in 68 of 114 explanations, yet shows the lowest accuracy and highest confidence of any model under blind presentation: articulate retrospective diagnosis that does not track the quality of its judgments. This is a three-item, single-topic, single-language case study meant to isolate a mechanism, not to estimate a population bias rate. Data, code, and raw model outputs are publicly released.
  </details>

- **[Kolibri: A Sovereign European Model on the Pareto Frontier](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31945)**  `arXiv:2610.31945`  
  _Aleph Alpha, Moritz Althaus, Artur Baranowski, Michael Barlow, Fabien C. Y. Benureau, Felix Berkenkamp, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This report introduces Kolibri, an English-German mixture-of-experts transformer with 78.1B total parameters and 3.46B active parameters per token, released as open weights under the Apache 2.0 licence. We built Kolibri for sovereign and specialised deployment, with a particular focus on German and on regulated domains such as public administration, industry, and aerospace. To support these settings, we control the full model-development process, including data, architecture, training infrastructure, post-training, and evaluation.We train Kolibri on 24T tokens across pre-training, mid-training, and long-context extension, with German accounting for more than 20% of the data mix by bytes. To reach this share, we complement German datasets with more than 2T German tokens that we curate from the web with our own pipeline and generate synthetically. Post-training combines supervised fine-tuning on curated German and English data with reinforcement learning on more than 1.2M internally curated tasks, which cover reasoning, tool use, instruction following, code, and retrieval. The model activates 4.4% of its parameters per token, and among the open models evaluated in this report, it lies on the Pareto frontier of quality and serving cost in English and German.Kolibri builds on Kolibri Origin, an earlier iteration that validated our full pipeline at scale. Both models come from our Model Factory, which connects data, experimentation, training, evaluation, and deployment through version-controlled workflows.
  </details>

- **[LazyFlash: Forward-Dense, Backward-Sparse Attention for Accelerated Long-Context Fine-Tuning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31911)**  `arXiv:2610.31911`  
  _Keqi Deng, Ruchao Fan, Jinyu Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  As large language models (LLMs) process increasingly long sequences, attention becomes a major computational bottleneck. Sparse attention mostly targets inference, yet many methods still train densely or cannot support sparse training, and their added complexity makes sparsity hard to turn into wall-clock speedups, leaving dense attention widely used in practice. This paper introduces LazyFlash, a forward-dense, backward-sparse attention method built on the observation that attention training is dominated by the backward pass. LazyFlash keeps the forward pass exactly dense, so the trained model remains a standard dense model that existing systems such as vLLM can serve without any change. For the backward pass, LazyFlash proposes a gradient-aware block sparsification design that ranks query blocks by their attention-output gradient norms and skips those contributing negligibly to parameter updates, turning the dense backward into a block-sparse one. A gradient-mass budget adapts the amount of skipped computation to each head and training step, while importance sampling preserves the dense gradient in expectation. These designs are implemented inside the tiled FlashAttention backward kernel, where skipped blocks are never loaded or computed, translating gradient sparsity into real wall-clock acceleration. Experiments on long-context text and speech LLMs show that LazyFlash maintains downstream performance while achieving up to 5.7x attention-backward speedup over FlashAttention and 1.7x end-to-end training speedup.
  </details>

- **[TaxoSAE: Rethinking Sparse Autoencoders through Taxonomic Logic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31894)**  `arXiv:2610.31894`  
  _Xinyu Guo, Minglai Yang, Clayton T. Morrison, Eduardo Blanco, Mihai Surdeanu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent Sparse Autoencoders (SAEs) decompose the representations of large language models (LLMs) into interpretable features, enabling precise readout and control of encoded knowledge. However, existing SAEs treat each feature as a standalone unit, whereas real-world knowledge is often taxonomically organized. As a result, readout carries no guarantee of taxonomic consistency, and control lacks the granularity to specify the scope of an edit. We propose TaxoSAE, which explicitly encodes taxonomical information, built on is-a (hypernym-hyponym) relations, into the SAE feature space. TaxoSAE mirrors a taxonomy with a stack of SAEs, one per taxonomy level, and introduces a taxonomic alignment loss that trains each concept to be active whenever one of its hyponyms is. On two factual QA taxonomies, one over biographical relations and one over animals, TaxoSAE enables (1) more accurate readout through the taxonomy with concentrated activations, e.g., +31.3 points in reading out the hypernyms of abstract concepts never queried in training; (2) more precise control, with +25.5 points in swap success, while suppressing a concept removes only its own answers (0-2% accuracy vs. 91-100% on other concepts); and, more importantly, (3) readout of unseen concepts through their hypernyms, with up to +37.9 points in hypernym readout accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Scaling cross-situational word learning on children's naturalistic experiences](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31842)**  `arXiv:2610.31842`  
  _Michael C. Frank, Alvin W. M. Tan, Khai Loong Aw, Robert Z. Sparks, Victor Moreno, Grace Keene-Lee, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  How do children learn the meanings of the simple, concrete words that form a majority of their early vocabulary? Statistical information about many word meanings can be recovered from the gradual accumulation of information about the co-occurrence of words with their referents, a proposal known as ``cross-situational learning''. Contrastive vision--language models allow this learning strategy to be tested on egocentric video data capturing children's early real-life experiences with visual and linguistic inputs. Initial successes of this method on small datasets provide a proof-of-concept, but vision models struggle to learn robust visual representations when trained on these same egocentric videos. How dependent is cross-situational word learning performance on the quality of the model's visual representations? And can cross-situational models scale to larger and more diverse datasets? To address these questions, we trained models on the BabyView corpus, including 2,441 hours of longitudinal egocentric video from 48 children. Models trained on this dataset learned object--label mappings, but performance was far higher when they used internet-pretrained visual representations as opposed to when visual representations were learned solely from the developmental video data. Further, the majority of the learning signal was carried by a small proportion of utterances that described a visible referent. Models learned lexical representations that showed recognizable human-like semantic structure, but model performance showed substantially different trajectories over training experiences than those observed in children. Cross-situational learning is a viable word learning strategy for early noun vocabulary, but constructing developmentally plausible models will require understanding how to learn high-quality visual representations from naturalistic data.
  </details>

- **[SPECMEM: Accelerating Agentic Tool Calling via Live Memory Management](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31840)**  `arXiv:2610.31840`  
  _Zihe Song, Wenting Zhao, Ye Liu, Yuanjie Zhu, Ke Xu, Shanghao Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Tool-calling agents are mostly optimized for accuracy; in deployment, however, their growing cost is latency: every tool call is decoded autoregressively. Speculative decoding accelerates this without changing the output, and retrieved past calls make strong drafts under real use, where each user's behavior tends to repeat. Yet existing systems keep no cross-session memory, or build the draft store once and never update it. We propose SpecMem, a tool-call memory kept alive and managed throughout deployment: verified calls are written back online into bounded per-user stores with LRU eviction, and a side-effect gate defers irreversible calls until verification confirms. On multi-session, multi-user query streams served by three open-weight models, a frozen store steadily loses acceptance while SpecMem holds it: a 27% gap in mean accepted tokens (+11% to +34% across benchmarks, tracking how much novel work arrives after the store is built) and up to 1.74x wall-clock speedup. Ablations attribute the gain almost entirely to keeping the store live; local per-user scoping adds no acceptance but closes a measured cross-user leakage channel, and gating on declared side effects, rather than on retrieval confidence, is what eliminates irreversible mistakes.
  </details>

- **[SAGE: Sense-Aligned Multimodal Grounding Evaluation with Gloss-Augmented Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31838)**  `arXiv:2610.31838`  
  _Sunjae Kwon, Suhaas Kiran DG, Zonghai Yao, Talha Chafekar, Deepali Singh, Hong Yu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This paper introduces Sense-Aligned Grounding Evaluation (SAGE), a unified 10-candidate image-selection benchmark that reformulates VWSD, VerSe, and WikiDiverse into a shared term-centric protocol covering noun-, verb-, and entity-centered grounding. SAGE includes confusable hard negatives and a context-swapped VerSe diagnostic to probe target-term grounding beyond direct caption--image compatibility. We further propose Gloss-Augmented Reasoning (GAR), a training-free framework combining definition grounding with constraint-guided semantic verification. Experiments show that strong VWSD performance does not transfer uniformly across grounding settings. With Gemma3-12B, definition augmentation improves all three subsets, while constraint-guided reasoning improves VWSD and yields particularly strong gains on VerSe, but is less effective on WikiDiverse. Combining the two signals achieves the best macro-average performance, improving Hits@1 from 65.90 to 71.22. These results show that SAGE supports both unified evaluation and fine-grained analysis of explicit semantic reasoning across grounding settings.
  </details>

- **[Latent Coherence: Do Frontier Language Models' Probability Judgments Obey the Law of Total Probability?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31833)**  `arXiv:2610.31833`  
  _Peter Potash_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  LawZero's Scientist AI trains a Predictor toward the Bayesian posterior, with an objective that penalizes inconsistent probabilities among latent explanations. We ask whether current frontier language models, with no such training, are already consistent in this sense zero-shot: do their probability judgments satisfy the law of total probability, $P(X)=\sum_z P(z)P(X|z)$, when the latent $z$ must be inferred from records in the context? In separate single-turn calls, models are asked for $P(X)$, for $P(z)$ over the latent's states, and for $P(X|z)$; a causal family applies the same test to $P(y|do(x))$ and the back-door adjustment. Every query has an exact ground-truth probability, and a subset of items is asked in three wordings to separate persistent incoherence from rewording noise. Across seven models, GPT-6.1 Sol and Claude Opus 5.5 are the most coherent (mean absolute residual 0.08 and 0.12 log-odds, about 2 percentage points). Claude Haiku 5.5, Gemini 3.8 Flash and DeepSeek V4-Pro show persistent incoherence (0.27-0.35 log-odds) that is mostly item-specific rather than a shared offset. Within every model, larger residuals go with larger errors against the truth (Spearman 0.40-0.62), so the residual, which needs no ground truth, flags unreliable answers. Reasoning length does not predict coherence, across models or within a model across rewordings of the same question. Simple consistency checks pass almost everywhere; the failures are specific to reasoning through a latent variable. Code and data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Free Lunch for LLMs Confidence: The RL Critic Already Knows](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31820)**  `arXiv:2610.31820`  
  _Shujin Wu, Heng Ji_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reliable confidence estimation is essential for trustworthy deployment of large language models (LLMs), but dedicated confidence training stage requires additional data and compute. We ask a fundamental question: what prediction problem does confidence training actually solve? Inspired by prior work on external confidence estimation, we compare self- and external estimators of comparable capacity trained on the same (question, response, correctness) triples. We show that an external model trained solely on a policy's behavioral traces can estimate the policy's confidence as accurately as the policy itself, without accessing the policy's internal representations. This points to a deeper connection with value function learning in reinforcement learning (RL): under a correctness-based outcome, we prove that confidence estimation and critic training are equivalent, both predicting the likelihood that a policy's response will be correct from its trajectory. A simple consequence follows: for LLMs trained with value-based RL, the existing critic can be reused as an off-the-shelf confidence estimator with no additional training or data. The policy remains unchanged, so its task performance is preserved, and the critic provides confidence throughout generation, including intermediate reasoning steps. Across math, code, tool calling, and general reasoning spanning a dozen domains, the reused critic is the best confidence estimator on every in-distribution split and the best-calibrated on 14 of 16, without hurting the task performance.
  </details>

- **[LAMB: A Modern Contextual Language Model for Latin](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31723)**  `arXiv:2610.31723`  
  _Aidan McCarthy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  This article introduces Latin Modern BERT (LAMB), an encoder for the Latin language based on the ModernBERT architecture, pretrained in two phases on more than 74B Latin tokens using a novel morphologically informed masking regimen. We also present two corpora for the Latin language. Acervus contains 24B BERT WordPiece tokens, including over 760K samples from heavily damaged OCR transcriptions, repaired by a ByT5 model trained for Latin text repair. The second dataset, Corpus Trinum, contains a mixture of over 100M strong and weak Latin language semantic triplets. Corpus Trinum is the first Latin semantic triplet dataset, opening the door for Latin language models to be adapted to general sentence semantic similarity tasks. We construct an experimental grid using five tasks and seventeen subtasks, applying a variety of finetuning configurations and subword aggregation techniques for 15 pretrained encoders, 6 LAMB training checkpoints, and an ablation model designed to quantify the impact of different masking regimens. Using this benchmark, we rank Latin encoders and find that LAMB outperforms all other evaluated models on 16 of 17 subtasks, coming within 0.8 percentage points of state of the art on the seventeenth. We provide additional analyses relating Latin encoder performance to tokenizer behavior, syntactic structure, finetuning configuration, pretraining state, subword aggregation, and variation in source texts and domains. In these, we identify unique acquisition dynamics for Latin open class words during terminal decay phases, and establish that for some task categories, finetuning configuration selection can have a greater effect on downstream scores than additional pretraining tokens.
  </details>

- **[Distributional Alignment of LLMs and Its Generalization Properties](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31687)**  `arXiv:2610.31687`  
  _Nelson Jo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used to simulate and probe human behavior, e.g., for surveys. For these applications, a key property is that outputs satisfy distributional alignment: to match its output distribution with some reference (in this case, human) distribution. An obstacle in achieving distributional alignment is generalizing from small training data. In this work, we study (1) how to distributionally align LLMs; and (2) how aligned LLMs might generalize to unseen samples and unrelated topics and tasks. Using two large-scale benchmarks, we find that LLMs can indeed achieve distributional alignment and generalize to unseen questions of the same domain. Cross-topic generalization depends on the relationship between topics (e.g., questions about politics are related to trust in institutions). However, aligned LLMs can degrade in surprising ways (e.g., lose their creative ability), suggesting that its generalization properties are inherently unpredictable and unstable.
  </details>

- **[From Target Selection to Condition Reuse: Auditing Residual Interventions in Tool-Use Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31686)**  `arXiv:2610.31686`  
  _Tianda Sun, Dimitar Kazakov_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Activation interventions can make a language model produce a target answer, but can the model use the intended condition in other computations? We study this question in Qwen3.5-27B on tool-use tasks where time or permissions determine eligibility and selection. Across 32 fresh worlds, two case-specific residual-writing variants raise familiar target-selection correctness from 35/128 to 119/128 and from 33/128 to 114/128. Yet these gains disappear when familiar facts and identifiers are recombined within the same selection task, while selection of previously rewarded identifiers increases. The gains persist across output formats but yield little improvement in independent judgments about whether a candidate is eligible or should be selected. Internal edits can nevertheless change how a source fact affects a judgment: in a prespecified secondary comparison, an early-layer source patch changes 50/64 judgments when that source is used, with one adverse change among 64 cases where it is not. This selective effect does not ensure correct complete actions. Together, these results separate target-selection control, condition-sensitive judgments, and complete-action correctness. Evaluating how an edited state is used, rather than only its trained answer, exposes these differences.
  </details>

- **[Knowledge Graphs Quality Assessment in the Era of LLMs: A Conceptual Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31684)**  `arXiv:2610.31684`  
  _Elwin Huaman_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Knowledge Graphs (KGs) have been popularized during the last few decades and are increasingly constructed and consumed by Large Language Models (LLMs). Various applications therefore rely on KGs to provide concise, complete, accurate, faithful, fresh, and traceable answers. However, what is the quality of those KGs? In which cases should a KG be used, and can it be trusted to ground an LLM? How might it be evaluated? We reviewed the literature on quality assessment (QA) of data, information, linked data, KGs, and the intersection of KGs and LLMs. We extended the current state-of-the-art frameworks to a catalog of 24 quality dimensions (QDs) and various quality metrics (QMs). Furthermore, we propose a general-purpose, customizable to a domain or task, and practical QA framework for assessing the quality of KGs.
  </details>

- **[The Schema Coherence Inventory: Measuring Whether an Assigned Belief Organizes a Language Model's Cognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31683)**  `arXiv:2610.31683`  
  _Yuqi Wu, Deyang Li, Katharine Hibbard, Ron Oswald, David Sinclair, Adegboyega Sapara, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Persona-conditioned language models are used as simulated patients and affective agents, but there is no validated way to ask whether a belief assigned to such a model organizes its behaviour or whether the model has merely adopted a mood. We present the Schema Coherence Inventory (SCI): 96 forced-choice items at eight levels of Beck's cognitive model, each offering six replies keyed to three negative self-schemas and their positive counterparts, scored by length-normalized log-likelihood so that nothing is generated or parsed. Affect alone can move a model onto a default belief, so the discriminant criterion is target specificity: a condition built for one belief must select that belief more often than conditions built for the other beliefs of its pole. Six validation studies over 107 conditions on five backbones (1.7B-14B parameters) support this reading. Nine clinically trained raters recover the keyed belief at 79.5% (chance 16.7%). Belief adapters are target-specific on every backbone (+17.7 points; 30/30 belief cells positive), and a condition's SCI profile identifies its own free generations in 24/30 cases against 5 expected by chance. A 48-item short form correlates r = 0.73 with the other 48 items. Applied to six published patient simulators, none carries its formulation's core belief better than a one-sentence statement of it. The validation route, which separates a construct from the affect that accompanies it and checks model judges against clinicians, offers a template for affective measurement in language models; we outline its extension across task types, raters, sites and languages. We release the items, scoring code and short form.
  </details>

- **[Multi modal perceived conversation success prediction for spontaneous autistic and non-autistic dyadic interactions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31682)**  `arXiv:2610.31682`  
  _Thanushi Withanage, Desi Jones, Noah Sasson, Elizabeth Redcay, Carol Espy-Wilson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversation success is important for communication, social bonds, and well-being and is often reduced in autistic-neurotypical conversations. Previous studies have predicted perceived conversation success using acoustic and lexical features in pediatric autism-enriched samples, or using nonverbal behavior in non-autistic group interactions. Other work has characterized prosodic, facial, motor, and turn-taking synchrony in autistic interactions, but has primarily targeted diagnostic classification or group differences. The present study instead predicts participant perceived conversation success from complementary articulatory, facial, acoustic-prosodic, and turn-taking measures across three spontaneous dyadic-interaction datasets including autistic and non autistic individuals. By leveraging multimodal features, we demonstrate that conversational success is inherently multidimensional and requires a multi-faceted analytical framework. Combining these complementary modalities offers the potential to improve prediction of perceived conversation success by capturing information unavailable from any single modality.
  </details>

- **[Rethinking Structured Generation: Can Graph-Based Reasoning Resolve Ambiguity?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31681)**  `arXiv:2610.31681`  
  _Ratun Rahman, Atit Pokharel_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Structured generation is typically formulated as a deterministic mapping from input to a single structured output. This assumption is fundamentally misaligned with natural language, which often admits multiple structurally distinct yet semantically valid interpretations. As a result, existing methods rely on single-reference supervision that collapses ambiguity into one prediction, leading to unstable and inconsistent outputs under underspecified inputs. We reformulate structured generation as an \textit{ambiguity-aware inference problem}, where the objective is to reason over a set of plausible structured outputs rather than predict a single solution. We show that standard training inherently suppresses valid alternatives, and instead adopt a multi-valid learning framework that preserves ambiguity during optimization. To operationalize this formulation, we construct a candidate space of structured outputs, represent each candidate as a graph capturing entities and their relationships, and perform selection via graph-based reasoning over semantic alignment and relational consistency. This enables principled comparison across competing interpretations beyond token-level likelihoods. Experiments on structured generation benchmarks demonstrate consistent improvements in accuracy, stability, and robustness under varying levels of ambiguity. These results highlight the importance of modeling ambiguity explicitly and suggest a shift from single-output prediction to set-level reasoning in structured generation.
  </details>

- **[MisconceptDialogue: Evaluation Criteria Shape Measured Success in LLM Math Tutoring](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31679)**  `arXiv:2610.31679`  
  _Woojin Lee, Yoonkyu Woo, Jinah Kim, Hyeok-Min Gwon, Jin-Xia Huang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models are increasingly used as math tutors, raising two questions: can a single LLM detect a student's error, diagnose the underlying misconception, and remediate it through multi-turn dialogue - and can we trust the numbers that report how well it does? We address both with MISCONCEPTDIALOGUE, a fully automated framework that runs the full pipeline on five open-weight LLM tutors (7B-14B) interacting with LLM-simulated students over 5,000+ dialogues - a deliberately simulated protocol (paired conditions, a wrong-diagnosis control unsafe with real learners, scale) whose results do not establish real-student learning. On measurement, our headline finding is uncomfortable for the field: benchmark-style exact-match scoring common in standard math NLP - a lower-bound stress test - reports 42.1% measured Remediation Success Rate on identical dialogues where a human-calibrated LLM-as-judge reports 81.3%, a 39.2 percentage-point spread larger than the combined effects of tutor model, teaching strategy, and supplied diagnostic information. This is not a single-judge artifact: four independent LLM judges and 200 human annotations converge to within a few points (kappa up to 0.91). On capability, the verdict is equally striking: the score barely changes whether we tell the tutor the student's actual misconception or a deliberately swapped one - under the tested diagnosis-presentation format, these tutors show little evidence of reliably conditioning on the diagnostic information they are handed. For LLM tutoring evaluation to mature, multi-criterion scoring with multi-judge convergence and human anchoring should become standard practice - and the measurement question deserves as much scrutiny as the capability question it claims to answer.
  </details>

- **[How Ground Truth Shapes Retrieval Scores: Evidence-Aware Evaluation of a Retrieval-Augmented Generation System for Breast Lesions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31606)**  `arXiv:2610.31606`  
  _Miguel Mochizuki Silva, Samuel Ismael, Mateus C. Dias, Thaís Gaudêncio, Yuri Malheiros, Bruno Barufaldi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Purpose: To describe an evidence-aware retrieval evaluation protocol for a clinical retrieval-augmented generation (RAG) system over breast lesion management literature, addressing the dependence of retrieval scores on annotation coverage. Methods: An 18-document corpus and an 18-query development benchmark, with one Yes/No or WH-question per document, were used. Retrieval combined dense embeddings, BM25, and cross-encoder reranking. Chunk sizes of 128 to 1024 tokens, overlaps of 0 to 20%, and four retriever configurations were evaluated. Relevance was defined by evidence spans mapped to all containing chunks, treating overlapping chunks with equivalent evidence as relevant. Retrieval was evaluated using R-Precision, Recall@K, MRR, and nDCG@K. Separately, generation was assessed on a 512-token index at K=1 to 5 using DeepEval claim-level metrics, with deepseek-v4-pro as the LLM judge, and BLEU/ROUGE metrics. Results: The selected hybrid retriever, using 384-token chunks and 20% overlap, retrieved accepted evidence in the top three results for 17 of 18 queries. Recall@3, R-Precision, MRR@3, and nDCG@3 each reached 0.94. Ground-truth curation increased evidence spans from 25 to 37 and accepted chunk targets from 36 to 51 without changing retrieval rankings. Conclusion: Cross-encoder reranking achieved the highest retrieval scores. Expanded relevance annotations increased recognized evidence without changing rankings, highlighting annotation sensitivity. The small development set and retrieval-informed curation require independent validation, including radiologist assessment of evidence sufficiency.
  </details>

- **[Mostly Reading: What Open-Book Scores Measure in Document-Level LoRA Adapters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31017)**  `arXiv:2610.31017`  
  _Martino M. L. Pulici, Cuong Xuan Chu, Evgeny Kharlamov, Volker Tresp_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Single-document LoRA adapters have been proposed as units of knowledge to be merged and retrieved; yet open-book scoring, with the document in the prompt, can credit a document's own adapter (its specialist) for reading that document as readily as for holding it. Training one adapter for each of 150 Wikipedia articles on Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct, we find that open-book scores mostly measure a reading behaviour every adapter shares: a random adapter trained on another document recovers 82 % of the specialist's open-book advantage over the base model but only 2 % of its advantage on a cloze probe that asks for the same document's facts without providing the source text, a share we cannot distinguish from zero. Content does reach the weights, since the specialists beat the base model in cloze recall by 0.094, and the geometry of their updates follows the documents they read; yet, in a test of 30 queries, adapters chosen by weight-space proximity or text embedding are not distinguishable from random ones on either probe, although that test cannot exclude an advantage of about four fifths of what a query's own adapter gains over a random one. At 16 documents or more, correctly scaled merges recover 87 % to 89 % of the specialists' open-book advantage but only 12 % to 16 % of their cloze advantage, with intervals that run from below zero to at most 32 %, against 94 % for joint training. We also report four silent errors, each of which produced a clean, wrong result.
  </details>

- **[ASTOR: Benchmarking and Improving Autoformulation for Soft-Constraint Optimization with a Formal Grammar](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26819)**  `arXiv:2610.26819`  
  _Amer Goel, Yeganeh Alimohammadi, Andrew Daw_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world decision problems combine two qualitatively different kinds of constraints: requirements that must be satisfied, called hard constraints, and preferences that can be violated, called soft constraints. Because these problems are often posed to decision-makers in natural language, large language models offer a natural tool for assisting with their solution. This has given rise to autoformulation, which focuses on using LLMs to translate natural-language descriptions of decision problems into executable mathematical optimization models. However, this research often neglects soft constraints like those proposed by consumers, managers, and planners, despite the fact that many practical problems require modeling them explicitly. We introduce ASTOR, an inference-time framework that maps a natural-language decision problem to a grammar-guided abstract syntax tree (AST) before deterministically compiling the resulting representation into executable optimization code. We also introduce SoftOR, a unified benchmark of natural language problems which focuses on soft constraint formulations. On SoftOR, ASTOR achieves the best aggregate accuracy among comparable single-request and multi-agent LLM methods while remaining competitive on standard autoformulation benchmarks that include only hard constraints. Code and data are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[AfriGuard: Safety and Robust Refusal For African Languages](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.26474)**  `arXiv:2610.26474`  
  _Israel Abebe Azime, Jesujoba O. Alabi, Iffat Maab, Alexander D. Cai, Tadesse Destaw Belay, Atnafu Lambebo Tonja, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) designed to interact with users are typically trained to respond appropriately to harmful or toxic content. However, ensuring appropriate responses becomes more challenging in diverse linguistic and cultural contexts, where safety risks may be expressed or interpreted differently. Building safe AI systems therefore requires detecting safety risks, understanding their nature, and responding appropriately, while accounting for linguistic and cultural variations that add complexity to each stage. In this work, we introduce AfriGuard, a culturally grounded multilingual dataset for studying harmful intent, safety-category understanding, and refusal behavior across 10 African languages and English. Our findings reveal that guardrail models underperform both open- and closed-source LLMs on safety-intent and safety-category classification, while models show limited understanding of appropriate safety behavior, scoring below 60% F1. We further find that models often fail to respond appropriately to unsafe prompts not because they explicitly refuse, but because they produce linguistically inadequate responses that result in incidental non-assistance rather than proper refusals. Finally, using AfriGuard, we develop efficient guardrail models that achieve performance competitive with models up to 30x larger, introduce refusal detectors that improve average performance by 43.9% over existing detectors, and demonstrate that supervised fine-tuning can substantially improve overall safety performance.
  </details>

- **[Muse Glimmer 30B: An On-Device Agentic Model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24628)**  `arXiv:2610.24628`  
  _Arash Ebrahim Abtahizadeh, Muna Aghamelu, Adil Ahmed, Juan C. Angeles Acuna, Alexis Audran-Reiss, Asaf Avrahamy, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce Muse Glimmer, an open-source 30B parameter causal language model with perception encoder, purpose-built for autonomous agentic tasks on consumer hardware. Muse Glimmer integrates multi-step reasoning, reliable tool use and failure recovery into a single model that runs locally on consumer devices without requiring cloud infrastructure. The model accepts text and image inputs and produces text outputs, was trained on data from over a hundred languages, and is quantized to approximately 4-bit precision for efficient single-GPU inference. Muse Glimmer achieves state-of-the-art performance for its size class on agentic benchmarks, setting a new standard compared to the models of its size: Gemma4-31B and Qwen3.6-27B on tool use, multi-step planning, and agentic task completion. We release the model under the Apache 2.0 license to foster open-source development of capable, safe, local AI agents.
  </details>

- **[Chinese Decision: A Family of Language Models for Single-Pass Probabilistic Decision-Making](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23170)**  `arXiv:2610.23170`  
  _Qiang Yan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many language-based applications need a probability distribution over a small set of decisions rather than an open-ended response. We present Chinese Decision, a family of Chinese-language decision models that adapts pretrained causal language models to this interface. Each question is compiled into a shared instruction-and-candidate prompt; a single forward pass reads the final-position logits of the candidate letters and normalizes them over the valid options, without autoregressive answer generation. The interface covers Boolean judgments, categorical choices, and ordinal scores. We report three completed members built on Qwen3.5 0.8B, 2B, and 4B backbones, each trained for one epoch on the same 13.048 million decisions and released with a validation-fitted temperature and a Q8_0 quantization. On the full 307,900-decision CJ-Bench, the 4B model reaches 85.97% overall accuracy, 75.69% general-domain accuracy, and 84.00% average accuracy across medicine, law, and finance with a single checkpoint, above every reported baseline in each domain, at 1.00% expected calibration error. Between the 2B and 4B members, which share data, objective, and minimum batch, parent-clustered paired bootstrap intervals show gains in all four domains, largest in the two low-resource domains (+8.5 and +7.1 points), whereas the 2B gain over 0.8B in law is not statistically resolved. Analyses of the stored predictions further show that a single global temperature lowers pooled calibration error while worsening some domains, that larger members rank their own errors better for selective prediction, and that merging and quantization each change 0.4-1.1% of top-1 predictions while leaving aggregate metrics nearly unchanged. All comparisons rest on single training runs and imported baselines.
  </details>

- **[Same Context, Different Agents: A Behavioral Study of Semantic Layers, Multi-Agent Orchestration and Memory Compaction in Data Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20959)**  `arXiv:2610.20959`  
  _Zhivar Sourati, Marianne Menglin Liu, Zheng Wang, Rongguang Wang, Sai Ashish Somayajula, Tao Sheng, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Answering questions over heterogeneous data means combining evidence from files, databases, documents and the web. Data agents automate this, but their success depends on context. Three context-engineering techniques (CETs) help by shaping it: semantic layers describe the data, orchestration splits work among agents, and compaction condenses interaction history. Existing studies validate each CET alone, leaving open how agent behavior shapes its effect. We present a comprehensive analysis of the three CETs, alone and combined, with seven large language models (LLMs) on 1,514 long-horizon tasks from five benchmarks, covering behavior, accuracy and cost. We find that data agents without CETs form three behavior groups: investigators, premature answerers that answer without inspecting data, and early stoppers that give up. Each CET delivers its known benefit, from more investigation to smaller prompts, but only the behavior group it matches turns that benefit into accuracy, such as premature answerers under orchestration. For investigators, the same CETs can hurt: the semantic layer stops them from looking, orchestration makes them overconfident, costing 5 percentage points, and compaction makes them repeat steps. Combining CETs yields smaller gains and losses than their sum. We derive guidance for choosing CETs by agent behavior, task demands and accuracy-cost trade-offs.
  </details>

- **[Cross-Model In-Context Neurofeedback Reveals (Limited) Activation Control in LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.20179)**  `arXiv:2610.20179`  
  _Davide Baldelli, Robyn Kozierok, Mirko Bronzi, Damiano Fornasiere_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Probes on activation vectors are widely used to detect misbehavior in language models, but their success relies on the assumption that models cannot control what their activations encode. Humans can learn, through neurofeedback, to control the firing of even single neurons, and recent studies investigated whether this ability extends to language models, reaching conflicting conclusions. No experimental design so far disentangles competence at the task from genuine access to one's own activations. We fill this gap with in-context neurofeedback sessions, in which a "subject" model is asked to maximize (or minimize) a score derived from its activations, shown back to it at every turn. In our first experiment, the sentences are generated either by the subject or by a second "writer" model, while the score always derives from the activations of the subject. We test ten open-weight models from five families, with sizes from 9B to 32B. Some models move their own scores considerably, but no more than a writer of comparable competence moves them. Is this because the task requires no privileged access, or because the subject controls its activations while processing the sentences, whoever wrote them? To answer this, we prefill a fixed sentence as the subject's reply at every turn and ask it to increase or decrease the score. Three out of ten models show small but consistent control effects. We further ask whether models can control their activations to the point of fooling an activation monitor. We make models repeat sentences a monitor would flag, such as a deceptive answer for a deception monitor, and ask them to raise or lower the monitor's score. No model learns to lower it, but one model can raise it. In-context control of a model's own activations might not yet be a threat to activation monitors, but it exists in a few models and needs to be tracked as models evolve.
  </details>

- **[ToolRACER: A Robust Agentic Conversation Emulation Resource for Agent Training and Evaluation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.09163)**  `arXiv:2610.09163`  
  _Arkajyoti Chakraborty, Aryan Tayal, Ishika Agarwal, Tanner Sorensen, Justin Chiu, Alessandro Di Bari, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Task-oriented conversational agents remain fragile under real world conversation scenarios as they rarely follow a predictable script, especially when users exhibit non-cooperative behavior. Existing function-calling benchmarks often emphasize successful, cooperative interactions and underrepresent adversarial conversation trajectories, thereby limiting the training resources available for developing robust agents. We present ToolRACER, a synthetic data generation pipeline that coordinates user, assistant and tool emulation models to generate and validated multi-turn interactions between a user and an agent. Using ToolRACER, we construct ToolRACERBench a robust multi-turn conversation benchmark spanning six domains, ranging over 55 varied personas, generating a validated corpus of 5.6K conversation trajectories, with approximately 66\% of conversations containing failure-prone conversation scenarios. We inject adversarial behaviors, producing validated conversational interaction trajectories that capture realistic, robust scenarios. We evaluate models trained on ToolRACERBench against internal benchmarks, as well as on function calling benchmarks such as $\tau^2$-bench, BFCLv3 and ACEBench to evaluate agentic accuracy and robustness. Models trained on ToolRACERBench improve end to end agentic accuracy across $\tau^2$-bench and ACEBench, demonstrating significant gains when mixed with in-domain dataset in small language models for agent capability tasks.
  </details>

- **[A Guideline-Augmented Multi-Agent Framework for Schema-as-Code Biomedical Named Entity Recognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02970)**  `arXiv:2610.02970`  
  _Songtao Li, Shidi Zhang, Jianyuan Yuan, Fengyu Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) have shown promising potential for biomedical named entity recognition (BioNER) through instruction following and in-context learning. However, existing LLM-based BioNER methods still face two key limitations. First, retrieved demonstrations and external biomedical knowledge provide limited support for dataset-specific annotation semantics, leaving entity boundaries, type scopes, and annotation conventions ambiguous. Second, free-form generation lacks sufficient structural control, often leading to invalid formats, hallucinated mentions, duplicated entities, and boundary errors. To address these limitations, we propose GAMA, a guideline-augmented multi-agent framework for schema-as-code BioNER. GAMA first induces candidate annotation rules from labeled training instances and verifies them against annotated data to construct reliable dataset-specific guideline memory. Guided by these verified rules, a planning component generates ranked span-type hypotheses with rationales, and a coding component converts them into schema-constrained entity objects. A verification module then checks span grounding, type validity, and structural compliance, and performs dual-loop refinement to correct invalid or low-confidence predictions. Experiments on three widely used BioNER datasets using two different LLMs demonstrate the effectiveness of GAMA, achieving the best performance in four out of six LLM-dataset combinations. Ablation studies further validate the contributions of its key components.
  </details>

- **[Enhancing Biomedical Named Entity Recognition via Multiple Programming Languages Instruction Tuning and Ensemble Method](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.02949)**  `arXiv:2610.02949`  
  _Songtao Li, Yijia Zhang, Jianyuan Yuan, Shidi Zhang, Fengyu Zhang, Hongfei Lin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Instruction tuning has become a common paradigm for applying large language models (LLMs) to biomedical named entity recognition (BioNER). However, existing instruction-tuning approaches still face two key challenges. First, conventional natural-language instructions typically serialize BioNER annotations as flat textual outputs, providing limited structural constraints for typed entity extraction. Second, high-quality biomedical annotations are limited, and learning from a single serialized output form may restrict structural diversity and reduce model robustness. Although external biomedical knowledge can be introduced to alleviate data scarcity, it often requires costly resource construction. To address these challenges, we propose MITE, a Multiple Programming Languages Instruction Tuning and Ensemble method for BioNER. MITE reformulates BioNER as a structure-to-structure generation task by representing both instructions and entity outputs in code-formatted representations. Specifically, each training instance is transformed into multiple programming-language formats, including Python, C++, and Java, while preserving the same underlying entity semantics. These language-specific representations provide structurally diverse supervision without requiring external biomedical knowledge or additional annotations. During inference, MITE aggregates predictions from different code formats through an entity-level voting strategy, reducing language-specific prediction variance and improving robustness. Experiments on three widely used BioNER datasets demonstrate that MITE achieves competitive performance against representative BERT-based and LLM-based baselines, attaining the best results on two benchmarks. Ablation studies further validate the effectiveness of multi-language representations.
  </details>

- **[LeakScale: Estimating the Causal Effect of Benchmark Exposure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.27176)**  `arXiv:2609.27176`  
  _Divyansh Singh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Benchmark contamination is usually treated as a provenance question: did evaluation material appear in training? Provenance cannot supply the quantity needed to interpret a benchmark score: how much exposure changed it. We introduce LeakScale, a causal measurement framework that makes benchmark-specific exposure manipulable and measures its effect. LeakScale constructs fresh executable tasks whose correct behavior depends on private, non-derivable information, counterfactually certifies every candidate answer, and trains complementary adapters so that every family is exposed in exactly one adapter and unexposed in the other. Across 2,048 families, two model families, two executable domains, three task formulations, and 589,824 generations, exposure increases exact-task accuracy in every model-by-domain cell. Primary effects range from +3.22 to +10.66 percentage points; every family-clustered 95% interval excludes zero, and all four primary tests survive Holm correction. The effect also remains positive in all eight surface-paraphrase and isomorphic-transfer measurements. These results show that benchmark exposure can causally and reproducibly alter reported performance, including after the evaluation surface changes. LeakScale shifts contamination analysis from detecting contact to measuring how strongly a score depends on it.
  </details>

- **[BabelArena: A Large-Scale Multilingual Benchmark for LLM Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2609.23490)**  `arXiv:2609.23490`  
  _Peng Kuang, Yuchun Fan, Jiangnan Li, Minghao Wu, Jialong Tang, Hao-Ran Wei, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM) agents increasingly execute multi-step workflows through tool use and interaction with users and environments. However, current agent evaluations are largely English-centric, limiting our understanding of agent capabilities in multilingual settings. We introduce BabelFlow, a benchmark-general agentic workflow that adapts existing agent benchmarks to new languages by analyzing runtime dependencies, coordinating structure-preserving translation, and combining multi-layer verification with human review to preserve task and evaluation semantics. Using BabelFlow, we construct BabelArena, a task-aligned benchmark comprising 16,146 instances derived from 702 canonical tasks across four benchmark families, 13 domains, and 23 languages. Experiments with five frontier models show that no single model dominates across benchmark families and that cross-language disparities extend well beyond task success. Lower-resource languages exhibit distinct failure patterns, with larger shares of tool-use and control-flow errors rather than answer-quality errors alone, pointing to gaps in reliable task execution across the resource levels of these languages. On the same tasks, agents in low-resource languages also consume substantially more tokens than in English (up to roughly twice the input) without proportional increases in interaction length, and language consistency degrades further on tasks requiring structured output, where switches are directed overwhelmingly toward English. We believe BabelArena provides a foundation for advancing research on reliable and efficient multilingual agents.
  </details>

- **[Beyond Static and Linear: What Attention Constraints Best Fit Human Reading Times?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.23818)**  `arXiv:2608.23818`  
  _Lanni Bu, Xiulin Yang, Christian Clark, Alex Warstadt, Ethan Gotlieb Wilcox_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Transformer-based language models are widely used as models of human language processing, yet their attention mechanisms allow lossless access to the full preceding context, unlike the limited memory systems of humans. We hypothesize that installing memory constraints into transformers' attention mechanisms can improve their fit to human behavioral data. While previous work has explored individual constraints in isolation, we conduct a systematic comparison of multiple attention-based memory mechanisms across different model sizes and training corpora, evaluating both psychometric predictive power for human reading times and grammatical competence. We additionally compare static constraints, in which the constraint strength is fixed throughout training, to dynamic memory curricula. We find that constraints that are sensitive to the content of intervening tokens consistently achieve the highest alignment with human reading times, outperforming distance-based constraints. We observe a dissociation between psychometric fit and grammatical competence under dynamic memory curricula, suggesting that Transformers cannot serve as a one-size-fits-all cognitive model.
  </details>

- **[Persona-Guided LLM Agents for Task-Oriented Dialogue](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.18085)**  `arXiv:2608.18085`  
  _Maryam Shoaeinaeini, Brent Harrison, A.B. Siddique_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Prior work has shown that large language models can express diverse personality traits in open-ended text generation, but it is unclear whether they can do so in goal-directed dialogue without harming task completion, and whether adapting to a user's personality improves the interaction. We study these questions in task-oriented dialogue (TOD) with a training-free framework in which a personality-conditioned user agent interacts with a system agent under three conditions: Neutral (no personality information), Try (adapt to cues observed in the dialogue), and Oracle (the user's trait label is given explicitly). Across GPT-4o, Qwen3-Next-80B, and Gemini 2.0 Flash, two Schema-Guided Dialogue domains, and ten Big Five poles (9,000 dialogues), each scored by two independent LLM judges, user agents express most traits while task performance remains high (89.94% completion, 85.27% success). The exceptions are introversive and closed users: these traits manifest mainly as absent behavior and are rarely detected. Both adaptive conditions improve pairwise user satisfaction and lower truthfulness, revealing a personalization-grounding trade-off, but only cue-responsive Try also improves constraint satisfaction and inform rate. How adaptation is implemented matters: Oracle's gains depend on how visibly the trait is expressed, and for disagreeable users the label leads Qwen3 and Gemini to mirror hostility. Try avoids this failure, its satisfaction gains do not depend on trait visibility, and it is preferred over Oracle overall. A 175-pair human study supports the reliability of the LLM judges: on clear-winner cases, annotators agree with the satisfaction judges more often than with each other (76.3% vs. 65.1% agreement). Our results suggest that responding to observable behavior is a more robust basis for social adaptation than conditioning on psychological labels.
  </details>

- **[Every Coin Has Two Sides: On the Dual Nature of Generalization in On-Policy Distillation of Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16647)**  `arXiv:2608.16647`  
  _Zhaoyi Li, Deyang Kong, Yuan Wei, Evan Yang, Ranran Shen, Mahardika Krisna Ihsani, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  On-policy distillation (OPD) transfers teacher capabilities by supervising trajectories sampled from the student's own policy, yet its generalization behavior remains poorly understood, as most studies evaluate OPD on a single domain and on benchmarks close to the training data. We present a controlled study that varies one generalization factor at a time, from in-domain distribution shifts to cross-domain transfer and the multi-teacher setting. We find that OPD transfers a teacher's reasoning behavior rather than its answers to particular problems: training difficulty barely matters, and even problems the teacher never solves are useful. Transfer depends strongly on the origin relationship between teacher and student: same-origin pairs bring the student close to the teacher across languages, reasoning horizons, and even other domains, whereas cross-origin pairs mostly fit the trained distribution. This broad reach is a double-edged sword: since routing prompts to domain experts cannot confine each teacher's influence, combining them yields a mixture-dependent seesaw among their capabilities. These results clarify when OPD generalizes and offer a useful perspective for diagnosing multi-teacher OPD.
  </details>

- **[When Humans Leave Context Unsaid: Implicit Context as a Risk for Large Language Models](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.09551)**  `arXiv:2608.09551`  
  _Bocheng Chen, Han Zi, Yuda Chen, Yawei Liu, Zimo Qi, Rongrong Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human language is grounded in pragmatics, necessitating implicit (extralinguistic) context to interpret language, e.g., world knowledge and social norms. However, such context is often implicit rather than explicitly expressed in language, requiring humans to infer the underlying contextual information necessary for language understanding. Because of its implicit nature and absence from textual corpora, it is often overlooked in the subsequent alignment process. In this paper, we demonstrate that the absence of such implicit context in training data is a risk for LLMs, and we refer to this risk as the implicit risk for LLMs. To exploit this implicit risk, we propose a method that guides LLMs to infer implicit contextual cues, such as presuppositions and implicatures, from prompts that imply social harm, and then leverages these inferred implicit context to elicit additional socially harmful content. Experimental results demonstrate that attacks exploiting the implicit risk we identify consistently outperform baseline methods by substantial margins across a diverse range of open- and closed-source models, underscoring the need for new defense methodologies that account for overlooked implicit context.
  </details>

- **[Romanized Arabic Across Dialects: Views, Usage Patterns, and Linguistic Variation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.02555)**  `arXiv:2608.02555`  
  _Amr Keleg, Ahmed Amine Ben Abdallah, Taha Yassine, Chadi Helwe, Imane Guellil, Nedjma Ousidhoum_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Arabizi refers to Arabic written in Latin script. Although previous studies have shown that the prevalence and usage of Arabizi vary by factors such as region and age group, most NLP research on Arabic texts treats it as a temporary phenomenon resulting from limited technological support for the Arabic script. In this work, we engage with Arabic speakers to collect insights on their perceptions and usage of Arabizi. We further examine writing norms among speakers of different dialects, focusing on Algerian, Egyptian, Lebanese, Moroccan, and Tunisian Arabic. To this end, we release two resources. First, we perform a character-level alignment of Arabic words to study inter- and intradialectal variation across these five dialects, based on words transliterated by the surveyed participants (VARabiziLex), finding systematic intradialectal regularity and interdialectal variation. Second, to study Arabic speakers' ability to identify this stylistic variation at the sentence level, we build VARabiziCues, a manually curated parallel corpus of sentences written in Arabic script alongside multiple Arabizi transliterations, collected from speakers of the same five dialects. Our study presents the largest human-centered, cross-dialectal study of Arabizi perceptions and practices to date.
  </details>

- **[A Cross-lingual Comparison of Human and Classification Model Entrainment Behavior in Code-switched Speech Settings](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25202)**  `arXiv:2607.25202`  
  _Debasmita Bhattacharya, Siying Ding, Alayna Nguyen, Julia Hirschberg_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Conversational entrainment is well-studied in monolingual and written contexts, but remains underexplored in spoken code-switching (CSW). We present a cross-lingual analysis of entrainment in Mandarin-English, Hindi-English, and Spanish-English dialogue, replicating and extending an established Spanish-English framework (Bhattacharya et al., 2024) to two new language pairs, and show that, while lexical entrainment generalizes across language pairs, entrainment over acoustic-prosodic and CSW style aspects exhibits context-specific variation. We build on these findings by asking whether classification models capture these human behavioral patterns. Using feature importance and ablation analyses, we find that classical and Transformer-based classifiers detect entrainment reasonably well but consistently prioritize features other than those most salient to human entraining behavior. Our approach introduces a human-grounded framework for evaluating model decision-making in multilingual stylistic contexts, and suggests challenges for developing conversational agents capable of producing naturalistic code-switched speech.
  </details>

- **[Expanding the Lexicon of Ge'ez Based African Languages: A Comparative Study of Amharic and Tigrinya](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.15209)**  `arXiv:2607.15209`  
  _Hailay Kidu Teklehaymanot, Debela Desalegn Yadeta, Wolfgang Nejdl_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Multilingual pre-trained language models such as XLM-R perform well for major languages but struggle with low-resource Ge'ez-script languages, largely because Latin-script-centric tokenizers split their words into many subwords. We introduce VEXMLM, a vocabulary-extended variant of XLM-R targeting Amharic and Tigrinya. We train language-specific SentencePiece tokenizers on monolingual corpora, extend XLM-R's vocabulary with 30k Ge'ez-script subwords, and initialize each new embedding to the mean of the pretrained embeddings. VEXMLM undergoes two-stage training: (1) continued masked language modeling on the monolingual corpora and (2) supervised fine-tuning on question answering and named entity recognition (Amharic and Tigrinya) and sentiment analysis (Amharic). VEXMLM lowers tokenizer fertility below that of XLM-R and Glot500 on both languages, by 28.0% (Amharic) and 45.9% (Tigrinya) relative to XLM-R. Downstream, it modestly improves named entity recognition over XLM-R, scores below XLM-R on extractive question answering, and is comparable on sentiment analysis. An ablation on Tigrinya NER shows that vocabulary expansion alone lowers accuracy on out-of-vocabulary words (words that XLM-R's tokenizer cannot represent or splits into more pieces than the expanded tokenizer), and that continued pretraining is required for the expanded model to exceed the baseline. Vocabulary expansion thus makes Ge'ez-script tokenization substantially more efficient, while its downstream benefit depends on the task and on adapting the new embeddings through continued pretraining. Resources: GitHub repository | Hugging Face model.
  </details>

- **[How Do I Know What to Say Next? Barenholtz's Autogenerative Theory as an Enrichment of Harrisean Integrationism](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.07891)**  `arXiv:2607.07891`  
  _J. Mark Bishop, Stephen J. Cowley_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Roy Harris's Integrationist linguistics offers a compelling critique of the referentialist tradition embedded deep at the heart of computational approaches to language, arguing that language is not a code that maps onto a pre-given world but a situated, bipartite activity oriented toward prospective joint action. Yet Integrationism leaves certain explanatory gaps: it does not fully account for the structural mechanism by which signs sustain prospective openness, it undertheorises the continuity between linguistic and non-linguistic semiotic activity, and it offers no detailed account of the structural properties of the accumulated archive of past integrations. This paper argues that Elan Barenholtz's autogenerative theory of language, developed in response to the behaviour of Large Language Models (LLMs), can fill precisely these gaps, enriching Integrationism without undermining any of its core commitments. Specifically, the autogenerative account provides: a structural mechanism for the prospective openness that Harris identifies as central to bipartite communication; a computational correlate for Harris's thesis of semiotic continuity between language and other sign-making activity; and a theory of the archive: what the accumulated residue of past integrations looks like and how new participants draw upon it. The synthesis preserves Harris's ontological primacy of the situated integrative act while adding explanatory content that Integrationism itself does not supply. For practitioners and researchers in natural language processing and large language model design, the argument offers a principled account of what the statistical structure that LLMs so effectively exploit actually is, and of what it cannot, by its nature, provide.
  </details>

- **[Characterize Then Distill: Mechanistic Reasoning in Large Output Spaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.06840)**  `arXiv:2606.06840`  
  _Debjyoti Saha Roy_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reasoning-trained language models can select, zero-shot, a small set of relevant labels from thousands to hundreds of thousands of candidates. We ask how they do it mechanistically, and whether the mechanism can be distilled. We treat each decision as a token-level event scored by the model's own decision margin: the token that picks a coarse region of the label space, the tokens that pick a label within it, and the token where the output departs from a near-miss named earlier in the reasoning. Attribution, exact mean-ablation, knock-in into another example's context and a null calibration give individual attention heads causal standing. On clinical coding of MIMIC-IV discharge summaries, with all 5,651 candidate codes in context, a small, global, phase-structured set of heads is necessary and sufficient on essentially every summary; distinct head families read the candidate region and the earlier near-miss, and blocking what they read removes their effect; for mentions elicitable in the reasoning, a disjoint mid-layer set makes the region elicitable several tokens before the code. The structure recurs in a second reasoner. We introduce MISTILL, which, unlike chain-of-thought distillation, also supervises the student's pooled attention at these decision events. Read on heads found after training, it nearly doubles the causal recovery of the contrastive decision in a cross-family student and adds a small, seed-stable gain in one that already carries most of it, with no detected task difference at matched precision; under matched selection it exceeds a frozen random-head pool on both recovery and task score. A pre-registered evidence-flip test detected no improvement in evidence-dependent decisions through the tested routes.
  </details>

- **[RCEM: Robust Conversational Search EMbedder in Distributional Shift](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.01697)**  `arXiv:2606.01697`  
  _Kilho Son, Paul Hsu, Cha Zhang, Dinei Florencio_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We propose RCEM, a Robust Conversational search EMbedder that is additionally equipped with LLM's query reformulation capability without losing base model's generalization. Unlike prior conversational dense retrieval approaches that learn direct conversation-to-passage matching, RCEM aligns conversations, prepended by special token, to LLM-rewritten queries, while preserving the original embedding space. The unchanged embedding space automatically maps the rewritten-query to the relevant passages. As a result, RCEM (1) reduces overfitting by simplifying the alignment task from long passages to shorter rewritten queries, (2) eliminates the need for conversation-to-passage relevance labels for training, and (3) maintains its original embedding space that allows conversational queries against indexes built by original embedder without rebuilding them. Extensive experiments show that RCEM consistently outperforms prior approaches, achieving up to 30% improvement under distributional shift.
  </details>

- **[GRKV: Global Regression for Training-Free KV Cache Compression in Long-Context LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.31105)**  `arXiv:2605.31105`  
  _Junjie Peng, You Wu, Haoyi Wu, Jialong Han, Xiaohua Xie, Kewei Tu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language models (LLMs) with extended context lengths rely on the key-value (KV) cache to support attention over prior tokens. However, maintaining the KV cache incurs substantial memory overhead, motivating KV-cache compression methods that enforce a fixed budget through eviction and merging. Modern eviction methods increasingly adopt span-based retention because preserving contiguous spans is empirically effective and better preserves semantic coherence. Yet, when combined with post-eviction merging, span-based retention concentrates merges onto a small set of span-boundary carrier tokens, producing a highly imbalanced merge pattern that exacerbates over-merging and increases information loss. To address this imbalance, we propose GRKV (Global Regression for KV Cache), a training-free KV-cache merging method that directly minimizes the discrepancy between compressed-cache and full-cache attention outputs. GRKV uses ridge-regression-based merge steps to distribute information from evicted tokens across retained tokens, while regularizing the updates to prevent over-smoothing. Across the LongBench and RULER long-context benchmarks, GRKV is the only merging method that improves overall performance with minimal overhead. Our code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Deep Reasoning in General Purpose Agents via Structured Meta-Cognition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.11388)**  `arXiv:2605.11388`  
  _Dean Light, Michael Theologitis, Kshitish Ghate, Shuyue Stella Li, Benjamin Newman, Chirag Shah, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans intuitively solve complex problems by flexibly shifting among reasoning modes: they plan, execute, revise intermediate goals, resolve ambiguity through associative judgment, and apply formal procedures to well-specified subproblems. Current LLM agents lack this flexibility, as their scaffolds hard-code such reasoning decisions in advance. These scaffolds are effective when their prescribed structure matches the task, but brittle when solving the task requires adapting the structure of reasoning itself. We introduce Deep Reasoning -- an inference-time approach for constructing task-specific scaffolds through structured meta-reasoning. Deep Reasoning uses a formal language that represents meta-reasoning as executable decompositions over associative inference, formal computation, and recursive subproblem solving, enabling decomposition principles to be encoded as in-context examples that guide test-time scaffold construction. We instantiate this approach in a general-purpose agent (DOLORES) that distributes complex tasks across more controlled reasoning threads. We evaluate it against state-of-the-art scaffolding methods across four hard benchmarks: multi-hop reasoning, long-chain question answering, long-context aggregation, and deep research-style information seeking. DOLORES outperforms all evaluated scaffolds across three model sizes and two model families, improving over the strongest evaluated scaffold baseline by 24.8% on average. DOLORES distributes cognition across structured, lower-load reasoning threads, thereby reducing premature termination and hallucinations. This advantage can even bridge the scaling gap, with an 8B version surpassing all evaluated 32B baselines from the same family in more than half the settings. These results point toward future agentic systems that treat scaffolding as adaptive reasoning, constructing the structure each task requires just-in-time.
  </details>

- **[MedAction: Towards Active Multi-turn Clinical Diagnostic LLMs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.07305)**  `arXiv:2605.07305`  
  _Hsin-Ling Hsu, Zizheng Wang, Donghua Zhang, Nai-Chia Chen, Jerry Wang, Jun-En Ding, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
