- **[Agent-Based Transport Model Calibration via Online Stochastic Batch Optimization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32698)**  `arXiv:2610.32698`  
  _Abdelkader Dib, Milos Balac_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Calibrating agent-based models (ABMs) is computationally demanding, as traditional black-box optimization typically requires hundreds or thousands of independent simulation runs. To reduce this burden, we introduce an online calibration framework that estimates parameters within a single simulation run. The approach combines black-box optimization with stochastic batch optimization, a method widely applied in machine learning but not yet explored in ABM calibration. Using MATSim as a case study, we treat each simulation iteration as a stochastic batch, enabling incremental parameter updates as agents make decisions. This online calibration paradigm allows model parameters and agent behaviors to co-evolve, achieving convergence without restarting the simulation. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our method by calibrating utility function parameters in MATSim for a large-scale transportation scenario of Switzerland. Results show that mode shares align closely with observed data within a typical simulation timeframe, confirming the feasibility and efficiency of the approach. By embedding calibration directly into the simulation loop, our method opens new possibilities for real-time, scalable, and efficient calibration of complex ABMs.
  </details>

- **[Markets of Minds: A 51-Billion-State Economy of Intelligence](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32190)**  `arXiv:2610.32190`  
  _Jusheng Zhang, Xuekun Wu, Wenhao Wang, Mark Wang, Ming-Hsuan Yang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When intelligence is shared, reasoning becomes a constrained allocation problem. In a population of agents with limited access to expert models, an intervention can benefit its recipient yet still be a poor use of shared capacity if another feasible intervention has greater expected value. Assistance must therefore be evaluated through both its marginal benefit and the opportunity cost induced by budgets, access, scarcity, and service capacity. To this end, we introduce Markets of Minds, a framework for allocating expert reasoning across agents before expert outputs are observed. For each feasible agent-service pair, the framework estimates the expected gain from assistance relative to the agent's default continuation, explicitly separating repair benefit from regression risk, and jointly clears assistance contracts under shared resource constraints. We then study this problem in a dependency-aware agent economy with 51.2 billion addressable persona-market states spanning capabilities, cognitive budgets, provider access, scarcity, and network roles. These dependencies couple allocation feasibility across agents rather than the outcome of a fixed contract. Empirically, in a matched replay of 240 cases under a common 30% assistance quota, the frozen allocator reaches 85.42% task-level persona adherence versus 73.86% for equal-capacity random allocation, a gain of 11.56 percentage points. The results show that assistance value is heterogeneous and can be negative, and that scarcity changes which interventions are worth purchasing. Together, these findings motivate a population-level view of intelligence where the value of assistance depends not only on what it can achieve, but also on which alternative uses of scarce cognitive capacity it displaces.
  </details>

- **[Toward an Agentic AI Approach to Public Health Data Collection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31746)**  `arXiv:2610.31746`  
  _Xueyi Zhang, Hongru Du, Ensheng Dong_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Disease surveillance increasingly integrates automated data collection, yet predefined sources and procedures require manual adaptation as reporting practices change. We explore agentic artificial intelligence (AI) as an approach to coordinating surveillance data collection across heterogeneous sources. Specifically, we built a multi-agent framework that integrates source discovery, source assessment, evidence structuring, event linking, and validation. Three retrospective applications illustrate its promise. The workflow recovered all 13 individual cases in a hantavirus outbreak, with links to 85 distinct publisher domains compared with 28 in the reference benchmark, reconstructed Canadian measles counts with a final total 0.33% below the national reference and higher cumulative counts on 79.8% of comparable days, and increased the share of days with recent Sierra Leone mpox reports from 28.2% to 65.6% by incorporating attributed media. These demonstrations also reveal limitations in record completeness and accuracy, while prospective gains in timeliness remain untested. We envision agentic AI as a complementary layer of public health data infrastructure, coordinating evidence-linked collection under surveillance goals and standards defined by public health professionals.
  </details>

- **[Not Every Agent Needs the Strongest Model: Unified Prompt-Model Optimization for Heterogeneous LLM-based Multi-Agent Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31743)**  `arXiv:2610.31743`  
  _Mingxiu Cai, Haoyang Xian, Xinyu Ma, Yutong Wang, Zhenxi Song, Jing Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems (MAS) rely on role-specific prompts and execution models to solve complex tasks collaboratively. However, prompt optimization typically keeps model assignments fixed, leaving differences in role requirements and their associated inference costs outside the search. We present UniMASO, a Unified framework for heterogeneous Multi-Agent System Optimization through the joint adaptation of prompts and model assignments. UniMASO evaluates candidate updates through local, downstream, and global feedback, using cost-aware Pareto search to retain useful quality-cost trade-offs. Topology-aware co-evolution then coordinates these updates according to agent roles and dependencies. Cross-level trajectory memory further supplies historical successes and failures to guide subsequent optimization. Experiments on five benchmarks and five execution topologies show that UniMASO achieves competitive task performance at substantially lower deployment cost than the strongest homogeneous configurations. Under centralized execution, it improves the average score from 69.79 to 70.40 while reducing token-weighted deployment cost by 85.2% relative to DeepSeek-V4-Pro with MASPO. These results support joint prompt-model adaptation as an effective approach to efficient multi-agent collaboration. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[S$^2$-MAS: A Society-Scale Multi-Agent Simulation Framework with 500 Million Household Persona Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.27746)**  `arXiv:2610.27746`  
  _Yang Li, Yike Wang, Di Wan, Chujun He, Zongsi Xu, Yitao Yu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Simulating economic responses at society scale requires agents whose decisions reflect household circumstances. We introduce S$^2$-MAS, a society-scale multi-agent simulation framework built on 500 million synthetic household personas grounded in real-survey data. Its Genese engine separates decisions from state updates, while reusable financial scenarios and application ledgers track consumption, cash, debt and payments. Trained with SFT + RLCD on 2.93 million examples, sharp-421m-mlx achieves 74.55% accuracy and 38.37% question-macro F1 on one-shot consumer financial-choice tasks derived from held-out survey responses. Its accuracy exceeds GLM-5.3-Flash, Jev 1.13, DeepSeek-V4.1-Flash, GPT-6-Luna and GPT-6-Astra in the common benchmark (full test for the first four; matched 500-group sample for Astra). We evaluate eight financial task families and simulate 12 million distinct household agents in paired purchase-funding conditions. Jointly raising purchase goals and approved credit limits increases consumption, with responses differing across age and income groups. Education, employment and household-size differences persist after age-income standardization and mainly reflect transitions from deferral to purchase. Asset and debt groups differ in cash drawdown, repayment and closing debt. S$^2$-MAS links heterogeneous household decisions to evolving consumption, cash and repayment paths, supporting economic experiments across household groups and population scales.
  </details>

- **[ROAR: Benchmarking LMM Agents under Real-Time Constraints in Multiplayer Action-Shooter Games](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24282)**  `arXiv:2610.24282`  
  _Dawei Wang, Di Zhao, Jianran Yu, Chengming Zhou, Marci Chi Ma, Xiaoyang Liu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large multimodal models (LMMs) are increasingly deployed as agents that perceive, reason, and act in interactive multi-agent environments. Yet most existing benchmarks for LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings remain turn-based: the environment and other agents pause while an agent performs inference. Such protocols obscure a fundamental challenge of real-time constraints, where the world continues to evolve and delayed decisions may become outdated before execution. Motivated by this limitation, we introduce ROAR, a multiplayer action-shooter game environment for evaluating LMM-based agents in multi-agent settings under real-time constraints. ROAR covers cooperative, competitive, mixed cooperative-competitive, and basic single-agent scenarios. We further propose a Multi-Agent Asynchronous Gym interface that decouples environment evolution from agent reasoning by running the environment and each agent in separate threads, enabling the environment to advance at fixed time intervals without waiting for LMM inference and allowing agents to reason independently without blocking one another. ROAR also features convenient deployment, multimodal observations, and customizable levels. ROAR offers a practical testbed for advancing LMM-based agents beyond static, turn-based evaluation toward real-time multi-agent decision-making.
  </details>

- **[A-MapReduce: Executing Wide Search via Agentic MapReduce](https://arxiv.org/abs/2602.01331)**  `arXiv:2602.01331`  
  _Mingju Chen, Guibin Zhang, Heng Chang, Xiao Wang, Shiji Zhou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contemporary large language model (LLM)-based multi-agent systems exhibit systematic advantages in deep research tasks, which emphasize iterative, vertically structured information seeking. However, when confronted with wide search tasks characterized by large-scale, breadth-oriented retrieval, existing agentic frameworks, primarily designed around sequential, vertically structured reasoning, remain stuck in expansive search objectives and inefficient long-horizon execution. To bridge this gap, we propose A-MapReduce, a MapReduce paradigm-inspired multi-agent execution framework that recasts wide search as a horizontally structured retrieval problem. Concretely, A-MapReduce implements parallel processing of massive retrieval targets through task-adaptive decomposition and structured result aggregation. Meanwhile, it leverages experiential memory to drive the continual evolution of query-conditioned task allocation and recomposition, enabling progressive improvement in large-scale wide-search regimes. Extensive experiments on five agentic benchmarks demonstrate that A-MapReduce is (i) high-performing, achieving state-of-the-art performance on WideSearch and DeepWideSearch, and delivering 5.11% - 17.50% average Item F1 improvements compared with strong baselines with OpenAI o3 or Gemini 2.5 Pro backbones; (ii) cost-effective and efficient, delivering superior cost-performance trade-offs and reducing running time by 45.8\% compared to representative multi-agent baselines. The code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[DexPort: A Hand-Held Skill Interface for Dexterous Hand Data Collection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32969)**  `arXiv:2610.32969`  
  _Amber Xie, Jiawei Fu, Xiaofeng Guo, Carmelo Sferrazza, Guanya Shi, Haozhi Qi_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collecting high-quality demonstrations remains a central bottleneck for learning dexterous manipulation. Teleoperation records executable robot actions, but it requires a complete robot setup and places the burden of coordinating many finger joints on the operator. Human demonstrations are easier to collect, but their motions must be estimated and retargeted to the robot, and they do not directly provide robot actions. In this paper, we introduce DexPort, a portable data-collection interface for high-DoF robot hands. It uses the same hand hardware for collection and deployment, avoiding a hand embodiment gap, and records both commanded joint targets and measured joint states. Our key observation is that a small set of skills can support a wide range of dexterous tasks. During collection, the operator moves the hand-held interface to specify the robot wrist motion and selects or adjusts skills that coordinate the finger motion. This removes the need to control each joint independently while retaining behaviors such as precise grasping and in-hand manipulation. We collect demonstrations across several real-world tasks and in unstructured environments without a robot arm. We then use behavior cloning to train visuomotor policies directly on the recorded wrist motions and hand commands, without skill labels. We evaluate across 4 tasks: pick-and-place with diverse objects, screwing with in-hand tool use, drawer stowage which requires simultaneous grasp and pulling, and a bimanual scanning task. We collect over 2,500 demonstrations with our portable device, in both laboratory and in-the-wild settings. Across our tasks, we see an improvement from 11% to 64% when using recorded robot commands instead of states, and an improvement from 36% to 78% for task success when using in-the-wild data.
  </details>

- **[ViT-H: Generative Video-To-Humanoid Control with Motion Tracking Feedback](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32966)**  `arXiv:2610.32966`  
  _Zimo He, Tianyi Bian, Zizhong Yang, Hongjie Li, Nan Jiang, Jieming Cui, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learning humanoid control from monocular human videos offers a scalable way to acquire diverse whole-body behaviors. Yet, existing reconstruction-retargeting-tracking pipelines are limited in system-level scalability by separately optimized intermediate stages and cannot directly optimize reference motion executability using downstream tracking feedback. We present ViT-H, a video-to-humanoid control framework following a generation-and-tracking paradigm. ViT-H directly generates humanoid reference motions from videos with a diffusion model, bypassing human motion reconstruction and retargeting, and uses motion-tracking outcomes to post-train the generator with direct preference optimization. We further introduce an autoregressive variant that enables real-time humanoid video teleoperation with sub-400 ms latency. Extensive experiments on the Unitree G1 demonstrate that ViT-H outperforms state-of-the-art reconstruction-retargeting-tracking pipelines in both motion quality and executability. We further show that ViT-H scales favorably with model size and with both pre-training and post-training data scale, while motion-tracking feedback consistently improves executability across different motion trackers.
  </details>

- **[RynnUnify: Towards Unified Embodied Brain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32965)**  `arXiv:2610.32965`  
  _Minghao Zhu, Bohan Hou, Kun Xiang, Zixuan Wang, Yinzhou Tang, Yifei Yang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We introduce RynnUnify, a 16B unified embodied model that combines strong embodied understanding with long-horizon visual planning through interleaved video--language reasoning. With capabilities spanning spatial reasoning, physical prediction, and grounding, RynnUnify connects an understanding of the physical world with visual anticipation of how it evolves across successive subtasks. Building on these capabilities, we further develop an agentic extension through reinforcement learning to selectively use video predictions for reasoning about unobserved states and interaction outcomes. We also develop a world-action extension of the full RynnUnify model, translating its understanding and prediction capabilities into robot control. To evaluate these capabilities, we introduce Rynn-VP and Rynn-GEU for long-horizon visual planning and anticipatory reasoning. Experiments show that RynnUnify achieves the highest average embodied understanding score among the evaluated unified multimodal models and improves the Rynn-VP overall score by 9.75 points over Cosmos 3 Nano. Its agentic extension improves the Rynn-GEU average by 6.0 points over direct reasoning, while the world-action extension achieves 51.5\% success on 20 selected composite RoboCasa365 tasks and 46.7\% average success across three real-robot tasks. Together, these results establish RynnUnify as a shared foundation for embodied understanding, visual planning, and robot control, taking a step toward a unified embodied brain.
  </details>

- **[Robotic Optical Coherence Tomography-Guided Geometric Modeling of Soft Tissue: 3D Representations for Data-Driven Closed-Loop Shape Control](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32961)**  `arXiv:2610.32961`  
  _Chaoyu Zhang, Guangshen Ma, Mark Draelos_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In medical robotics, data-driven soft-tissue shape control requires deforming tissue toward desired morphologies through sequential physical interactions. This challenge is particularly important in microscale manipulation, where localized deformation is difficult to observe, represent, and control, while physics-based models can be computationally expensive and difficult to adapt to realistic environments. Optical coherence tomography (OCT) provides high-resolution volumetric perception of tissue deformation, but target-conditioned control requires effective representations of both observed and desired tissue states. In this work, we introduce an OCT-guided data-driven framework for tissue shape control, learned from user-guided demonstrations and executed with closed-loop OCT feedback. We study full-volume, tissue-masked sparse-volume, and voxelized point-cloud representations, with or without residual conditioning, using deterministic (Explicit) and generative diffusion (Diffusion) policies. Across 12 configurations and 576 phantom rollouts on 3-DoF depth and 6-DoF directional tasks, OCT-derived 3D representations support both Explicit and Diffusion policies, with performance depending on representation and residual conditioning. Residual conditioning reduces directional Shape RMSE from 0.748 to 0.555 mm for full-volume Diffusion, while voxelized point-cloud Explicit with residuals achieves 0.534 mm for depth and 0.443 mm for directional control, with comparable accuracy at intermediate unseen targets and consistent performance across deployments. We further evaluate the framework in 60 ex vivo porcine rollouts, extending to biological tissue with final geometric errors comparable to human demonstrations. These results establish volumetric OCT as current and goal representations for learned closed-loop tissue shape control across deterministic and generative action models.
  </details>

- **[If at First You Don't Succeed, Backtrack: Lightweight Recovery for Frozen VLAs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32941)**  `arXiv:2610.32941`  
  _Thomas Landeg, Thierry Tambe_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models perform well on robotic manipulation, yet remain brittle once their own actions carry them into states their training data barely covers. Recovering from such failures usually requires recovery demonstrations, policy retraining, or a separate model at inference time. We introduce Backtrack, a lightweight failure detection and recovery mechanism for frozen VLAs that needs none of these. A small failure prediction head, trained only on outcome-labeled rollouts of the frozen policy, reads the VLA's existing internal representations to estimate whether the current episode will fail. When the predicted failure probability exceeds a threshold, a controller pauses the policy, retraces the arm's recently achieved motion, and hands control back to the unchanged policy so it can retry from an earlier configuration. The head adds only 0.48-0.88% to the policy's parameter count. We evaluate Backtrack on LIBERO with three VLAs spanning two action-generation families: SmolVLA and $\pi$0.5, which use flow-matching action experts, and the autoregressive $\pi$0-FAST. On LIBERO-Long, the suite of long-horizon multi-step tasks and the one on which all three policies fail most often, Backtrack reduces each policy's failure rate by 19-48%, raising success by up to 7.4 percentage points; on the three shorter suites, where control success is already 85-99%, its effect is smaller. On MetaWorld, all twelve controller configurations tested with SmolVLA improve on the unassisted policy.
  </details>

- **[HoloBoxing: A Simple Recipe for Humanoid Boxing with 7 Minutes of Single-GPU Training](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32909)**  `arXiv:2610.32909`  
  _Xihan Ma, Bo Zhang, Boxiong Li, Yuhan Bao, Ziyue Liu, Yucheng Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  HoloBoxing presents a simple recipe for learning humanoid boxing by building on a pretrained whole-body controller. The recipe separates reusable motor skills from task decisions: a compact planner requests future body-point targets, while the frozen controller handles joint-level execution. A learned decoder maps 32-dimensional actions to structured target windows, and measured-state imitation initializes the planner before reinforcement learning. Task learning then refines interaction behavior while reusing the decoder and controller, avoiding the need to relearn whole-body control. This combination keeps task optimization focused on a compact upper policy and supplies an initial behavior before reward-driven exploration. Starting from that available controller, the recipe accounts for about seven minutes of additional training, including reused representation fitting and a single-GPU task run. This excludes controller pretraining, data preparation, evaluation, and search. Simulated boxing experiments and component controls evaluate the resulting behavior and the roles of imitation initialization and the target representation.
  </details>

- **[When Human Corrections Travel: Safety in Federated VLA Adaptation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32888)**  `arXiv:2610.32888`  
  _Vigneshwaran Subbaraju, Obaidullah Zaland, Monowar Bhuyan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) policies can adapt to local human intervention, while federated learning can combine distributed robot experience without pooling raw records. We examine their prospective intersection: sharing adaptations derived from diverse human corrections across tasks, environments, people, and robot embodiments. Corrections differ in modality, safety semantics(urgency, behavioral scope, etc.), and privacy sensitivity. A shared update that improves mean task success may suppress a rare safety intervention or produce an unsafe response on another robot. We argue for three requirements: preserve the context and uncertainty of multimodal corrections when deciding what to share; assess candidate updates in each receiving robot's closed loop before deployment; and evaluate leakage alongside retention of safety-critical evidence. Immediate, legible defensive behavior remains necessary during human interaction. We offer counterexamples and a falsifiable evaluation agenda, not experimental results.
  </details>

- **[Predictive Long-Horizon Progress Modeling with Action-Aware Quantification](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32776)**  `arXiv:2610.32776`  
  _Tianchi Xu, Yalin Zheng, Yangxin Zhu, Ruonan Ren, Zheng Fu, Yunlong Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Quantitative and dense task progress feedback is essential for robotic manipulation across data curation, policy evaluation, and reinforcement learning. Existing progress annotations often rely on sparse keyframe markings derived from subjective manual judgments, limiting their scalability and cross-task consistency, particularly in long-horizon manipulation. To overcome these challenges, we propose action-aware quantification, a unified progress specification that decomposes long-horizon trajectories into object-level subtasks via a vision-language model (VLM) and assigns weighted action credits to capture fine-grained execution dynamics. Using VLM auto-labeling and human calibration, we construct an 800+ hour training video dataset and LOOM, a long-horizon progress benchmark containing 19,618 human-calibrated evaluation frames. To model these dense progress dynamics, we introduce NimCritic, a predictive progress critic trained via Joint Video-Progress Learning. We further incorporate Historical Progress Perturbation to mitigate sequential drift, and design a contrastive Video-Progress Alignment objective that bridges asymmetric expert streams by mutually grounding intermediate visual dynamics and denoised progress trajectories. NimCritic matches or outperforms state-of-the-art process reward models (PRMs) on the established Video Progress Benchmark (VPB) and LOOM, surpassing GVL (GPT-6-Sol) on VPB by about 65% in terminal success accuracy (TSA). On the large-scale RoboArena policy evaluation benchmark, it also retrieves human-selected successful rollouts with about 97% overall recall. These results establish action-aware quantification as a scalable foundation for dense progress feedback in robotic manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Representation for Agentic Self-Play in Automated Laboratories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32755)**  `arXiv:2610.32755`  
  _Yves Delley, John Coller, Zachary Cutts, Alexander Daryin, Mathias Bellaiche, Jun W. Kim, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Navigating complex, high-dimensional biological search spaces necessitates scalable physical and computational intelligence to explore beyond local optima through iterative self-play across ideation, experimentation, and automation. Translating experimental designs into reliable physical execution remains bottlenecked by static laboratory automation and unmodeled physical variability. To address this, we introduce VERified Infrastructure for Discovery Laboratories, or VERID, a foundational infrastructure designed to bridge the gap between the high-level reasoning of artificial intelligence (AI) agents and complex, error-prone physical realities of wet labs. VERID is a persistent, high-fidelity semantic digital twin of the laboratory workcell that provides four core services: queryable introspection for agentic reasoning, time-compressed simulation for protocol validation, comprehensive trace persistence for a complete scientific record, and a framework for safe execution.We present three case studies using aqueous dye and deionized water mixtures to validate the optical and fluidic foundations of VERID. These studies demonstrate how VERID enables pre-emptive detection of critical errors in experimental protocols, stateful mid-run error recovery and post-hoc identification of subtle physical failures. The simulation functions as a discrete accounting engine, allowing agents to safely and inexpensively explore experimental designs in silico and refine experimental strategies through iterative self-play. VERID also captures structured multimodal experimental data for downstream analysis, knowledge mining and agent training. We argue that this semantic layer is an essential prerequisite for deploying autonomous agents in the biochemical and biomedical sciences, ensuring data integrity, operational safety, and paving the way for tackling previously intractable grand challenges in biology.
  </details>

- **[Adaptive World-Models for Anomaly Recognition (AWARE): A Framework for Robot Dynamics Prediction and Introspection](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32742)**  `arXiv:2610.32742`  
  _Luke Beddow, George Mavroghenis, Rodrigo Chacon Quesada, Kamil Dreczkowski, Cong Sun, Jiankai Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Unrecognized anomalies in critical infrastructure can rapidly cascade into catastrophic physical or operational failures. Current anomaly detection approaches often rely on direct access to the monitored system by utilizing onboard sensors, such as motor encoders, to detect anomalous states. However, legacy hardware often lacks these sensors, or strict security protocols may prohibit access altogether. We therefore present AWARE, a world model framework that is capable of anomaly detection using only external camera monitoring via system dynamics estimation and state prediction. Evaluated across 240 real-world robotic crane trajectories, including payload collisions and motor degradations, AWARE demonstrates a 26% improvement in anomaly classification over our strongest baseline under noisy, camera-only state estimation. Furthermore, the inferred dynamics parameter can be decoded to identify the source of faults, localizing the degrading motor with 88% accuracy.
  </details>

- **[Conditional Neural Processes for Self-Supervised Goal Discovery in Action Imitation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32688)**  `arXiv:2610.32688`  
  _Marco Gabriele Fedozzi, Yukie Nagai, Francesco Rea, Alessandra Sciutti_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Inspired by infants' ability to learn novel tasks by imitating others, in this work we evaluate to what extent Conditional Neural Processes can autonomously learn representations of actions that are informative about their underlying goal. We show that the addition of an attention mechanism in the hidden space fosters the emergence of robust goal representations early on in the observation of an action sequence, making it a promising model for a robotic self-supervised general learner.
  </details>

- **[Monte Carlo-Based Indoor UAV Localization in Dynamic Environments Using Only Four Onboard Range Sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32645)**  `arXiv:2610.32645`  
  _Mohammad Reza Ranjbar Divkoti, A. Pedro Aguiar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Indoor UAV localization with minimal onboard sensing remains challenging, particularly in dynamic environments. We propose augmented filtered Monte Carlo localization (AF-MCL) for 2D localization using only four single-point range sensors and measured heading. AF-MCL rejects range measurements inconsistent with the known static map and adaptively injects particles to recover from localization divergence and kidnapping. Beam classification is performed only at the previous position estimate, making its ray-casting cost independent of the particle count. The particle distribution additionally provides an online measure of localization uncertainty. Real-world experiments in a 2.10x1.63 m environment yield mean position errors of 6.4-7.7 cm under static and dynamic conditions. Simulations in 6x6 m and 10x10 m environments yield 4.5-5.2 cm mean errors; under dynamic obstacles, standard MCL reaches 12.3 cm and reliable MCL 7.7 cm. The results show that robust indoor localization under dynamic obstacles is achievable with only four single-point range measurements and heading.
  </details>

- **[Natural-Language to Nav2: Operational Policies for Costmap-Based Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32633)**  `arXiv:2610.32633`  
  _Jiyeong Chae, Hyunkyo Seo, Heonjae Lee, Kyung-Joon Park_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Autonomous mobile robots support material transport in warehouses and industrial facilities, where navigation must adapt to changing operating conditions. Sensor observations reveal physical obstacles, but do not by themselves specify temporary access rules, route preferences, or exceptions known to operators. This paper presents a natural-language policy interface that brings this knowledge into ROS 2 Nav2. A local large language model converts instructions into JSON policies specifying destinations, spatial constraints, and temporal or event conditions. Deterministic checks govern mission acceptance, and three costmap adapters project accepted policies into the existing navigation stack without changing its planner or controller. On 150 researcher-authored English commands, four local models produce 107-142 correct accepted policies, compared with 44 for a rule-based parser, with 3-20 incorrect acceptances per model. Fault injection records rejection of eight endpoint/JSON faults and 29 validator-stage faults. Navigation experiments use correct task policies to evaluate keepouts, zone costs, person-dependent regions, and event-conditioned passage in simulation and on a TurtleBot3. For Tasks 1-3, mean travel times are 18.4-24.5% lower in simulation and 23.1-35.0% lower on the physical testbed than with the baseline lacking those policies. A native KeepoutFilter comparison and two-robot demonstrations examine basic keepout behavior and shared policy projection. The interface connects operator instructions to explicit navigation policies while retaining Nav2's planning and control components.
  </details>

- **[OWLS: Open-world Language-conditioned Speed Fields for Zero-Shot Rover Navigation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32596)**  `arXiv:2610.32596`  
  _Chahyon Ku, Erica Tevere, Georgios Georgakis, Rudra Mukherjee, Bernadette Bucher_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Semantics can provide a proxy for physical traversability. Terrain that looks like loose sand or a rock field is likely to have similar physical properties. Open-vocabulary vision-language models (VLMs) enable this physical reasoning over terrain outside any fixed label set. We present Open-world Language-conditioned Speed Fields (OWLS), which plans directly over the dense feature map of a VLM. Prompted with a description of traversable terrain, the VLM scores every pixel of a single camera image; OWLS projects these scores onto the ground, adapts them to the platform with two morphological operations, and uses the result as the speed field of a minimum-time planner, so terrain appearance and obstacle clearance shape one trajectory. OWLS needs no task-specific training, no terrain annotation, no persistent map, and no exteroceptive sensor beyond the camera. We evaluate it on a physical rover indoors and off-road, on simulated Mars and Moon rovers over 200 procedurally generated terrains, and on real planetary imagery.
  </details>

- **[UAV-SwarmAgent: Scalable Multi-UAV Reconnaissance and Task Allocation via Code Generation and Vision-Language Reasoning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32559)**  `arXiv:2610.32559`  
  _Oleg Sautenkov, Yasheerah Yaqoot, Muhammad Ahsan Mustafa, Grigorii Kalinin, Chih-Yung Wen, Dzmitry Tsetserukou_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Supervising a multi-UAV reconnaissance mission usually means plotting waypoints and watching every drone. We present UAV SwarmAgent, a hierarchical multi-agent system in which an operator states the mission in one natural-language sentence and a ground-station agent writes and executes code that turns it into per-UAV routes. The agent grounds inspection targets in an aerial map with a vision-language model, splits them among N UAVs by balancing route length, and dispatches worker agents that verify incidents with a compact onboard decision model. In simulation over 4 incident types and 3 foundation models, the swarm located the incident in 244 of 255 missions (95.7%). A paired scaling study with one to 5 UAVs shows that flight time follows an Amdahl-type law. The second UAV delivers half of the attainable saving and the speedup is bounded at 1.52x. We discuss what this predictability, and the false alarms we observed, imply for an operator who supervises the swarm, and outline the user study needed to test it.
  </details>

- **[HGWM: Hierarchical Graph-Guided World Model for Zero-Shot Object Navigation via Graph Matching](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32554)**  `arXiv:2610.32554`  
  _I-tak Ieong, Hao Tang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Zero-shot object navigation requires an embodied agent to choose where to search while retaining evidence from previous observations. HGWM couples an LLM-generated room-anchor prior with persistent spatial-semantic memory. A coarse stage assigns visual-semantic scores to candidate headings on a 0-10 scale; a fine stage measures symbolic compatibility between observed objects, room context, and the goal prior. Phase-dependent fusion combines these scores with an LLM direction recommendation before local action selection. On HM3D v0.1, the reported HGWM configuration achieves 62.6% success rate (SR) and 31.5% success weighted by path length (SPL), compared with 58.1% and 31.2% for WMNav. On MP3D, the corresponding values are 46.8% and 17.9%, compared with 45.4% and 17.2%. The results indicate higher success point estimates and modest path-efficiency gains. On HM3D v0.2, the full model obtains 74.4% SR and 31.9% SPL. Additional component ablations reduce SR by 0.9-2.3 percentage points and SPL by 0.6-1.5 points. These are author-reported aggregate measurements. The accompanying implementation repairs pass offline tests; episode-level rerun records are not available. Evaluation is confined to simulation.
  </details>

- **[Optimization, Optimal Control, and Reinforcement Learning for Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32538)**  `arXiv:2610.32538`  
  _Yonghyeon Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  These lecture notes provide an introduction to optimization, optimal control, and reinforcement learning for robotics, emphasizing their mathematical connections and practical relevance. They present established material compiled and edited by Yonghyeon Lee, rather than new research results. The notes were developed through a human and AI collaborative writing process, with AI assistance in drafting, organizing, and refining the exposition. This first version is a preliminary collection of notes, not yet a fully developed textbook, and currently contains no figures or graphs. The notes may be revised, corrected, and expanded in future versions.
  </details>

- **[Facultative Swarming: A Capability, Not a Category, for Multi-Robot Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32526)**  `arXiv:2610.32526`  
  _Ning Zhou, Edmund R. Hunt_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Collective robotics is split by a long-standing but increasingly artificial boundary between centralised multi-robot systems that prioritise optimality and decentralised swarms that prioritise robustness and adaptivity. We propose a unified control framework, shifting from `obligate' swarms---systems constrained to pure self-organisation---to facultatively swarming systems: hybrid architectures that operate as centrally planned systems under permissive conditions yet degrade gracefully into decentralised swarming under environmental stress. By reframing swarming as a dynamic capability, rather than a rigid system category, we outline a pathway towards collective robotic systems that benefit from centralised optimality when conditions permit and from the flexibility of self-organisation when they do not.
  </details>

- **[From Freehand Demonstrations to Autonomous Robotic Ultrasound: An Anatomy-Anchored Learning Framework](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32525)**  `arXiv:2610.32525`  
  _Yuan Bi, Fangxu Han, Ye Su, Yiping Zhou, Zhongliang Jiang, Nassir Navab_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Ultrasound imaging is highly operator-dependent, motivating robotic systems that reproduce expert scanning skills consistently. These skills involve coordinated interpretation of anatomical landmarks, ultrasound images, and probe-tissue interactions that are difficult to encode in hand-designed control strategies. Imitation learning offers an alternative, but its observation and action representations should reflect the anatomically structured nature of scanning. We propose an anatomy-anchored imitation-learning framework that combines anatomy-guided probe placement with learned ultrasound navigation. The placement module identifies a task-specific anatomical starting point from external visual observations and establishes the initial probe pose as the reference frame for scanning. A flow-based policy then generates scanning motions from anchor-relative probe poses, ultrasound images, probe-mounted camera views, and contact forces, while a termination head predicts task completion. This avoids continuous reliance on third-person vision and reduces dependence on the robot-patient configuration. A handheld acquisition platform enables multimodal demonstrations to be collected during natural freehand scanning without a robot. On 16 unseen participants, task success reaches 96%, 94%, 88%, and 94% for kidney, gallbladder, and liver standard-plane acquisition and radial artery sweeping, respectively. Compared with a fine-tuned pi_0 model, success on the abdominal tasks improves by 10-42 percentage points, with lower time-to-target, peak contact force, and trajectory jerk. Ablations support the complementary roles of anatomical anchoring, egocentric vision, and force feedback. End-to-end trials with automatic placement achieve 90-100% success, and the policies transfer without retraining to a second robot with 83-100% success.
  </details>

- **[CoCarry: Contact-Support-Structured Retargeting for Dual-Humanoid Cooperative Carrying](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32460)**  `arXiv:2610.32460`  
  _Ziyu Lin, Shihang Du, Tianhang Wang, Xiaodong Liu, Runze Zhang, Zongyou Yu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent advances in large-scale human motion data, motion retargeting, and motion imitation have enabled increasingly capable whole-body control for individual humanoids. Cooperative carrying is more challenging because successful coordination requires not only plausible individual motions, but also consistent cross-agent and agent-object relations, including shared grasp, relative configuration, and whole-body support. We introduce CoCarry, a cooperative-carrying dataset containing 220 synchronized two-person sequences across 21 task schemes, five shared-object geometries, four terrain types, and approximately 61 minutes of motion. We further propose Contact-Support-Structured Retargeting, which starts from nominal individual motions and jointly refines both humanoids around the same prescribed object trajectory. Cooperation-aware object-local hand anchors preserve compatible four-hand grasp relations, while complementary upper object-interaction and lower support structures retain object-relative geometry and coordinated support. Across all 220 CoCarry sequences retargeted to two A3 humanoids, our method achieves 0.9995 contact preservation, reduces penetration duration by 67.0% and 71.0% relative to GMR and OmniRetarget, respectively, and maintains a 100% retargeting success rate. On CORE4D with Unitree G1 humanoids, it achieves 0.9906 contact preservation with zero measured sliding. We further validate the generated references through physics simulation and multiple real-world cooperative carrying experiments on two physical A3 humanoids, demonstrating their usability for downstream dual-humanoid control.
  </details>

- **[$R^2E:$ A Role-driven Reward Evolutionary Framework for Automated Reward Function Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32439)**  `arXiv:2610.32439`  
  _Shouhao Chang, Xuan Liu, Hongye Zhu, Xinning Chen, Shigeng Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Designing effective reward functions is a critical challenge in Reinforcement Learning (RL), which traditionally requires costly manual trial-and-error. While Large Language Model (LLM)-based methods have shown promise in reward design, undifferentiated sampling and limited use of historical feedback often lead to insufficient exploration of the reward space and optimization instability. To address these challenges, we propose $R^2E$, a Role-driven Reward Evolutionary framework for automated reward function design that structures the process through explicit role specialization. $R^2E$ employs multiple LLM roles with complementary objectives: an Explorer that promotes novelty to expand the search space, a Guardian that performs conservative refinements to improve training stability, and an Evolver that recombines high-performing rewards via crossover and mutation to integrate effective structures. A dynamic scheduling strategy coordinates these roles, progressively shifting the search from broad exploration to focused exploitation. To mitigate optimization instability and provide a medium for cross-role collaboration, $R^2E$ incorporates a Global Elite Pool that retains the best-performing rewards to guide subsequent generations, thereby enabling coordinated interaction among roles and ensuring a reliable and consistent refinement process. Extensive experiments on multiple robotic tasks demonstrate the effectiveness of our method.
  </details>

- **[Graphs of Trajectory Bundles for Nonlinear Motion Planning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32365)**  `arXiv:2610.32365`  
  _Joris Verhagen, Jon Arrizabalaga, Jana Tumova, Zachary Manchester_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  We present the Graphs of Trajectory Bundles (GTB) method for motion planning of systems with nonlinear and black-box dynamics, combining sampling-based motion planning with trajectory optimization and graph search. Based on a graph-of-convex-sets decomposition of the workspace, GTB iteratively constructs a graph of trajectory bundles that captures the local dynamics of the system at any vertex. By solving a shortest-path problem on this graph, we find maximally dynamically feasible iterates that satisfy the system's dynamics in the limit and any constraints such as collision avoidance or additional safety or specification constraints by construction of the underlying graph. As a result, GTB can handle highly nonlinear, unstable, and even black-box dynamics with complex collision and specification constraints, making it a powerful tool for motion planning of complex systems.
  </details>

- **[LogLook: Execution-Grounded Memory for Frontier-Model Robot Planners](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32348)**  `arXiv:2610.32348`  
  _Tianyi Jiang, Tianyao Tong, Zhenshan Bing_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Many manipulation tasks require a robot to remember what it has done and what it has seen. Frontier vision-language models can plan such tasks without task-specific training, but how such a planner should remember an episode is an open question. We argue that its memory must keep track of what the planner has done and learned, and must let the planner choose what to look at, since every frame costs tokens; a note rewritten at every step meets both poorly. We propose LogLook, whose execution-grounded memory lets the system keep the record, an execution log and the model's earlier notes, while the model decides where to look: frames are returned only where it expects a change, and demonstrations are skimmed before it inspects the parts it chooses. Measured with the benchmark's motion planner, LogLook matches the success of a note-only memory with 43% fewer tokens and less than half the images, and fails far fewer episodes through what it remembered or saw. With direct control, LogLook solves 93.5% of the RoboMME test set.
  </details>

- **[DynaSlots: Slot-Centric 3D Dynamics Pretraining for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32330)**  `arXiv:2610.32330`  
  _Meng Li, Lijiang Chen, Qi Zhao, Jun Jiang, Longhao Zou, Shuchang Lyu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot manipulation involves localized changes: picking up a cup moves the cup while leaving the surrounding scene largely unchanged. Yet existing 3D visual pretraining methods, including recent dynamics-aware variants, model state transitions at the scene level, compressing the motion of everything in a scene into a single global latent action. This conflates semantically distinct transitions in scenes with multiple moving parts, obscures dependencies between changing regions, and leaves the learned representation vulnerable to task-irrelevant scene content. We introduce DynaSlots, an action-free, label-free pretraining framework that decomposes each point cloud frame into a small set of temporally consistent slots and models dynamics at this per-slot level rather than the scene level. A temporal slot propagation module promotes consistent slot assignments across frames; an inverse dynamics model then extracts one latent action per slot from feature differences, and a diffusion-based forward dynamics model, paired with a self-supervised interaction head, predicts each slot's future state while learning pairwise proximity-and-motion relations. Experiments on Meta-World, Adroit, and a RealMan RM65-B robot demonstrate that DynaSlots outperforms prior scene-level baselines. It achieves superior success rates in simulation and exhibits strong robustness to scene clutter and unseen objects in real-world manipulation. Code is available at \href{this https URL}{this https URL}.
  </details>

- **[MAST-VLA: Skill-Conditioned Adaptation with Trajectory-Affordance Priors for Multi-Skill Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32312)**  `arXiv:2610.32312`  
  _Jiachi Yang, Zhiyi Xu, Hongquan Zhang, Xin Tan, Zhizhong Zhang, Jingyu Gong, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generalist robot manipulation requires a single vision-language-action (VLA) policy to execute diverse skills across scenes and instructions. However, current multi-skill VLA policies often leave two aspects of an instruction insufficiently modeled: which primitive skill to execute and where/how to contact and move objects. We propose MAST-VLA (Multi-skill Adaptation with Skill-conditioned experts and Trajectory-affordance priors), a dual-conditioning framework for robot manipulation. MAST-VLA introduces skill-conditioned LoRA Mixture-of-Experts (LoRA-MoE) adapters in the LLM backbone, where skill-query tokens and routing biases guide low-rank expert specialization, and converts a predicted affordance heatmap into a patch-level trajectory-affordance spatial prior for the action head. On the CALVIN ABC$\rightarrow$D long-horizon multi-skill cross-scene generalization setting, MAST-VLA achieves state-of-the-art performance, reaching an Avg. Len of 4.65 over five-subtask evaluation sequences. Real-robot multi-skill sequence experiments further validate its deployability under real perception and control noise.
  </details>

- **[Delay Randomization Widens the Delay Margin of Learned Humanoid Motion Trackers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32277)**  `arXiv:2610.32277`  
  _Shaoqin Li, Yi Sun_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Learned humanoid controllers are usually trained in one simulator and then checked in a second one before they go near hardware. We ask what that check says about command delay. We train motion-tracking policies for a 24-joint humanoid on five motions taken from monocular video and test them in an independent CPU MuJoCo harness, which we first verify against the training model. Every policy completes its motion in at least 26 of 30 episodes under the training randomization. With 40 ms of constant command delay, however, survival drops to 15% on average (0% - 45%), and the delay margin is only 20 - 30 ms. Randomizing the delay during training (0 - 50 ms) lifts survival at 40 ms to 95% on average (75% - 100%) and moves the margin to 50 - 70 ms. A control arm trained for the same number of extra iterations without delay does not improve, so the gain comes from the randomization itself. This holds for all five motions with three training seeds each, and a policy trained in PhysX behaves the same way. The price is slightly looser tracking (0.02 - 0.06 rad on four motions) and, for the most dynamic motion, less robustness to other perturbations. We also show that the first sim-to-sim gap we saw came from the harness, a missing floor and a wrong timestep, and not from the physics engines. The 0 - 20 ms delay ranges common in locomotion training lie inside the margin an unrandomized tracker already has. The benefit comes from randomizing beyond it, so the range should be set from the robot's measured latency, and delay margin belongs next to other sim-to-sim metrics.
  </details>

- **[From Tabletop to Walking Bot: Deploying a VLA Model on a Legged Manipulator Robot](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32271)**  `arXiv:2610.32271`  
  _Giovanni Minelli, Antonio Langella, Giovanni B. Dessy, Lorenzo Amatucci, Giulio Turrisi, Victor Barasuol, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-language-action (VLA) models are typically pretrained on broad, diverse datasets and subsequently fine-tuned on a comparatively small dataset tailored to a specific target robot. Such adaptation recipe is now widely established, achieving its most mature results on fixed-base arms due to their static base dynamics and consistent reference frame. Mounting the same arm on a quadruped extends the workspace to the entire floor, but fundamentally changes the embodiment: rather than controlling the arm in isolation, the policy must jointly coordinate base navigation, body posture, and end-effector motion. In this work, we study the practical demands of this embodiment change across data collection, model adaptation, and inference design to achieve closed-loop loco-manipulation on a Unitree Go2 with an AgileX Piper arm. We report the choices that made this real-robot deployment feasible, namely model variant, action horizon, and demonstration conventions, together with a study tailored to a pick-and-place task, separating data coverage from model capacity. We show that expanding demonstration coverage reduces held-out end-effector error by 30%, and incorporating secondary skills (such as pushing rather than grasping) introduces new behavioral modes without degrading baseline performance. Finally, closed-loop real-world experiments show how dataset composition shapes success and failure, validating the pipeline for more complex loco-manipulation.
  </details>

- **[RoboCode: Learning Reusable Action Primitives for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32225)**  `arXiv:2610.32225`  
  _Xianyue Peng, Devi Amarsaikhan, Iman Soltani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Recent robot manipulation policies commonly predict continuous action chunks directly from observations, without explicitly modeling reusable structure in the action space. However, manipulation demonstrations often contain recurring short-horizon motion patterns that are shared across tasks. We introduce \textsc{RoboCode}, which learns these patterns through a shared soft-gated primitive action interface. Rather than assigning each action chunk to a single discrete code, \textsc{RoboCode} represents it as a soft composition of multiple learned primitives, providing a flexible intermediate representation of short-horizon actions. The primitive interface is learned from action chunks pooled across multiple tasks and then frozen while task-specific state encoders are trained to map current state inputs into the shared latent space.Experiments on five ManiSkill manipulation tasks address three questions. First, the learned primitive interface reconstructs action chunks with an average reconstruction MSE of 0.0187. Second, analysis of code activations reveals structured code reuse across tasks. Third, \textsc{RoboCode} achieves the highest or tied closed-loop success rate on all evaluated tasks, with an average success rate of 75.6%, compared with 68.8% for ACT and 41.6% for the larger Diffusion Policy baseline. These results suggest that soft-gated primitive codebooks provide an effective and interpretable intermediate action representation for robot manipulation.
  </details>

- **[Do VLAs Know They Will Fail? Interpretable Success and Failure Patterns in Physical AI Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32208)**  `arXiv:2610.32208`  
  _Christian A. Rogers, Qian Lin, Daniel S. Brown_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models have emerged as powerful general-purpose robot policies, but their successes and failures remain difficult to diagnose and understand. In this work, we show that sparse autoencoder (SAE) representations of OpenVLA activations contain outcome-predictive information and can be used to predict task failure before the robot reaches an unrecoverable state. Moreover, to provide insights into what common behaviors may contribute to these outcomes, we identify individual SAE features whose strong activations are associated with specific stages of success or failure, and uncover recurring behavioral patterns by localizing temporal intervals where these features exhibit significant activation changes. We further develop a VLM-based interpretation pipeline to generate behavioral descriptions of these patterns and validate their correspondence to feature activations on held-out intervals.
  </details>

- **[RAISE-Vision: An Automated Contact Angle Measurement Platform for Contact Lens Wettability Characterization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32188)**  `arXiv:2610.32188`  
  _Erin Ng, Mohammad Nazeri, Julie S Liu, Tithi Choksi, Lyndon Jones, Frank Gu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contact angle (CA) is a common parameter measured in the development of novel contact lens materials and surface coating technologies. Existing literature on in vitro CA of contact lenses via the sessile drop method is hindered in repeatability, reliability, and comparability due to inter-laboratory and inter-operator protocol variation. In this work, the Robotic Autonomous Imaging Surface Evaluator for vision applications (RAISE-Vision) is introduced as an automated platform for CA measurement on curved hydrogel surfaces, with specific application to in vitro contact lens wettability analysis throughout air exposure. Customized 3D printed hardware, such as lens mounts, camera holders and drying apparatuses, was designed to facilitate an automated repeated sessile drop workflow within a liquid handler. Collected images were directly fed to a custom open-source image processing script for CA determination on a curved surface that combines the elliptical fit and tangent methods to allow for variations in droplet shape. The CA of senofilcon A contact lenses measured using the RAISE-Vision platform increased from $62.6 \pm 5.5^\circ$ to $83.4 \pm 2.1^\circ$ over 10 sequential measurements in air and was able to replicate results obtained using an analogous manual protocol ($p = 0.470$). The custom image processing script was also separately demonstrated to generate comparable results to a commercially available CA determination software ($p = 0.343$). Aside from minimizing operator variability for CA measurement, RAISE-Vision provides a platform for standardized in vitro contact lens wettability analysis and understanding of surface wetting behaviour during air exposure.
  </details>

- **[Geometry-Aware Reinforcement Learning for Close-Fitting Wearable Insertion](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32143)**  `arXiv:2610.32143`  
  _Takuma Tsukakoshi, Elle Miller, Tamon Miyake, Namiko Saito, Yushi Wang, Shigeki Sugano, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot-assisted dressing is particularly challenging due to narrow openings and articulated body parts. Even small errors in the relative pose between the body part and garment opening can cause snagging, missed insertion, or incomplete dressing. To address these challenges, we formulate dressing as a geometry-aware insertion problem that represents task-relevant spatial relationships between the body part and object opening, rather than relying on the full deformable-object state. Based on this formulation, we propose a Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework combining a staged curriculum, which progressively guides the policy from coarse alignment to insertion and final placement, with a finger-wise insertion soft gate that provides continuous feedback on individual finger insertion progress and helps suppress premature insertion. We evaluate the framework on rigid and deformable bracelet dressing tasks through component ablations, systematic variations in the initial hand pose, and closed-loop execution of the learned policies on a real robotic system. On the post-training deformable evaluation, the full method reaches 84.7 +- 1.2% success. The insertion gate accounts for the largest gain, and adding the curriculum on top of that gate reaches the top score. On a mannequin hand the same full policy succeeds on 15/20 real-robot trials.
  </details>

- **[TravSUITE: Traversability via Self-Supervised, Uncertainty-Aware IRL and Terrain Estimation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32130)**  `arXiv:2610.32130`  
  _Samuel Triest, Amirreza Shaban, David D. Fan, Wenshan Wang, Sebastian Scherer_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Traversability analysis in off-road settings remains a fundamental challenge for mobile robots. Key difficulties include constructing an accurate and expressive local map from multimodal sensor data, and using the map to design traversability rules that yield desirable navigation behavior. Importantly, this system must be resilient to the limited sensing regime brought about by complex environments and high speeds. In this paper, we present TravSUITE, a traversability system suitable for high-speed navigation in off-road environments. TravSUITE consists of two major components: 1) an efficient voxel mapper that leverages visual foundation models (VFMs) to build a rich geometric-semantic local map from streams of on-board sensor data, and 2) a unified neural network that jointly predicts traversability-relevant quantities in bird's eye view (BEV), including geometry, semantics, speed and cost. Our training strategy is entirely annotation-free and self-supervised, leveraging tasks such as map inpainting and inverse reinforcement learning (IRL) to learn both map representations and traversability. We perform a thorough ablation study and comparison to state-of-the-art approaches, and the results indicate that cost learning and auxiliary inpainting each contribute significantly to planning quality, and their combination is critical for achieving state-of-the-art performance in off-road path planning. We also design a simple risk adaptation mechanism to leverage our method's uncertainty estimates at deploy-time, and demonstrate that a combination of inpainting and risk estimation can result in 80% fewer navigation errors and 5% faster autonomous traversal speeds in real-world hardware experiments.
  </details>

- **[Coupling Execution Horizon and Sampling Depth in Flow-Matching VLA Policies](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32102)**  `arXiv:2610.32102`  
  _Jiabing Yang, Yuan Xu, Yixiang Chen, Qisen Ma, Peiyan Li, Kai Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Flow-matching Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models generate expressive continuous trajectories for robotic manipulation, yet balancing real-time responsiveness with execution stability remains a persistent challenge. Standard deployment workflows typically treat numerical solver depth and execution horizon as decoupled choices. The horizon amortizes inference latency, while solver depth is kept high to preserve integration fidelity. In this paper, we reveal a fundamental compute-execution coupling in flow-matching VLAs governed by a systematic sign reversal. While coarse numerical integration degrades precision under rapid replanning, it markedly improves task performance under extended open-loop commitment. Geometrically, numerical truncation contracts lateral action dispersion around an invariant trajectory centroid, serving as an implicit regularizer that suppresses compounding open-loop drift. Across LIBERO, RoboTwin 2.0, and real-world Franka manipulation on representative VLA backbones including $\pi_0$, $\pi_{0.5}$, and GR00T, exploiting this joint coupling consistently achieves superior task success while drastically lowering inference latency, entirely training-free and without architectural modifications. These findings establish numerical sampling depth as an operational variance regulator, shifting its role from a static precision budget to an active deployment knob in robot learning. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[TTT in VLA Models: Exploring Training with Human Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32094)**  `arXiv:2610.32094`  
  _Qiangong Zhou, Jiafeng Liang, Yimin Dong, Yang He, Hongxiang Li_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Long-horizon robot tasks require retaining and using past information. We integrate Test-Time Training (TTT), which stores context in fast weights, into InternVLA-A1 and Pi0.5 to study action generation conditioned on human demonstrations through training on paired demonstrations and robot trajectories. We implement memory transfer between the two phases and truncated backpropagation, and examine training objectives, gradient coverage, and contrastive regularization across video, short-video, and single-frame cue tasks. In our experiments, adding TTT and training on paired data at the current scale did not lead to consistent target selection based on demonstrations. Under the tested settings, shorter sequences, full trajectory gradient connectivity, and contrastive regularization did not clearly improve responsiveness to demonstrations. When demonstrations were reduced to single-frame cues, real-robot execution still showed a preference for cups at fixed positions. We document the implementation, training configurations, and observations to support further inspection and replication.
  </details>

- **[DiscoDemo: Discovering Efficient and Diverse Robot Demonstrations for Imitation Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.32093)**  `arXiv:2610.32093`  
  _Minho Park, Kinam Kim, Donghu Kim, Byungkun Lee, Dongyoon Hwang, Yongjae Shin, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Behavior cloning for robotic manipulation is highly sensitive to the coverage of its training data, yet collecting diverse successful demonstrations remains costly. We study reinforcement learning (RL) not as a deployable controller, but as a data generator for downstream visuomotor imitation learning. Our key premise is that multiple successful solution modes cover a broader range of successful trajectories than repeatedly collecting a single narrow solution. We introduce DiscoDemo, which trains a privileged latent-conditioned RL generator with a parallel reverse curriculum and a success-gated diversity objective to discover diverse solutions while preserving task success. The trained generator produces multiple successful closed-loop behaviors, which are safety-filtered and exported as a static dataset for downstream imitation learning. Across four manipulation tasks, DiscoDemo produces broader successful-state coverage and improves downstream visuomotor policies in both simulation and the real world. Averaged across tasks, downstream success increases to 62% from 40% for the strongest baseline in simulation, and to 78% from 39% on the real robot, respectively. These results suggest that successful solution diversity improves imitation learning for a pretrained flow-matching VLA and remains complementary to corrective data collection. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Robotic Budding: From Homogeneous Cells to Diverse Morphologies in Physical Self-Reproduction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31929)**  `arXiv:2610.31929`  
  _Siyuan Zhang, Junyan Liu, Wanglin He, Hod Lipson_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Physical self-reproduction in modular robots remains difficult because a robot must not only move, but also collect, align, dock, and separate modules reliably in hardware. Taking inspiration from how genetically identical cells form the varied tissues of an organism, we introduce a single standardized robotic module that combines a magnetic docking interface with two actuated joints and folds from a flat, two-dimensional configuration into three-dimensional structures. Modules of this single design can be assembled into two morphologies: a three-module "trilobite" and a five-module "seal". Using a small set of discrete motion commands, a human operator steers an assembled parent robot to dock magnetically with a sequence of spare modules, which are assembled into a second, structurally identical individual that then separates from the parent. Because the parent retains all of its original modules, this is a budding-style form of physical reproduction. Here, we report preliminary hardware demonstrations of this full process on both morphologies. We discuss the current scope of the system, including its present reliance on direct human teleoperation rather than autonomous control, and outline directions toward autonomous execution of this process and repeated validation across a larger number of independent trials.
  </details>

- **[Input Bias Correction in Gaussian Process Regression](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31920)**  `arXiv:2610.31920`  
  _Muzaffar Qureshi, Tochukwu E. Ogri, Trivikram Satharasi, Kyle Volle, Rushikesh Kamalapurkar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  In traditional Gaussian process (GP) regression, the inputs are typically assumed to be known, and uncertainty enters only through the measurements. When scalar fields are mapped using GP regression with measurements obtained from a mobile sensor, the inputs correspond to the estimated pose of the mobile sensor. The goal in this letter is to understand how a recorded GP, trained on erroneous inputs (e.g., pose estimates from dead-reckoning), can be corrected in a numerically efficient way when better estimates of the inputs become available. In the developed bias-aware GP (Ba-GP) framework, the recorded GP posterior-mean gradient is used to propagate estimated input bias and noise into mean and covariance corrections. The resulting additive covariance structure is then exploited to develop numerically efficient first-order correction algorithms. A simulation study and an indoor light-intensity mapping experiment show that Ba-GP improves predictive accuracy and uncertainty calibration relative to noisy-input GP baselines, with an order-of-magnitude reduction in computational cost relative to full GP retraining.
  </details>

- **[Distributed Swarm Morphogenesis with Neural Cellular Automata](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31900)**  `arXiv:2610.31900`  
  _Ray Verma, Eliseo Ferrante, Anthony Tzes_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Swarm morphogenesis requires robots to assemble desired shapes through local interactions. We present a modular framework integrating neural cellular automata (NCA) with existing site selection and collision-aware control. Within this framework, we introduce a decentralized attachment-site discovery mechanism. Robots already in the formation update and share local NCA states to identify new attachment sites, enabling progressive growth without a predefined list of sites during deployment. To bridge the mismatch between conventional NCA growth and gradual physical assembly, we additionally propose offline sparse-retention fine-tuning that simulates partial attachment, together with a false-positive loss that penalizes invalid site activations. Simulations demonstrate formation of diverse lattice morphologies, scaling to several hundred robots, and substantial improvements in morphology accuracy from the proposed fine-tuning. Experiments with Crazyflie quadrotors validate the integrated execution pipeline using robot-relative observations reconstructed from external tracking.
  </details>

- **[Move, don't think: sample-efficient collision avoidance with lossy proximity sensors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31891)**  `arXiv:2610.31891`  
  _Carson Kohlbrenner, Niraj Pudasaini, William Xie, Naren Sivagnanadasan, Nikolaus Correll, Alessandro Roncone_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Data-driven robots must learn to avoid collisions with as few demonstrations as possible to avoid collecting large datasets of destructive collision examples. We explore whole-body proximity sensors as inductive biases for learning reflexive collision-dodging behavior and find that lossy sensors with low resolution were stronger biases than high-resolution coverage. We also found that, perhaps unintuitively, anticipating when and where contact happens on the body of a robot reduced its learning sample efficiency. We investigated eight candidate representations in a reinforcement learning environment, which included latent compression, future contact anticipation, lossy corruption, and privileged state estimation. The candidate representations were tested by training over 200 humanoid robots to play a simplified version of dodgeball end-to-end under an intentionally constrained sample and compute budget.
  </details>

- **[Emergent Cooperative Thermoregulation in Lunar Swarm Robotics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31868)**  `arXiv:2610.31868`  
  _Anais Jeger, Harry Holt, Lennart Puck_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Swarms of small robots are an attractive way to survey lunar caves and lava tubes. Missions such as NASA's CADRE will fly cooperating rovers for distributed mapping, but only in daylight. The 354 h lunar night drops the surface towards 95 K, and the radioisotope heaters that let single rovers survive it cannot be supplied at swarm scale. In this study we investigate whether a swarm can instead use its own members as thermal shielding. We train one policy, shared by all twenty robots, by reinforcement learning with an evolution-strategies estimator. The policy receives a single reward, once, at the end of the night: the mean temperature of the five coldest robots. Nothing in that reward refers to position, spacing, exposure or motion. Two behaviors nonetheless emerge. The swarm aggregates, and robots circulate between the sheltered core and the exposed rim, the same pair of behaviors with which emperor penguins survive the Antarctic winter. We measure the survival threshold, the smallest stored energy per robot at which every robot is still alive at sunrise. Across ten runs it is 155.6 +/- 1.3 pcm-h, where one pcm-h is 7.2 kJ of stored heat. That is 37% below a motionless huddle and within 13% of a physics-based lower bound, and at a budget where the motionless huddle loses six robots, every robot survives. The behavior needs no shared spatial reference and no robot-robot contact, and transfers from twenty robots to forty without retraining.
  </details>

- **[Full-Weight VLA Fine-Tuning Without an fp32 Master Copy Silently Erases Most Updates](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31859)**  `arXiv:2610.31859`  
  _Shubham Singh_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  When a vision-language-action (VLA) model is fine-tuned with its trainable weights in bfloat16 (bf16) and no fp32 master copy, AdamW writes each update straight into bf16, silently rounding away any update smaller than 1/512 of a weight. In 9 of 12 open VLA codebases a default or documented recipe trains this way, 4 on full weight matrices, including LeRobot's SmolVLA default and its $\pi_0$/$\pi_{0.5}$ guides. In our LIBERO runs without the copy, a probe every 100 steps finds at least 79% of updates to bf16 weights erased. On the Hugging Face Hub, 99% of sampled full-weight SmolVLA fine-tunes store the action expert in bf16, and 84% of recent $\pi_{0.5}$ fine-tunes with a training configuration set the bf16 flag. On LIBERO-Object, with rules fixed in advance, an fp32 copy raises success over LeRobot's storage from 4.3% to 49.3% for SmolVLA and from 5.1% to 80.9% for $\pi_{0.5}$ at a ten-times lower learning rate, where both models are undertrained, and from 89.0% to 97.7% for $\pi_{0.5}$ at its default rate. For SmolVLA at its default rate it adds 10.2 points with open-loop 50-action chunks but only 4.2 when re-planning every 10 actions. In these five comparisons and two more against SmolVLA with every trainable weight in bf16, every run with the copy beats every run without it (p < 0.05); all gaps but the 8.7- and 4.2-point ones clear our 10-point threshold. The fix is to keep the trainable weights in fp32 during training, a one-line change for SmolVLA in LeRobot.
  </details>

- **[Multimodal MPC via Bayesian Set Optimization for Dynamic Manipulation of Deformable Linear Objects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31852)**  `arXiv:2610.31852`  
  _Youyuan Long, Gokhan Solak, Arash Ajoudani_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dynamic manipulation of deformable linear objects (DLOs) remains challenging due to underactuated, high-dimensional dynamics, delayed deformation responses, and the presence of multiple feasible motions under obstacle constraints. This paper presents BM2PC, a Bayesian Multimodal MPC framework for agile manipulation of DLOs. Building on the control-as-inference posterior, BM2PC formulates multimodal planning as a set optimization problem that balances control quality and behavioral diversity. An efficient incremental update maintains multiple promising control modes with low additional computational overhead. For real-world deployment, BM2PC incorporates an execution dynamics model that predicts actual end-effector motion and compensates for execution mismatch during fast manipulation. We evaluate BM2PC on rope tracking and obstacle avoidance in simulation and hardware, and on simulated disturbance rejection. In trajectory tracking, BM2PC generalizes better to unseen trajectories than a learning-based baseline without task-specific policy training or fine-tuning. Real-world experiments demonstrate accurate long-horizon tracking, reducing RMSE by 44.0% on the longest task. In obstacle avoidance, BM2PC achieves comparable performance to existing multimodal MPC methods while reducing planning time by 29% to 66%. These results demonstrate accurate dynamic control and efficient multimodal planning within a unified MPC framework. The core implementation of BM2PC and supplementary videos are available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[EgoForesight: Effectively Scaling Policy Learning with Noisy Egocentric Actions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31845)**  `arXiv:2610.31845`  
  _Chubin Zhang, Changyuan Wang, Yirui Wang, Ao Li, Shilin Ma, Yue Su, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling policy learning requires broad experience and reliable supervision. Robot demonstrations provide high-quality trajectory supervision but are costly to collect. Egocentric videos broaden task and environment coverage, yet motion reconstruction and retargeting introduce noisy action labels. To address this challenge, we introduce EgoForesight, a vision-language-action framework that learns jointly from both sources by reducing the influence of unreliable action labels and exploiting demonstrated outcomes as complementary supervision. A joint vision-action flow couples future visual representation prediction with action generation, unifying reliability-aware action learning with outcome-informed representation transfer. Specifically, reliability-conditioned corruption allocates action supervision across noise levels according to source reliability, while reliability-weighted regression regulates each source's contribution.
