Complementing this treatment of noisy labels, privileged-future representation distillation draws supervision from demonstrated outcomes. An exponential-moving-average teacher observes the current scene and clean future to encode action-relevant structure from the resulting scene change. Its deeper representations supervise an earlier student layer receiving a corrupted future, providing outcome-informed structure beyond noisy action annotations. Together, these mechanisms support effective scaling with noisy egocentric actions. Over the tested range, EgoForesight exhibits steeper scaling slopes than the baseline, reducing real-world open-loop action MSE by 20.5% and improving RoboCasa few-shot success by 8.1 percentage points at 30,000 hours of pretraining data. After post-training, it also achieves strong absolute performance in both simulated and real-world dexterous manipulation.
  </details>

- **[FORCA: A Force-Aware Coding Agent for Contact-Rich Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31841)**  `arXiv:2610.31841`  
  _Xiao Tan, Yixuan Wang, Zhexi Wang, Wenzhen Yuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Coding agents can translate language instructions into robot programs. For contact-rich manipulation, a key challenge is to generate and refine control logic that responds to physical interaction. We present FORCA, a coding agent that takes visual observations and wrist force/torque (F/T) measurements as input to write Python programs for contact probing and force-responsive motion. Between executions, the agent inspects force records alongside visual outcomes to diagnose failures and revise both motion and force-feedback control logic. FORCA requires no demonstrations, task-specific training, or simulation. Across five real-robot tasks, we demonstrate its effectiveness in three forms of contact-rich manipulation: improving manipulation accuracy, adapting to changes in object or surface properties, and regulating contact under surface variation and object motion. Compared with a vision-only variant of the same agent, FORCA improves grasp accuracy and task success and reduces contact loss in wiping and peeling.
  </details>

- **[WorldMirror: Agentic Real-to-Sim Generation of Interactive Worlds from In-the-Wild Robot Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31834)**  `arXiv:2610.31834`  
  _Tianling Xu, Weijie Wan, Honghao Su, Kailun Su, Junyuan Tang, Huichi Zhou, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Existing approaches for robot learning in simulation often require substantial manual effort to construct scenes and specify tasks, limiting training scalability and data diversity. Meanwhile, in-the-wild robot demonstrations naturally provide diverse and realistic observations of real-world interactions. Leveraging these demonstrations for simulation requires both high-fidelity visual reconstruction and physically consistent interactions. General-purpose agents offer flexible adaptation to diverse scenarios and strong reasoning about 3D spatial relationships, while specialized generative models provide high-fidelity reconstruction of scene appearance and geometry. We introduce WorldMirror, an agentic real-to-sim framework that constructs interactive digital twins from in-the-wild robot demonstrations. Our central idea is to formulate real-to-sim construction as the iterative refinement of an executable world program. A coding agent decomposes the observed scene, plans reconstruction steps, and orchestrates specialized image and 3D generation tools, refining the program through verification feedback. A camera-pose-anchored scene representation organizes the world into static visual context, support geometry, and editable interactive assets while preserving spatial relationships among the scene, objects, and robot. The agent uses this representation to diagnose and correct reconstruction errors, enabling physically consistent interaction and trajectory replay. Experiments on DROID demonstrate that WorldMirror can reconstruct visually faithful and geometrically consistent interactive worlds while preserving the physical validity of recorded robot trajectories.
  </details>

- **[SEVI: Safe Evidential Real-World Reinforcement Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31829)**  `arXiv:2610.31829`  
  _Shaekh Mohammad Shithil, Lantao Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Real-world reinforcement learning has enabled contact-rich manipulation tasks to be trained directly on physical robots, but unsafe interactions during online learning remain a key barrier to practical deployment. We propose Safe Evidential Real-World Reinforcement Learning (SEVI), a framework for reliability-aware safety intervention during real-world policy learning. SEVI introduces an Evidential Safety Critic that estimates both future constraint-violation risk and epistemic uncertainty in a single forward pass. During online learning, SEVI evaluates each task-policy action before execution; if the action is predicted to be unsafe or the prediction is insufficiently supported by evidence, SEVI overrides it with an offline-trained recovery policy that guides the robot toward a lower-risk region. We evaluate SEVI on two simulated manipulation tasks and a real-world USB insertion task under in-distribution and distribution-shifted conditions. Across task-learning and safety-critic baselines, SEVI achieves the strongest trajectory-level safety prediction, the highest final task success, and the lowest unsafe-event counts. SEVI also reduces unsafe events by 13.4 to 57.1 percent relative to recovery-based safe RL while preserving low-latency execution-time filtering. The project page is available at:this https URL
  </details>

- **[PrefO2O: Zero-Shot Preference-Based Reinforcement Learning via Offline-to-Online Latent Preference Transfer](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31812)**  `arXiv:2610.31812`  
  _Ruiqi Wang, Dezhong Zhao, Xuesu Xiao, Erdem Biyik, Byung-Cheol Min, Guohua Chen_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Preference-based reinforcement learning (PbRL) has emerged as a promising paradigm for learning robot behaviors aligned with human intent. However, its scalability is fundamentally limited by the need to collect expensive human preference labels for every new task. In this work, we present PrefO2O, an offline-to-online latent preference transfer framework that enables zero-shot PbRL on unseen target tasks without requiring any human feedback. The core idea is to learn a task-invariant latent preference space from a small amount of offline preference data on source tasks, where transferable preference structures are disentangled from task-specific variations. This learned manifold allows PrefO2O to transfer preference structures and reward priors to new tasks under distribution shift, while confidence-weighted optimal transport alignment generates reliable pseudo-preference supervision for online reward adaptation. Extensive experiments on robotic manipulation and locomotion benchmarks demonstrate that PrefO2O substantially outperforms existing preference-transfer baselines and matches or exceeds PbRL methods trained with real human feedback, while generalizing across multi-to-one and one-to-one transfer settings as well as across different robot embodiments.
  </details>

- **[KiRoSe: Using Kinematic Data for Efficient Segmentation of Robot Demonstrations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31801)**  `arXiv:2610.31801`  
  _Shuangyu Xie, Kaiyuan Chen, Muhammad Zubair Irshad, Richard Cheng, Sergey Zakharov, Ken Goldberg_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Segmenting robot demonstration data into disjoint temporal and semantic subsegments is valuable for learning, cleaning, retrieval, and analysis. Segmentation is often performed manually in a tedious and error prone process. Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have the potential for automating this process, but directly ingesting raw multi camera video streams into MLLMs can be costly and can quickly exhaust context windows. In this paper, we introduce KiRoSe (Kinematics for Robot Segmentation), a novel framework that focuses on the companion stream of robot kinematic data. Inspired by the principle of classical entropy coding, such as Huffman coding, where frequently occurring symbols are represented more compactly, we propose Sparse Motion Tokens (SMTs), a discrete kinematic vocabulary designed to compactly represent recurring, temporally localized motion patterns, which we refer to as kinematic events. SMTs are learned by encoding kinematic trajectories with temporal segment supervision and quantizing the resulting features. We use increasingly available robot demonstration subtask annotations to supervise an open language model to interpret the resulting token sequences. Evaluation against human labeled segmentation shows that KiRoSe can substantially outperform baseline segmentation methods in F1 quality and transfers zero shot to unseen robot embodiments and egocentric human motion, while preserving the language models open vocabulary knowledge. Beyond segmentation, KiRoSe tracks task progress on an unseen robot and detects when an action is being reversed.(website:this https URL)
  </details>

- **[FLORA: An Open Model Suite for Scaling Robotics Data Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31779)**  `arXiv:2610.31779`  
  _Sicong Leng, Yunze Man, Zhiqi Li, Jing Wang, Yuhe Zhang, Ryan Punamiya, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  The life-cycle of robotics foundation models crucially depends on data labels. Outcome reward labels are needed for monitoring real-world deployments. Process reward labels are needed during RL post-training. Timestamped captions are needed for filtering and balancing pretraining data. We present Flora, an industrial-quality data engine used for training GR00T models, that simultaneously covers these capabilities. Flora achieves SOTA scores on the captioning benchmark WGO-Bench and reward benchmarks RoboReward and Robometer. On internal benchmarks it is competitive with GPT-6 Astra while beating other frontier models. The model is substantially cheaper than API-based alternatives, roughly 125 times cheaper than GPT-6 Astra. Florais battle tested at scale, labeling 200,000 hours of data per day across 512 GB300s. By open-sourcing our data engine, we enable researchers to scale custom robotics datasets and explore emerging applications such as physical auto-research and human-to-robot transfer.
  </details>

- **[See What I Need, Dream What I Want: A Navigation Foundation Model on a Self-Constructed Representation Space](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31762)**  `arXiv:2610.31762`  
  _Jiawei Chen, Yinxi Wang, Zihan Yang, Meijun Gao, Yaowei Liu, Shuming Zhang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  What visual representation should a navigation policy act on? Navigation policies built on vision foundation models largely leave representation design to the encoders' pretraining objectives. We present RepresentNav, a navigation foundation model built on a self-constructed representation space: a visual representation constructed by the policy's own objectives from vision foundation models. RepresentNav builds this space with Representation Residuals, a compact three-layer transformer that attends over features spanning the full depth of multiple complementary vision foundation models, so that the policy learns to see what it needs for navigation. In the same space it dreams what it wants: before every action a latent chain-of-thought predicts the representation of the scene a moment ahead. The dream is trained end-to-end with the policy as a joint-embedding predictive architecture (JEPA), with a next-representation prediction loss and a regularizer on its targets. RepresentNav sets a new state of the art on VLNVerse, VLN-CE R2R, EVT-Bench, and two point-goal benchmarks, and its gains carry over to a physical robot amid clutter and moving people. On VLNVerse fine-grained, RepresentNav achieves 83.80% success, outperforming the previous state of the art by 20.05 percentage points, and on EVT-Bench distracted tracking it reaches 85.5% with a single camera (+10.1). With the same encoders, the self-constructed space clearly outperforms concatenating their outputs and learns to weight their layers unevenly, and a dream in it cuts collisions by a quarter. A single dreamed latent decodes into the depth, semantics, and 3D occupancy of the scene a moment ahead, more accurately than pretrained encoders applied to the real future frame, although occupancy is never supervised, and the rollout tracks the robot's own motion over the next second: the dream emerges as a compact latent world model.
  </details>

- **[RGraph-Eval: Task-Grounded Scene Graphs for Evaluating Robot Video Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.31121)**  `arXiv:2610.31121`  
  _Aleksandr Lazarev, Sofia Zimina, Roman Kuznetsov, Tatiana Zemskova, Iaroslav Koshelev, Dmitry Yudin_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generated robot videos can look convincing while depicting the wrong interaction: a different object is moved, a grasp disappears, or a placement ends outside its destination. We introduce RGraph-Eval, a reference-based framework that makes these discrepancies explicit through task-grounded temporal scene graphs. Unlike approaches that construct separate evidence representations for different evaluation dimensions, we organize task entities, interactions, and motion within a unified, interpretable scene graph representation. A foundation-model pipeline identifies manipulation roles, grounds persistent object masks, infers and verifies state transitions and action intervals, and reconstructs manipulated-object motion in three dimensions. Applying this representation to a generated video and a reference demonstration supports an interpretable RGraph-Eval score with visible-object, relation-change, and 3D path-shape components. We also introduce ShareRobot-SG, a collection of 1,000 manipulation scenes from ten ShareRobot sources annotated with the multimodal representation. Our evaluation separates graph-generation quality from agreement with human video judgments and includes comparisons with adapted SVG2 and SG-Ego, as well as a benchmark of three video generators. The framework connects robot-video assessment to the observable entities and interactions underlying a task while making the graph annotations reusable beyond evaluation. Code will be publicly available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[LocoManiLab: A Unified Testbed for Data, Policies, Controllers, and Deployment in Humanoid Loco-Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30876)**  `arXiv:2610.30876`  
  _Dewei Wang, Jinrui Han, Jiyuan Shi, Chenyun Zhang, Xianbo Lu, Jiabao Wu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid robots must coordinate locomotion and manipulation in everyday environments, yet progress is difficult to assess across differing policies, controllers, and task settings. LocoManiLab provides an integrated testbed spanning data collection and generation, policy training, evaluation, and physical deployment. Its task suite covers 234 atomic and compositional tasks across five household scene categories, supported by 51.5 hours of simulated teleoperation. The evaluation covers twelve policies with a shared controller across task and scene splits and perturbation levels, two policies across five controllers, and studies of demonstration scale and data-source mixtures. Success rates fall on unseen tasks and scenes and generally decline as visual perturbations increase, with differences in generalization and robustness across policies. For data generation, a humanoid motion generation model is fine-tuned for loco-manipulation to study the alignment of its outputs with task execution requirements and its integration into the data generation pipeline. Three simulated loco-manipulation tasks closely match real-world setups, enabling evaluation of three policies in both domains. Similar policy rankings support the reliability of simulation-based evaluation.
  </details>

- **[Robot Motion Transition with a Human Motion Prior](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.30817)**  `arXiv:2610.30817`  
  _Taehyun Byun, Taemoon Jeong, Yoonbyung Chai, Woojin Wie, Insu Park, Kyungjae Lee, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoid motion-tracking policies can reproduce diverse behaviors, yet connecting independently acquired motions into a continuous sequence remains challenging. Interpolation connects boundary poses without considering whether their relative position, heading, and timing provide suitable conditions for a transition. A generative motion prior can produce richer motion, but poorly arranged boundaries may cause it to generate excessive locomotion, turning, or another unintended action. We present Spatio-Temporal Alignment with a Motion Prior (STAMP), a framework for connecting independently acquired humanoid motions through a Robot-to-Human-to-Robot process. STAMP uses human motion space to draw on rich motion priors and reuse transition knowledge across robot embodiments. Its key component is a learned alignment model that determines the boundary conditions for motion inbetweening. Given the two motions retargeted to a human rig, the alignment model ranks candidate positions, headings, and transition durations for the following motion. A human motion prior generates the transition under the selected alignment. The completed sequence is retargeted to the robot and used as a reference to train a motion-tracking policy. We evaluate the tracking success of the generated transitions on Unitree~G1 and~ROBOTIS K1. Physical robot demonstrations further show that STAMP connects independently acquired motions into long-horizon sequences. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Training in Deployment: Human-in-the-loop Online Post-Training for Robot Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29264)**  `arXiv:2610.29264`  
  _Chun Gu, Xiaofei Wei, Yiming Chen, Bozhou Zhang, Juntong Zhang, Xiatian Zhu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Large pretrained robot foundation models provide strong priors for manipulation, yet adapting them to target tasks with offline teleoperated demonstrations often fails to cover the contact-rich, recovery, and precision-critical states encountered during deployment. Existing methods mitigate this issue by augmenting offline demonstrations with human-in-the-loop data or online reinforcement learning. Despite this, offline post-training adapts slowly because it cannot immediately absorb deployment experience, while online RL typically relies on value functions or auxiliary actor-critic models, increasing both training complexity and instability. We present TIDE (Training In DEployment), a human-in-the-loop online post-training system. TIDE introduces a provenance-conditioned supervised learning framework that updates the policy online from human interventions and policy-generated rollouts collected during deployment, without a learned critic, reward model, or action scorer. Across four real-world bimanual manipulation tasks, TIDE improves milestone-normalized task progress and binary full-task completion over the warm-start policy and matched additional offline demonstrations, and performs comparably to the fixed-policy collection baseline while making updated checkpoints available during deployment. Each task-specific online post-training session, from the start of deployment rollouts until the final checkpoint is available, completes in less than one hour while learning and checkpoint handling run asynchronously.
  </details>

- **[DepState-JEPA: Learning Depth-Grounded State Representation for Visual Perception and Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29246)**  `arXiv:2610.29246`  
  _Xiujian Liang, Liuyuan Na, Zehao Du, Jiacheng Xu, Weixi Song, Mingyang Sun, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Predictive representations for robot manipulation should connect scene geometry with the state changes induced by actions. Our intuition is that understanding what an action will change starts with understanding where things are: complementary RGB and depth observations ground a common scene representation from which to infer those changes. Guided by this intuition, we introduce DepState-JEPA, a dual-stream RGB--Depth joint-embedding predictive architecture that connects spatial understanding with action-conditioned prediction through a shared latent state, DepState. Built on V-JEPA~2.1, the framework uses depth both as a complementary observation stream and to ground both modalities in metric 3D space, aggregating shared information into DepState while retaining modality-private features for decoding. Ray-conditioned readouts predict local features without target depth; after an action-conditioned transition, the same decoders predict future RGB and depth features using the predicted state and fixed initial private features. Robot-state readouts provide recursive proprioceptive inputs; object-trajectory supervision and calibrated forward kinematics shape state learning through motion targets and joint--TCP consistency. Comparisons show stronger RGB-encoder transfer after RGB-D adaptation, feature completion, conditioned visual forecasting, and manipulation success. Within-model ablations identify contributions from depth content, metric coordinates, and future-state matching, with additional control gains from FK regularization. The project page is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[SMR: Learning streaming motion retargeting for humanoids from human semantic](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.29169)**  `arXiv:2610.29169`  
  _Chun Gu, Juntong Zhang, Jingyu Li, Li Zhang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Retargeting human motion to humanoids requires preserving motion semantics while accounting for the robot's physical constraints. Kinematic pipelines prescribe a robot trajectory through geometric matching, without establishing whether it can be executed under the robot's dynamics. Prior physics-supervised neural methods train bidirectional full-sequence models on physics-refined kinematic trajectories, remaining tied to prescribed kinematic solutions and requiring noncausal context. We propose SMR, a streaming neural motion retargeter learned from semantic-conditioned physics experts. We convert human motion into robot-aware task-space targets for body landmark positions, orientations, and velocities. Physics experts track these targets directly in simulation under contact, dynamics, and actuator constraints. We train a single strictly causal Transformer on their executed state trajectories that complete the motion and pass semantic and physical validity checks. At each incoming frame, the student uses only current and past human observations to generate a robot motion reference for a downstream whole-body controller. Experiments demonstrate that semantic-conditioned physics experts generate motion with higher semantic fidelity and physical validity than direct optimization-based retargeting, while the resulting streaming retargeter achieves improved closed-loop performance over kinematic and physics-refined baselines under a common whole-body controller.
  </details>

- **[Localization and Neural Splatting on the Edge: An FPGA Scene Memory for Robots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.25068)**  `arXiv:2610.25068`  
  _Haohua Que, Mingkai Liu, Tianle Zhu, Handong Yao_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Resource-constrained robots and autonomous vehicles need scene memories that localize a camera and render nearby views without a desktop GPU. Scene-coordinate regression (SCR) and 3D Gaussian splatting (3DGS) fit because they avoid an SfM stage, but ACEsplat-style pipelines are GPU-centered, and moving them to an edge FPGA is hard because SCR is pose-sensitive and 3DGS rendering is irregular and capacity-sensitive. We present LENS, an edge scene-memory acceleration system on the AMD KR260 that brings an ACEsplat-style workload to a low-power FPGA/ARM platform. Rather than proposing a new scene model, LENS co-designs the two dominant kernels with the board data path: an INT16 SCR accelerator that preserves pose accuracy, and a bit-exact fixed-point ordered 3DGS renderer that rewrites alpha transmittance as a log-sum-exp recurrence and removes provably useless streamed Gaussians. All LENS latency, power, and quality results are board-measured. The SCR engine preserves DSAC* localization accuracy at 25.42 ms, the on-board renderer reaches 34.11 dB on Cambridge against ACEsplat's 33.20, novel-view synthesis on RealEstate10K reaches 27.84 dB against 26.11, and all ten Wayspots scenes reach the ACEsplat rendering target. LENS wins performance per watt over a Jetson Orin NX on every deployment task, by 1.9x to 5.7x at about a quarter of the power, and is faster in absolute latency on localization and Wayspots rendering. On-device training reaches ACEsplat-level quality but not the GPU's time or energy, marking the remaining boundary for a future training accelerator.
  </details>

- **[ContactBench 1.0: A Benchmark for Everyday Contact-Rich Robotic Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.24512)**  `arXiv:2610.24512`  
  _Jingyi Xiang, Yixuan Wang, Jeannette Bohg, Wenzhen Yuan_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Contact-rich manipulation underlies many everyday human activities, yet evaluating robots on such tasks remains difficult: the tasks involve diverse contact behaviors and are hard to reproduce across laboratories. Drawing on observations of everyday human manipulation, we identify eight essential skills that provide a practical framework for studying contact-rich manipulation. We introduce ContactBench 1.0, an extensible benchmark that instantiates these skills through 36 reference tasks and more than 40 object assets. The assets capture task-relevant geometry and contact behavior, can be fabricated reproducibly on consumer-grade 3D printers, and are each paired with a matching simulation model. Controlled task variations in both the real world and simulation enable evaluation of how well robot systems generalize. We evaluate recent representative vision-only and visual-tactile policy learning methods on six benchmark tasks. The results reveal that while vision-only policies struggle to perceive or react to contact events, visual-tactile policies do not meaningfully improve their performance, highlighting that contact-rich manipulation remains a significant challenge for current policy learning methods. By providing common task definitions alongside physical and simulated assets, ContactBench 1.0 offers a shared testbed for developing, evaluating, and comparing contact-rich manipulation systems. The benchmark will be open-sourced atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[No Eyes, No Problem: What Does Touch Contribute to Blind Baoding Ball Manipulation?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23438)**  `arXiv:2610.23438`  
  _Ayush Deshmukh, Nalin Agarwal, Elle Miller, Sethu Vijayakumar_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humans manipulate objects within their hands through touch alone with remarkable ease, but for robots, achieving sustained real-world manipulation without vision remains an open challenge. Although reinforcement learning (RL) can generate sophisticated blind manipulation policies in simulation, transferring them to a real dexterous hand remains hindered by continuously changing contacts, motion-induced tactile information drift, and the gap between simulated and physical dynamics. We study this problem through Baoding ball rotation on the Shadow Dexterous Hand Lite, using only proprioception and binary tactile feedback as observations. We achieve successful zero-shot transfer through a combination of a sensor-aligned, coupling-aware embodiment in simulation, tactile calibration, and two novel episodic domain randomisations: slew-rate action limits and STuck-AT Taxels. On the physical hand, the policy achieves sustained blind Baoding rotation, completing $\textbf{225 consecutive $\mathbf{180^\circ}$ rotations (112.5 full rotations) at $\sim0.36 \;\text{rot/s}$}$ in a single continuous trial without any vision or object-state information. Furthermore, we evaluate the contribution of tactile sensing relative to proprioception alone and identify the hand regions where tactile information is most important. This is the strongest reported hardware result for Baoding ball manipulation in both rotation count and speed, irrespective of the sensing modality used. By effectively bridging the sim-to-real gap, our framework sets a new benchmark for sustained tactile-driven dexterous manipulation on physical hardware.
  </details>

- **[Robotic Spatiotemporal Editing: A Unified Interface for Controllable Action Generation via Masked Action Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.23002)**  `arXiv:2610.23002`  
  _Yanbang Li, Bu He, Yi Tian, Yu-Chiao Huang, Chenyu Zhang, Ruoqu Chen, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Generative policies can synthesize robot behaviors, yet controlling and editing them through diverse spatiotemporal references remains challenging. In the real world, human intent requires robot behaviors that satisfy constraints on where, when, and how actions are executed. Our key insight is that spatiotemporal control references, such as keypoints, flows, orientations, and keyposes, can be viewed as partial specifications of the same temporal Cartesian action space. We introduce the MAP (Masked Action Space) interface to unify these action references and formulate controllable manipulation as an action inpainting problem. Our Inpainting Policy (IP) reconstructs missing action dimensions of the end-effector, combining progress-guided alignment with multi-mask training supporting unseen action-constraint combinations. We further adopt a training-free RePaint variant that iteratively inpaints the action during denoising to reduce control error. On ManiSkill and LIBERO-10, our policy achieves average task success rates of 92.67% and 64.67%,this http URL-world experiments demonstrate spatiotemporal control with average positional and rotational errors of 44.3 mm and 7°, respectively, showing that our policy can use diverse references to guide executable robot behaviors.
  </details>

- **[Design and Analysis of a Nonlinear Parallel Mechanism Coupling for Dynamic Humanoid Legs](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.16604)**  `arXiv:2610.16604`  
  _Rohit Kumar, Heiner Peters, Felix Wiebe, Franek Stark, Janne Janzen, Dennis Mronga, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Humanoids increasingly demand lightweight leg mechanisms for agile, dynamic, and energy-efficient locomotion. Reducing distal inertia through proximal actuation has thus become a common design goal. Yet, compact transmissions that preserve torque capability and efficient force transmission remain hard to achieve. This paper presents a novel series-parallel leg design combining a knee parallel mechanism, an ankle parallel mechanism, and a nonlinear knee-ankle coupling where all actuators placed closer to the hip. As the knee flexes, the coupling passively assists ankle dorsiflexion for foot clearance and, as it extends it assists plantarflexion for push-off, redistributing ankle load through the knee transmission. We derive the coupling's nonlinear transmission models and validate them in simulation and on hardware. The design shows that the shared transmission lets both joints draw on each other's actuator capacity by easing the knee's peak torque demand under load and the ankle's peak speed demand during fast motion. With the leg's proximal actuator placement, the leg achieves an effective reflected mass of 0.37 kg at the foot for a 6 kg leg, demonstrating very low distal leg inertia for dynamic humanoid robots.
  </details>

- **[ArtifactArena: Evaluating Models by What They Build in the Physical World](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06511)**  `arXiv:2610.06511`  
  _Kushagra Tiwary, David Mayo, Nikhil Behari, Xiangzhou Sun, Abdulrahman Alabdulkareem, Isaac Galatzer-Levy, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  To evaluate the frontier, we must measure models not by what they say, but by what they can engineer and build in grounded physical environments. We introduce ArtifactArena, an open-ended platform where models face a physically grounded hardware-software co-design challenge: engineering fully functional robots to compete in a simulated arena. We evaluate a frontier model's zero-shot, verifier guided refinement, and open-ended physical design capabilities through three harnesses that refine their bot artifacts based on text descriptions, physics simulator feedback, and gameplay data. We benchmark these capabilities with an Elo ranking of frontier models derived from head-to-head tournaments between their artifacts. By releasing this framework and tournament infrastructure for ongoing community submissions, we establish a living, non-saturating testbed to continuously measure the expanding limits of open-ended intelligence in the physical world. Please visitthis https URLfor more information.
  </details>

- **[DASH: A da Vinci Adapter for Serial-link and Humanoid Robots as an Accessible Platform for Surgical Robotics Research](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05792)**  `arXiv:2610.05792`  
  _Sara Wickenhiser, Junrong Zhou, Zekai Liang, Lizzie Peiros, Liu Shanglei, Michael C. Yip_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robotic minimally invasive surgery offers well-documented clinical benefits, but the cost and infrastructure requirements of purpose-built platforms limit access in rural and lower-resourced facilities. Recent work has teleoperated general-purpose robots for laparoscopic tasks and in vivo procedures, but relied on handheld instruments coupled through passive linkages rather than native robotic actuation. Instead, we adapt da Vinci Classic and Xi instruments onto general-purpose robots that can integrate in clinical workflows. We present DASH, a da Vinci Adapter for Serial-link and Humanoid platforms, consisting of two types of adapters that require no modification to the instruments themselves. Each adapter is compatible with many robotic platform load capacities, engages the instrument's native latch, and wirelessly identifies inserted tools to load instrument-specific kinematics and coupling matrices. Teleoperated experiments demonstrate the efficacy of DASH and its benefits for enabling research access to surgical robotic platforms.
  </details>

- **[RMMBench: A Comprehensive Benchmark for Robotic Mobile Manipulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05414)**  `arXiv:2610.05414`  
  _Huapeng Li, Fuxiang Feng, Jinqiu Fan, Shuo Yang, Fengjiao Chen, Xuezhi Cao, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Although the advancement of vision-language models (VLMs) has endowed robots with enhanced environmental understanding and task reasoning, a comprehensive evaluation methodology is important to advance the integration of VLMs in robotic navigation and manipulation. However, current benchmarks lack a comprehensive method to evaluate diverse robotic tasks, and evaluation metrics remain relatively constrained, making it difficult to assess the embodied capabilities of VLMs in a thorough and fine-grained manner. To address this issue, we propose RMMBench, an evaluation benchmark that requires robots to understand language instructions and perform long-horizon tasks in continuous spaces. RMMBench seamlessly integrates high- and low-level embodied tasks into a unified framework, constructing a "navigation-manipulation" task suite comprising 70 canonical task scenarios that range from localized manipulation to long-horizon composite navigation. The results reveal that leading VLMs still face major challenges in spatial localization when performing mobile manipulation tasks, and also highlight the necessity of enhancing the spatial perception capability of robots during long-horizon interactions. RMMBench can be accessed atthis https URL
  </details>

- **[Are Frontier VLM Agents Ready to Be Robot Generalists? An Empirical Study with the Embodied Agent Arena](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.00854)**  `arXiv:2610.00854`  
  _Haojian Huang, Pukun Zhao, Zexi Li, Yehang Zhang, Yangkai Wei, Wenqian Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A robot generalist must interpret a scene, act on it, and satisfy every condition of a task. How far do frontier vision-language models (VLMs) meet these demands? We introduce Embodied Agent Arena, evaluating ten VLM agents across Geometry, Spatial Reasoning, Affordance, Task Planning, and Manipulation. Its 1,000 cases combine 32 established sources with GeoProbe, our 168-case benchmark for camera, motion, depth, and scale estimation. A minimal harness leaves task decisions to the model through source-native interfaces. Closed-weight models lead all five domains, whereas open-weight VLMs show useful spatial reasoning but substantially lower planning and manipulation success. Beneath these averages, similar geometric errors conceal different scale and shape distortions, and valid region estimates need not identify usable action points. During execution, agents can achieve early subgoals yet lose object control, bind an action to the wrong receiving region, or stop before the required physical state holds. Additional computation does not uniformly resolve these failures: code repair can restore execution without completing the task, while self-review can reverse correct judgments. Failure costs also differ: the strongest planners concentrate failed-token expenditure in prolonged body-control tasks, whereas other models fail broadly across procedures. Matched traces show how successful recovery uses feedback to restore a missing prerequisite and advance the remaining task.
  </details>

- **[Do Robotic World Models Really Follow Actions? Diagnosing and Aligning Action-Conditioned Generation for Policy Learning](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.24885)**  `arXiv:2608.24885`  
  _Sixiang Chen, Jiaming Liu, Jixian Wu, Yichen Guo, Tinghao Wang, QR Li, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Action-conditioned world models increasingly serve as learned simulators for robotic policy improvement, yet their reliability rests on an unverified assumption: generated futures faithfully reflect the consequences of arbitrary valid actions. Systematic evaluation against action-specific ground truth remains limited beyond expert demonstrations. To address this gap, we introduce WorldEcho, which evaluates action following over a broader action distribution through visual integrity and SE(3) end-effector trajectory alignment. Our diagnosis shows that expert-trained models perform better on demonstrated actions but struggle with off-expert trajectories, producing motion inconsistent with the commanded actions or visually invalid rollouts. To mitigate these failures, we propose WorldSync, which broadens action coverage, grounds intermediate video representations in action-induced robot dynamics through an Action-Forcing Expert, and aligns predicted and ground-truth changes under action interventions. Experiments show that WorldSync reduces integrity-gated error on RoboTwin and, when used for iterative policy improvement, yields higher success rates in simulation and on real robots.
  </details>

- **[PartialBiGrasp: Inferring Hidden Local Geometry for Bimanual Grasping from Partial Views](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.19188)**  `arXiv:2608.19188`  
  _Ayush Kaura, Vignesh Vembar, Md Faizal Karim, Keshab Patra, K Madhava Krishna_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Dual-arm robotic grasping is essential for manipulating large, heavy, and geometrically complex objects that cannot be reliably handled using a single manipulator. These large objects often contain only sparse graspable regions determined by local geometric properties such as thickness, edge structure, and gripper clearance. Prior bimanual grasping methods assume access to a full point cloud of the object which inherently contains this geometric information, but may not be accessible in real scenarios. This work proposes PartialBiGrasp, a dual-arm grasp generation framework that operates directly on partial point cloud observations. Our model learns geometric features implicitly through convolutional occupancy networks, enabling local reasoning about graspability, collision-free contact regions, and object thickness. We leverage this understanding to generate force-closure compliant grasp pairs, which are further refined using a sampling-based optimization to correct for ambiguity caused by incomplete geometry. We evaluate our approach using analytical force-closure metrics, large-scale simulation experiments, and real-world robot evaluations on noisy partial point clouds of novel objects, demonstrating robust and physically stable dual-arm grasp generation.
  </details>

- **[OccamView: Object-Conditioned View Selection for Frame-Budgeted Active 3D Gaussian Reconstruction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16499)**  `arXiv:2608.16499`  
  _Hongbo Gao, Wei Zhang, Zeyu Ni, Dihao Zhu, Ruifeng Li, Yunke Wang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Active 3D Gaussian reconstruction fundamentally relies on selecting informative next-best views under limited sensing budgets. Existing active 3DGS methods primarily plan viewpoints according to geometric information gain, treating object-induced hidden regions in the same manner as general unexplored space. Under tight frame budgets, such geometry-driven strategies may prioritize global scene coverage while leaving partially observed objects incompletely reconstructed. To address this limitation, we propose OccamView, an object-conditioned view-selection framework for frame-budgeted active 3D Gaussian reconstruction. Rather than predicting unseen object geometry or performing shape completion, OccamView maintains an online object memory from open-vocabulary detections grounded in measured RGB-D observations and represents unresolved local occupancy around detected objects as conservative hidden-region proxies. Candidate viewpoints are then evaluated using an occlusion-aware proxy-coverage score. Furthermore, we introduce a Geo-Floor mechanism that restricts object-conditioned re-ranking to geometrically competitive candidates, allowing object-conditioned cues to guide complementary observations while preserving the geometry-driven exploration behavior of the underlying planner. Experiments on Replica and Matterport3D under a unified frame-budgeted protocol show that OccamView consistently reduces Completion and improves Completion Ratio across five frame budgets, with particularly pronounced gains under limited frame budgets. These results demonstrate that lightweight object-conditioned cues effectively complement geometry-driven active view planning.
  </details>

- **[Unified Condition-Action Modeling for Accurate One-Step Action Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.16153)**  `arXiv:2608.16153`  
  _Xinyu Zhou, Zikun Cai, Kuangji Zuo, Gen Li, Boyu Ma, Yanshuo Lu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot manipulation requires policies that are both accurate and efficient, as robot control must respond to changing observations under tight latency constraints. Recent diffusion and flow policies are promising, but they often treat conditions as auxiliary signals rather than jointly evolving them with action trajectories. We find that this limitation can be effectively mitigated by a simple yet effective unified condition-action modeling design that represents conditions and actions in a shared token space, allowing a compact model to achieve high performance while improving both inference speed and accuracy. Therefore, we propose UCA-Flow, a unified condition-action modeling framework for accurate one-step action generation. Our method unifies observation conditions, timestep conditions, interval conditions, and action tokens into a single sequence, and processes them with a Unified Condition-Action Transformer for joint condition-action representation learning. As a result, condition representations are dynamically reconstructed according to the current generation stage, highlighting information most relevant for action refinement. Furthermore, we introduce an improved dual-pass supervision scheme over $u$ and $v$ for stronger optimization of unified condition-action modeling. UCA-Flow achieves an average success rate of 86.4%, improving over the strongest baseline by 7.5 percentage points, with a task-averaged inference latency of $3.0$ ms. It achieves $43.9\times$ and $32.2\times$ speedups over DP3 and Simple DP3, and remains $4.2\times$ and $2.2\times$ faster than one-step FlowPolicy and MP1, respectively. Project page:this https URL
  </details>

- **[RAPAC-DP: Response-Aligned Pending-Action Compensation for Diffusion Policies under Delayed Execution](https://arxiv.org/abs/2608.15924)**  `arXiv:2608.15924`  
  _Tao Wang, Wei Wang, Jianhui Wang, ZhengYang Ye, Qi Wang, Weidi Huang, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Cloud-side inference expands the computational resources available to imitation-learning policies, but communication and inference latencies create a mismatch between prediction and execution. We propose RAPAC-DP, a response-aligned pending-action compensation framework for diffusion- and flow-based action generators. By conditioning on actions committed during inference, RAPAC-DP generates a full-horizon action chunk aligned with the scheduled handover time. A lightweight pending-action encoder and LoRA adapters learn this compensation while keeping the pretrained base policy frozen. We construct delay-conditioned training samples from delay-free demonstrations, requiring neither an explicit dynamics model nor additional data collected under delayed execution. In simulation benchmarks at the largest evaluated delays, RAPAC-DP retains 81.4\% of its overall delay-free performance on Kinetix and achieves a mean success rate of 63.3\% across three RoboMimic tasks. Across 450 physical trials on a Franka Panda manipulator, RAPAC-DP increases the high-delay mean success rate from 28.9\% for RTC to 60.0\% across static and conveyor-based pick-and-place tasks.
  </details>

- **[Motion Generation With Environmental Constraints](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.25053)**  `arXiv:2607.25053`  
  _Előd Páll, Oliver Brock_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robot motion planning faces challenges in high-dimensional spaces and uncertain environments, often constrained by the need for collision-free motions. We advocate an alternative approach, Environmental Constraint Exploitation (ECE), where deliberate contact with the environment simplifies planning by reducing dimensionality and computational complexity. By integrating ECE into motion planning algorithms, we bias exploration to task-relevant regions and leverage contact for uncertainty reduction to improve robustness during execution. We evaluate ECE benefits with RRT-based planners and demonstrate their practical benefits in a real-world application. This work consolidates and extends prior research, showcasing how ECE simplifies motion planning while enhancing adaptability and performance in complex environments.
  </details>

- **[Semantic Anchoring for Robotic Action Representations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13597)**  `arXiv:2607.13597`  
  _Yuan Xu, Youheng Shi, Chengyang Li, Wentao Zhu, Yizhou Wang_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models inherit rich semantic representations from pretrained Vision-Language Models, yet fine-tuning on limited robot demonstrations degrades this structure and undermines generalization. A fundamental question therefore arises: what constitutes a good action representation? Inspired by the mirror neuron theory's insight that observation and execution share an intention-level encoding, we examine whether a robot's action representations preserve the semantic structure captured by pretrained encoders. Systematic probing confirms that this structure erodes during finetuning, and that its quality synchronizes with both task success and out-of-distribution generalization. We further introduce a plug-and-play method that anchors action representations to a semantic manifold while decomposing representations into a shared semantic channel and a private channel, all discarded at inference, leaving the deployed model unchanged. Validated on different VLA backbones across simulation and real-world benchmarks, our method yields up to +18.7% on real-world in-distribution tasks and +21.5% on out-of-distribution generalization.
  </details>

- **[WAM-TTT: Steering World-Action Models by Watching Human Play at Test Time](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06988)**  `arXiv:2607.06988`  
  _Yusen Feng, Bingchen Han, Jiangran Lyu, Kai Liu, Yixin Zheng, Yuxuan Wan, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Steering robot foundation models (RFMs) toward new task variants or user-preferred behaviors remains challenging, often requiring additional robot demonstrations, task-specific fine-tuning, or long-context conditioning. We present WAM-TTT, a test-time training framework for steering world action models from raw human videos. Rather than treating human videos as trajectories to imitate, WAM-TTT absorbs them into a lightweight adaptive memory inside a frozen WAM through self-supervised video prediction. To make this memory useful for control, we introduce a meta-training stage that aligns human demonstrations with robot behaviors using paired human-robot data and a key--value memory reconstruction objective. At test time, only unlabeled human videos are required to adapt the memory, while the pretrained WAM remains frozen. This enables efficient and reusable steering without robot actions, human-side annotations, or task-specific fine-tuning, while preserving the generalization ability of the foundation model. Extensive experiments show that WAM-TTT consistently outperforms in-context human-video conditioning baselines across diverse manipulation tasks and generalization settings.
  </details>

- **[Flow-ERD: Agent-type Aware Flow Matching With Entropy-Regularized Distillation for Diverse Traffic Simulation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.06957)**  `arXiv:2607.06957`  
  _Seulbin Hwang, Kiyoung Om, Dae Jung Kim, Jinhan Lee_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Realistic and diverse traffic simulation is essential to autonomous driving development. Yet prevailing benchmarks predominantly reward realism, and recent methods have optimized accordingly, leaving diversity underexplored. We introduce Flow-ERD, a multi-agent simulator that pursues realism and diversity jointly. Its backbone, Agent-Type Aware Flow Matching (AFM), couples flow matching's multi-modal expressiveness with type-specific kinematic execution. It preserves fine-grained diversity while keeping motions consistent with each agent type. A second stage, Entropy-Regularized Distillation (ERD), fine-tunes the closed-loop rollout distribution with an entropy-regularized reverse-KL objective. This mitigates closed-loop covariate shift while explicitly preventing collapse onto high-density modes. We evaluate Flow-ERD with Cross-Pair Diversity and its validity-gated variant alongside standard realism scores. Validation experiments further show that tuning entropy regularization recovers most of the pretrained backbone's diversity while retaining realism gains. Flow-ERD ranks first in test-split realism on the Waymo Open Sim Agents Challenge (WOSAC) and dominates the validation realism--diversity Pareto front among reproducible baselines.
  </details>

- **[GaP: A Graph-as-Policy Multi-Agent Self-Learning Harness For Variational Automation Tasks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2607.05369)**  `arXiv:2607.05369`  
  _Kaiyuan Chen, Shuangyu Xie, Letian Fu, Justin Yu, William Pacini, Sandeep Bajamahal, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  For robots to work reliably in commercial and industrial applications, recent advances in agentic coding systems have potential to combine interpretable robot programming with the open-world adaptability of model-free policies. We focus on "Variational Automation" (VA), a class of tasks that have larger variations in object geometry and pose than fixed automation. Model-free policies often struggle to close the reliability gap for VA tasks, which must be executed persistently and reliably in commercial and industrial applications. Motivated by prior work on Task and Motion Planning (TAMP) and the Robot Operating System (ROS), we introduce Graph-as-Policy (GaP), a multi-agent coding harness that generates directed computation graphs with perception, planning, and control nodes from a Modular Open Robot Skill Library (MORSL). GaP generates an internal simulation environment to rehearse task instances with different graphs in parallel to iteratively refine the graph structure and parameters to improve success rates and throughput. Evaluation with 8 new open VA task benchmarks, 4 in simulation and 4 in real-world, suggests that GaP can achieve success rates that significantly outperform baselines. Details, code, and data:this https URL
  </details>

- **[Inference-Time Robot Behavior Steering through Physically-Aware Reconfiguration of Task-Structure](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.26588)**  `arXiv:2606.26588`  
  _Yiyuan Pan, Hanjiang Hu, Shangtao Li, Xusheng Luo, Changliu Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  A central challenge in deploying robot policies is inference-time policy steering: redirecting a policy to satisfy natural-language preferences unseen in training, without retraining. Existing methods fail in two modes: end-to-end policies such as vision-language-action models (VLAs) require fine-tuning or expert-level guidance, while neuro-symbolic methods rely on predefined symbols whose edits can yield logically valid but physically infeasible plans. We propose ReStruct, a zero-shot method built on a neural automaton policy that decomposes a visuomotor policy into a high-level state-machine skeleton and a low-level residual controller. A vision-language model (VLM) grounds the preference in the skeleton's learned symbols, refining them if needed, and compiles it into a temporal-logic automaton, whose synchronous product with the skeleton reconfigures the task structure. With the controller frozen, the skeleton's action priors are updated for physically-aware control. In simulation and real-world experiments, ReStruct steers preferences from object-centric specifications to temporal-logic constraints, including novel combinations of demonstrated behaviors that require compositional generalization. Average preference-following success is 70.9% in simulation and 92.5% on a real robot, surpassing baselines like the prompt-steered vision-language-action model {\pi}0.5.
  </details>

- **[Language as a Sensor: Calibrated Spatial Belief Estimation in 3D Scenes from Natural Language](https://arxiv.org/abs/2606.08666)**  `arXiv:2606.08666`  
  _Aryan Naveen, Jason Xinyu Liu, Luca Carlone, Andreea Bobu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Robots deployed in human-centric environments routinely receive natural-language descriptions of spatial information ("I left my backpack on the table") that reference parts of the world beyond their perceptual field of view. Traditional metric-semantic mapping ignores this signal, while off-the-shelf multimodal models remain limited in 3D spatial reasoning and are not directly amenable to fusion with other sensor modalities. To convert language observations into a calibrated spatial distribution, we train a Language Sensor Model (LSM) that maps each utterance and its scene-graph context to a multimodal distribution, with mixture weights encoding referential ambiguity (e.g., which table) and component covariances encoding spatial uncertainty (e.g., where on the table the target lies). We then introduce VL-Map (Vision-Language Metric-Semantic Mapping), a probabilistic framework that treats these language predictions as stochastic observations and fuses them with onboard perception within a unified belief map. On the VLA-3D benchmark as well as on a real-world mobile robot, LSM is the only language predictor whose covariance estimates remain within the calibrated regime; fused into VL-Map, it leads to more accurate predictions of the target object location (about 70% more probability mass on the true target compared to the strongest foundation-model baseline). Project website:this https URL
  </details>

- **[3D Generation for Embodied AI and Robotic Simulation: A Survey](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.26509)**  `arXiv:2604.26509`  
  _Tianwei Ye, Yifan Mao, Minwen Liao, Jian Liu, Chunchao Guo, Dazhao Du, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Embodied AI and robotic systems increasingly depend on scalable, diverse, and physically grounded 3D content for simulation-based training and real-world deployment. While 3D generative modeling has advanced rapidly, embodied applications impose requirements far beyond visual realism: generated objects must carry kinematic structure and material properties, scenes must support interaction and task execution, and the resulting content must bridge the gap between simulation and reality. This survey reviews 3D generation for embodied AI and organizes the literature around three roles that 3D generation plays in embodied systems. In Data Generator, 3D generation produces simulation-ready objects and assets, including articulated, physically grounded, and deformable content for downstream interaction; in Simulation Environments, it constructs interactive and task-oriented worlds, spanning structure-aware, controllable, and agentic scene generation; and in Sim2Real Bridge, it supports digital twin reconstruction, data augmentation, and synthetic demonstrations for downstream robot learning and real-world transfer. We also show that the field is shifting from visual realism toward interaction readiness, and we identify the main bottlenecks, including limited physical annotations, the gap between geometric quality and physical validity, fragmented evaluation, and the persistent sim-to-real divide, that must be addressed for 3D generation to become a dependable foundation for embodied intelligence. Our project page is atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[Factor Graph-Based Continuum Robot State Estimation with Magnus Expansion Priors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2604.15619)**  `arXiv:2604.15619`  
  _Lorenzo Ticozzi, Sabyasachi Dash, Yangkun Liu, Patricio A. Vela, Panagiotis Tsiotras_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Reconstructing the shape of continuum manipulators from sparse, noisy sensor data is a challenging task, owing to the infinite-dimensional nature of such systems. Existing approaches broadly trade off between parametric methods that yield compact state representations but lack probabilistic structure, and Cosserat rod inference on factor graphs, which provides principled uncertainty quantification at the cost of a state dimension that grows with the spatial discretization. This letter combines the strengths of both these approaches by estimating the coefficients of a compact Geometric Variable Strain (GVS) parameterization within a factor graph framework. A novel kinematic factor, derived from the Magnus expansion of the strain field, encodes the closed-form rod geometry as a prior constraint linking the GVS strain coefficients to the backbone pose variables. The resulting formulation yields a compact state vector, is directly amenable to model-based control, and it retains the modularity, probabilistic treatment, and computational efficiency of factor graph inference. The proposed method is evaluated on both simulated and experimental data and compared against a Gaussian Process (GP) regression baseline, demonstrating increased accuracy in partial and sparse measurement scenarios while preserving computational efficiency.
  </details>

- **[Emergent Neural Automaton Policies: Learning Symbolic Structure from Visuomotor Trajectories](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.25903)**  `arXiv:2603.25903`  
  _Yiyuan Pan, Xusheng Luo, Hanjiang Hu, Peiqi Yu, Changliu Liu_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Scaling robot learning to long-horizon tasks remains a formidable challenge. While end-to-end policies often lack the structural priors needed for effective long-term reasoning, traditional neuro-symbolic methods rely heavily on hand-crafted symbolic priors. We introduce ENAP (Emergent Neural Automaton Policy), a neuro-symbolic imitation learning framework in which a bi-level policy adaptively emerges from visuomotor demonstrations. Specifically, we employ adaptive clustering and an extension of the L* algorithm to learn the task structure from demonstrations as a Mealy state machine, which serves as an interpretable high-level planner capturing latent task modes. This discrete structure then guides a low-level reactive residual network to learn precise continuous control via behavior cloning (BC). By explicitly modeling discrete transitions and continuous residuals, ENAP achieves sample efficiency and interpretability without task-specific labels. Experiments on complex manipulation and long-horizon tasks show that ENAP outperforms state-of-the-art end-to-end vision-language-action (VLA) policies by up to 27% in low-data regimes. ENAP can also distill task structure from the latent space of a pre-trained VLA, surpassing it on CALVIN benchmark with 39% fewer parameters (Fig. 1).
  </details>

- **[VORL-EXPLORE: A Hybrid Learning Planning Approach to Multi-Robot Exploration in Dynamic Environments](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.07973)**  `arXiv:2603.07973`  
  _Ning Liu, Sen Shen, Zheng Li, Sheng Liu, Dongkun Han, Shangke Lyu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Hierarchical multi-robot exploration decouples frontier allocation from local navigation, making systems brittle in dynamic environments. Without awareness of execution difficulty, allocators may send robots toward frontiers whose routes are congested or unsafe, causing bottlenecks, oscillatory replanning, and redundant coverage. We propose VORL-EXPLORE, a hybrid learning and planning framework that makes exploration execution-aware through execution fidelity, an estimate of local navigability and execution reliability. This fidelity score is used to penalize risky or crowded frontiers in a fidelity-coupled Voronoi allocation objective with inter-robot repulsion, and to arbitrate between global $A^*$ guidance and a reactive reinforcement learning policy during navigation. As a result, robots follow efficient global plans in reliable regions and rely more on safer local reactions in crowded or changing areas. The fidelity model is recalibrated using self-supervised pseudo-labels derived from recent progress and safety outcomes. Quantitative experiments on randomized dynamic grids and an ablation under severe traffic show higher success rates, shorter exploration lengths, and lower inter-robot coverage overlap. A Gazebo factory scenario is included as a deployment illustration. The source code is available atthis https URL.
  </details>

- **[CARE: Consistent Action-Response Execution for Human-Guided Robot Transport](https://arxiv.org/abs/2601.16686)**  `arXiv:2601.16686`  
  _Ning Liu, Sen Shen, Zheng Li, Matthew D'Souza, Jen Jen Chung, Lan Wu, et al._
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Human-guided collaborative transport becomes interaction-critical when the operator can continue along a route segment that is not executable by the robot--payload system. In this regime, operator motion is not an exogenous reference: it simultaneously communicates a persistent route tendency and adapts to the robot's realized progress. A common modular formulation predicts one operator future from observation history, scores multiple robot proposals against that shared forecast, and then modifies the selected command for local feasibility. This can compare candidates under responses they would not induce and can condition prediction on an action that is never executed. We formulate local control over executable-action-indexed joint human--robot transitions and instantiate it in Consistent Action--Response Execution (CARE). Each nominal proposal or bounded alternative is first mapped to an executable action; that same action conditions the route-aligned operator-response prediction, the matched robot--operator rollout, and a fixed interaction utility for candidate selection. Bounded reciprocal refinement handles transient route--feasibility mismatch, whereas persistent progress loss replaces the nominal proposal with a progress-oriented recovery proposal while retaining the same downstream executable-action-conditioned evaluation. Across training and held-out maps, CARE preserves near-saturated performance when the indicated route remains locally usable and achieves the highest observed success and service availability when the operator can continue but the robot must detour. It also yields lower jerk-proxy values and measured controller latency than dynamic-window and Timed Elastic Band baselines. Factorial ablations distinguish reciprocal refinement from progress recovery, delayed-response tests probe temporal mismatch, and an indoor deployment demonstrates physical executability.
  </details>

- **[Deep Learning Warm Starts for Trajectory Optimization on the International Space Station](https://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05588)**  `arXiv:2505.05588`  
  _Somrita Banerjee, Abhishek Cauligi, Marco Pavone_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Trajectory optimization is a cornerstone of modern robot autonomy, enabling systems to compute trajectories and controls in real-time while respecting safety and physical constraints. However, it has seen limited usage in spaceflight applications due to its heavy computational demands that exceed the capability of most flight computers. In this work, we provide results on the first in-space demonstration of using machine learning-based warm starts for accelerating trajectory optimization for the Astrobee free-flying robot onboard the International Space Station (ISS). We formulate a data-driven optimal control approach that trains a neural network to learn the structure of the trajectory generation problem being solved using sequential convex programming (SCP). Onboard, this trained neural network predicts solutions for the trajectory generation problem and relies on using the SCP solver to enforce safety constraints for the system. Our trained network reduces the number of solver iterations required for convergence in cases including rotational dynamics by 60% and in cases with obstacles drawn from the training distribution of the warm start model by 50%. This work represents a significant milestone in the use of learning-based control for spaceflight applications and a stepping stone for future advances in the use of machine learning for autonomous guidance, navigation, & control.
  </details>

- **[Haptic bilateral teleoperation system for free-hand dental procedures](https://arxiv.org/abs/2503.21288)**  `arXiv:2503.21288`  
  _Lorenzo Pagliara, Vincenzo Petrone, Enrico Ferrentino, Andrea Chiacchio, Giovanni Russo_
  <details><summary>Abstract</summary>
  Free-hand dental procedures are typically repetitive, time-consuming and require high precision and manual dexterity. Robots can play a key role in improving procedural accuracy and safety, enhancing patient comfort, and reducing operator workload. However, robotic solutions for free-hand procedures remain limited or completely lacking. To address this gap, we develop a haptic bilateral teleoperation system (HBTS) for free-hand dental procedures (FH-HBTS). The system includes a mechanical end-effector, compatible with standard clinical tools, and equipped with an endoscopic camera for improved visibility of the intervention site. By ensuring motion and force correspondence between the operator's and the robot's actions, monitored through visual feedback, we enhance the operator's sensory awareness and motor accuracy. Furthermore, to ensure procedural safety, we limit interaction forces by scaling the motion references provided to the admittance controller based solely on measured contact forces. This ensures effective force limitation in all contact states without requiring prior knowledge of the environment. The proposed FH-HBTS is validated through a technical evaluation and an in-vitro pre-clinical study conducted on a dental model under clinically representative, yet simplified, conditions. The results show that the system improves the naturalness, safety, and accuracy of teleoperation, highlighting its potential to enhance free-hand dental procedures.
  </details>
